    *   **Proprietary LLMs:** Using proprietary LLMs (GPT-40, GPT-4.1, 03-mini) limits the reproducibility and accessibility of the research. While the authors have no control over this, it makes independent verification difficult. It also is difficult to ascertain the exact nature of the "reasoning" capabilities built into models like 03-mini, other than what OpenAI publicly states.
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** While the Financial PhraseBank dataset is standard, a dataset of ~5k sentences may be relatively small for modern LLM evaluation, potentially impacting the generalizability of the findings. The paper's generalizability could benefit from validation on other, similar sentiment datasets in the finance domain.
    *   **Definition of Reasoning:** The paper equates "reasoning" with strategies like CoT, but LLMs do not truly *reason* in the human sense. They generate text based on learned patterns. This distinction needs to be kept in mind, and the findings may primarily reflect how different prompting styles affect language generation patterns. The paper does mention this, but the title and overall tone still tend to frame the topic in the context of actual LLM "reasoning," rather than just different prompting paradigms.
    *   **Inconsistencies in Model-Method combinations:** The 'No-CoT' prompting paradigm is applied only to GPT-40, GPT-4.1 and o3-mini models, leaving FinBERT to be evaluated in the zero-shot model only with no CoT applied. The addition of CoT prompting paradigm to FinBERT could help evaluate its performance within those constraints.
    *   **Lack of Financial Outcome Data:** The study focuses solely on aligning with human-labeled sentiment, *not* on actual financial outcomes (e.g., stock price prediction). While the annotators were instructed to rate sentences based on expected short-term stock price reactions, without testing the models on real price data, the practical implications are unclear.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The core novelty lies in demonstrating the potential drawbacks of explicitly encouraging LLM reasoning in a specific domain (financial sentiment analysis). While other studies have hinted at similar effects in different tasks, this paper provides a thorough, domain-specific exploration. The results are significant because they challenge the prevailing assumption that LLMs *always* benefit from increased reasoning capabilities. It also raises important questions about how to best match LLM prompting strategies to the nature of the task. It might not be novel to LLM researchers studying prompting strategies, but it is potentially impactful for financial firms seeking to deploy LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development and deployment of LLMs for financial applications by encouraging a more nuanced understanding of how to leverage reasoning strategies. It might also spur further research into the factors that determine when reasoning is beneficial versus detrimental.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7** because the paper presents a counterintuitive and valuable finding, supported by a rigorous methodology and insightful analysis. The paper's weaknesses, particularly the use of proprietary LLMs and the lack of financial outcome data, somewhat limit its broad impact and generalizability. However, the core message—that more reasoning isn't always better—is important and well-supported, with solid implications for financial applications. The impact would be greater if validated against multiple datasets or real-world financial trading data.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04575v1)
- **Authors**: Qingchuan Li, Jiatong Li, Zirui Liu, Mingyue Cheng, Yuting Zeng, Qi Liu, Tongxuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Neuro-symbolic approaches combining large language models (LLMs) with solvers excels in logical reasoning problems need long reasoning chains. In this paradigm, LLMs serve as translators, converting natural language reasoning problems into formal logic formulas. Then reliable symbolic solvers return correct solutions. Despite their success, we find that LLMs, as translators, struggle to handle lexical diversification, a common linguistic phenomenon, indicating that LLMs as logic translators are unreliable in real-world scenarios. Moreover, existing logical reasoning benchmarks lack lexical diversity, failing to challenge LLMs' ability to translate such text and thus obscuring this issue. In this work, we propose SCALe, a benchmark designed to address this significant gap through **logic-invariant lexical diversification**. By using LLMs to transform original benchmark datasets into lexically diversified but logically equivalent versions, we evaluate LLMs' ability to consistently map diverse expressions to uniform logical symbols on these new datasets. Experiments using SCALe further confirm that current LLMs exhibit deficiencies in this capability. Building directly on the deficiencies identified through our benchmark, we propose a new method, MenTaL, to address this limitation. This method guides LLMs to first construct a table unifying diverse expressions before performing translation. Applying MenTaL through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) significantly improves the performance of LLM translators on lexically diversified text. Our code is now available at https://github.com/wufeiwuwoshihua/LexicalDiver.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Are LLMs Reliable Translators of Logical Reasoning Across Lexically Diversified Contexts?":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical vulnerability in the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as translators within neuro-symbolic reasoning frameworks. It argues that LLMs, despite their general success, often fail to consistently map semantically equivalent but lexically diverse natural language expressions to uniform logical symbols. This inconsistency undermines the reliability of these systems, causing solver failures and hindering real-world applicability.

To highlight this deficiency, the authors introduce SCALe, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the "Semantic Consistency Mapping Ability with Logic-invariant Lexical Diversification."  SCALe leverages LLMs to create lexically diversified versions of existing reasoning datasets while preserving the logical structure. Through experiments, they demonstrate that current LLMs experience a significant performance drop on SCALe, confirming their struggles with lexical diversity.

The paper then proposes MenTaL, a "Mental representation Table-guided formal Logic translation framework," to mitigate this issue. MenTaL prompts LLMs to explicitly construct a mapping table unifying diverse expressions before performing translation. Experimental results demonstrate that MenTaL significantly improves the performance of LLMs on lexically diversified text through in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **SCALe Benchmark:** The introduction of SCALe is a significant contribution. Existing logical reasoning benchmarks often lack lexical diversity, masking LLMs' translation inconsistencies. SCALe provides a systematic way to assess this critical ability.  The method of using LLMs to generate diversifications is itself novel.
*   **MenTaL Framework:** The MenTaL framework, while drawing inspiration from cognitive science, is a practical and novel approach to improve LLMs' semantic consistency mapping.  The idea of guiding LLMs to explicitly construct a mapping table before translation is innovative.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing a Real-World Problem:** The paper tackles a practical problem that limits the adoption of LLM-based reasoning systems in real-world applications where lexical variation is prevalent.
*   **Improving Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning:** Enhancing the reliability of LLMs as translators directly strengthens neuro-symbolic reasoning approaches, potentially unlocking more complex and robust reasoning capabilities.
*   **Practical Solution:** The MenTaL framework offers a concrete and effective solution that can be readily integrated into existing LLM-based reasoning pipelines. The demonstration of its effectiveness via both in-context learning and fine-tuning broadens its appeal.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation of SCALe, including tests with human participants and error analyses. The demonstration of performance gains with MenTaL via both in-context learning and fine-tuning across multiple models and datasets further strengthens the significance.
*  **Broader Impacts:** The paper addresses a critical vulnerability in the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as translators within neuro-symbolic reasoning frameworks. It highlights the challenges in real-world applications where lexical variation is prevalent and provides a solution, but the paper also notes the potential negative impacts including the misuse to produce manipulative arguments with a facade of rigor and the resource demands for training.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Diversification Control:** The authors acknowledge a lack of precise control in the lexical diversification process, relying on LLMs for generation. This could introduce subtle semantic shifts despite the attempts to maintain logic equivalence. More rigorous control mechanisms would enhance the validity of the benchmark.
*   **Scope of Datasets:** While SCALe covers a range of logical reasoning tasks, the extent to which its findings generalize to more complex and nuanced domains remains an open question.  Expanding the benchmark to include datasets with richer linguistic phenomena would further strengthen its impact.
*   **Scalability of MenTaL*:** The MenTaL* method relies on human-defined rules, which is hard to implement and may be complex.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and addressing a significant weakness in LLM-based reasoning systems. The SCALe benchmark provides a rigorous means of evaluating semantic consistency mapping ability, while the MenTaL framework offers a practical and effective solution. While some limitations exist regarding the control of lexical diversification and generalizability, the paper has clear novelty and the potential to significantly impact the field of neuro-symbolic reasoning. The study is well-executed, and the results are compelling.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Selecting Demonstrations for Many-Shot In-Context Learning via Gradient Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04579v1)
- **Authors**: Jianfei Zhang, Bei Li, Jun Bai, Rumei Li, Yanmeng Wang, Chenghua Lin, Wenge Rong
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) empowers Large Language Models (LLMs) for rapid task adaptation without Fine-Tuning (FT), but its reliance on demonstration selection remains a critical challenge. While many-shot ICL shows promising performance through scaled demonstrations, the selection method for many-shot demonstrations remains limited to random selection in existing work. Since the conventional instance-level retrieval is not suitable for many-shot scenarios, we hypothesize that the data requirements for in-context learning and fine-tuning are analogous. To this end, we introduce a novel gradient matching approach that selects demonstrations by aligning fine-tuning gradients between the entire training set of the target task and the selected examples, so as to approach the learning effect on the entire training set within the selected examples. Through gradient matching on relatively small models, e.g., Qwen2.5-3B or Llama3-8B, our method consistently outperforms random selection on larger LLMs from 4-shot to 128-shot scenarios across 9 diverse datasets. For instance, it surpasses random selection by 4% on Qwen2.5-72B and Llama3-70B, and by around 2% on 5 closed-source LLMs. This work unlocks more reliable and effective many-shot ICL, paving the way for its broader application.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of demonstration selection for many-shot in-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs).  It proposes a novel gradient matching approach called Curriculum Latent Gradient (CLG). CLG selects demonstrations by aligning the fine-tuning gradients of the entire target task training set with those of the selected examples. This approach aims to mimic the learning effect on the full training set within the selected subset of demonstrations.  The paper demonstrates that CLG consistently outperforms random selection across various datasets and model sizes, including both open-source and closed-source LLMs, unlocking more reliable and effective many-shot ICL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the gradient matching approach for demonstration selection. The paper bridges the gap between data selection for fine-tuning and demonstration selection for ICL.  The idea of aligning learning dynamics (represented by gradients) to choose demonstrations is a valuable contribution.  While the notion of using latent task embeddings builds on previous work (Wang et al., 2023), the CLG framework and its focus on gradients are distinct.
* **Significance:** Many-shot ICL is a relatively recent and important area of research, and demonstration selection is a key bottleneck. This paper tackles a relevant and timely problem. The results demonstrate consistent and appreciable performance gains over random selection, a widely-adopted baseline. Moreover, the demonstrated transferability to closed-source models is a significant practical contribution.  The computational cost analysis is also important as it addresses a vital aspect for practical adoption. The paper unlocks potential for more efficient ICL deployment across various real-world applications.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods for many-shot ICL.
    * **Novel Approach:** The CLG method is well-motivated and grounded in learning principles.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper provides extensive experimental validation across diverse datasets and LLMs.  The comparative analysis against baselines is thorough, including analyses of diversity, efficiency, and transferability.  The detailed ablation studies (e.g., the gradient mismatching experiments) provide valuable insights.
    * **Practical Considerations:** The discussion of computational cost and open-source code availability add to the practical value of the research.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The CLG method is complex and requires a relatively intricate implementation. While the paper provides code, the implementation might be a barrier for some researchers/practitioners.
    * **Computational Overhead:** The method still requires a 'pre-selection' step that involves training the latent concept learning model. This is less efficient compared to simple retrieval-based selection methods. Though the paper argues it is justified by the performance gains, it does add to the overall complexity.
    * **Limited exploration of other factors**: While the paper addresses the important problem of selecting *which* demonstrations, it is less comprehensive on *how* to construct them or address issues such as demonstration order. The analysis of these factors is somewhat superficial.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to stimulate further research in demonstration selection for ICL, particularly focusing on methods that consider the learning dynamics within LLMs.  It provides a strong foundation for exploring alternative gradient-based or learning-based selection strategies. Also, the finding related to diverse downstream LMs is of interest.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to a critical problem in the rapidly evolving field of ICL. The empirical results are compelling, and the paper addresses practical considerations. While there are some limitations related to complexity and computational overhead, the significant performance gains and potential influence on future research justify a relatively high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LESS: Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-Supervised Learning for Speech Foundational Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04586v1)
- **Authors**: Wen Ding, Fan Qian
- **Abstract**: We introduce LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a versatile framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to correct pseudo labels generated from in-the-wild data. Within the LESS framework, pseudo-labeled text from Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) or Automatic Speech Translation (AST) of the unsupervised data is refined by an LLM, and augmented by a data filtering strategy to optimize LLM knowledge transfer efficiency. Experiments on both Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST tasks show that LESS achieves a notable absolute WER reduction of 3.77% on the Wenet Speech test set, as well as BLEU scores of 34.0 and 64.7 on Callhome and Fisher test sets respectively. These results validate the adaptability of LESS across different languages, tasks, and domains. Ablation studies conducted with various LLMs and prompt configurations provide novel insights into leveraging LLM-derived knowledge for speech processing applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LESS (Large Language Model Enhanced Semi-supervised Learning), a framework for improving speech foundational models (SFMs) using LLMs to refine pseudo-labels generated from in-the-wild data.  LESS leverages LLMs to correct transcripts produced by ASR or AST systems and incorporates a data filtering strategy to optimize the use of LLM-derived knowledge.  The authors present experiments on Mandarin ASR and Spanish-to-English AST tasks, demonstrating WER and BLEU score improvements. The work also includes ablation studies examining the impact of different LLMs and prompt configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to refine pseudo-labels for speech models is not entirely new; the paper cites several related works that explore similar concepts, specifically one that focuses on code-switching. However, the specific combination of techniques in LESS, particularly the LLM refinement with targeted prompts *and* the integration of a data filtering stage designed to enhance LLM knowledge transfer efficiency, constitutes a novel contribution. Moreover, this is a significant advancement on previous work that focuses on code-switching in that this work focuses on completely in-the-wild data, and demonstrates its application on multilingual, multitask SFMs. The approach itself isn't radically different conceptually, but the scale (SFMs) and the focus on wild data add to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its ability to leverage readily available, in-the-wild data for improving speech models. This is particularly valuable because high-quality labeled speech data is scarce compared to text data.  The demonstrated improvements in WER and BLEU scores, especially on out-of-domain datasets, indicate that LESS can enhance the robustness and generalization capabilities of SFMs.
    The use of open-source LLMs and SFMs makes the research reproducible and increases its potential impact on the community. Furthermore, the ablation studies provide valuable insights into how to effectively incorporate LLMs into speech processing pipelines. This can help guide future research in this area.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating significant improvements on both ASR and AST tasks and across multiple languages.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the impact of different LLMs and prompt configurations.
    *   The use of publicly available models and data promotes reproducibility.
    *   The work addresses a practical problem: the scarcity of labeled speech data.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The computational cost of using LLMs for pseudo-label refinement is not thoroughly addressed. While the paper mentions using NVIDIA NIM, a more detailed analysis of the resource requirements would be beneficial.
    *   The reliance on a specific data filtering threshold (hypo\_wer) could be a limitation. The sensitivity of the performance to this threshold could be explored further.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth comparison to other SSL techniques for speech. While some related work is cited, a more comprehensive analysis would strengthen the paper.
    *   The number of iterations of NST could be increased to demonstrate convergence more clearly, as the paper only explores two or three iterations. The paper could benefit from a more detailed exploration of this to improve results.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in speech processing, particularly in the areas of semi-supervised learning, transfer learning, and the integration of LLMs with SFMs. The framework provides a practical and effective way to improve speech model performance using abundant, unlabeled data.

**Overall:**

The LESS framework provides a practical and effective approach for enhancing speech foundational models. The research demonstrates a clever use of LLMs to refine pseudo-labels and significantly improve model accuracy and robustness. The framework is easy to implement. While some limitations exist in terms of computational cost analysis and threshold sensitivity, the overall contribution is valuable and addresses a real need in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

I assigned a score of 7.5 because while the idea of using LLMs to enhance pseudo-labels isn't entirely groundbreaking, the specific combination of techniques (prompting, data filtering), the scale of the experiments (SFMs, in-the-wild data), and the clear demonstration of improvements warrant a strong score. The ablation studies add significant value. The weaknesses, such as the limited analysis of computational cost and threshold sensitivity, prevent a higher score. The paper is well executed and clearly demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach, and the focus on SFMs is a key contribution that moves the field beyond prior work done on smaller models. However, the conceptual novelty is not *exceptionally* high.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safe: Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models via Retrospective Step-aware Formal Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04592v1)
- **Authors**: Chengwu Liu, Ye Yuan, Yichun Yin, Yan Xu, Xin Xu, Zaoyu Chen, Yasheng Wang, Lifeng Shang, Qun Liu, Ming Zhang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has become the de facto method to elicit reasoning capabilities from large language models (LLMs). However, to mitigate hallucinations in CoT that are notoriously difficult to detect, current methods such as process reward models (PRMs) or self-consistency operate as opaque boxes and do not provide checkable evidence for their judgments, possibly limiting their effectiveness. To address this issue, we draw inspiration from the idea that "the gold standard for supporting a mathematical claim is to provide a proof". We propose a retrospective, step-aware formal verification framework $Safe$. Rather than assigning arbitrary scores, we strive to articulate mathematical claims in formal mathematical language Lean 4 at each reasoning step and provide formal proofs to identify hallucinations. We evaluate our framework $Safe$ across multiple language models and various mathematical datasets, demonstrating a significant performance improvement while offering interpretable and verifiable evidence. We also propose $FormalStep$ as a benchmark for step correctness theorem proving with $30,809$ formal statements. To the best of our knowledge, our work represents the first endeavor to utilize formal mathematical language Lean 4 for verifying natural language content generated by LLMs, aligning with the reason why formal mathematical languages were created in the first place: to provide a robust foundation for hallucination-prone human-written proofs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Safe," a novel framework for enhancing the safety and reliability of mathematical reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). It achieves this by incorporating a retrospective, step-aware formal verification process. Instead of solely relying on opaque metrics like process reward models (PRMs), Safe leverages the formal mathematical language Lean 4 to articulate and formally prove the correctness of each reasoning step generated by the LLM.  The framework decomposes complex reasoning chains into simpler steps, auto-formalizes each step into a Lean 4 statement, and then utilizes automated theorem provers to verify the statement.  The results of these formal verifications are then combined with prospective scoring (PRMs) to improve overall performance and provide interpretable, verifiable evidence of correctness.  The paper also introduces FormalStep, a new benchmark dataset for step-correctness theorem proving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

*   **Formal Verification of Intermediate Steps:** Using formal mathematical languages (Lean 4) to verify the correctness of *individual steps* in LLM reasoning chains is a significant departure from existing methods that primarily focus on evaluating the final output or assigning scores to entire trajectories. This provides a finer-grained and more reliable mechanism for detecting hallucinations.

*   **Retrospective Approach:** Unlike PRMs which predict future correctness (prospective verification), Safe retrospectively validates steps, providing concrete evidence after the LLM has already produced the reasoning. This combination with prospective verification is also novel.

*   **Integration with PRMs:** Combining formal verification scores with existing process reward models provides a robust and high-performing approach, leveraging the strengths of both symbolic reasoning and data-driven learning.

*   **FormalStep Dataset:** The creation of a dataset specifically designed for step correctness theorem proving, FormalStep, is a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Reliability:** By incorporating formal verification, Safe can significantly improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM-generated mathematical reasoning, making them less prone to errors and hallucinations. This has important implications for applications where accuracy is paramount.

*   **Interpretability:** The use of formal proofs provides a clear and interpretable rationale for the correctness of each reasoning step, increasing confidence in the LLM's conclusions. This contrasts with the "black box" nature of many existing methods.

*   **Broader Applicability:** While the paper focuses on mathematical reasoning, the general principles of formal verification could be adapted to other domains where verifiable and trustworthy AI is required, such as code generation, legal reasoning, and scientific discovery.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of hallucinations in LLM reasoning and provides a compelling rationale for the use of formal verification.

*   **Well-Defined Framework:** Safe is a well-defined and conceptually clear framework with a modular design, incorporating auto-formalization, automated theorem proving, and score aggregation.

*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of Safe across multiple language models and mathematical datasets, consistently outperforming existing baselines. The ablation studies provide further insights into the importance of the different components of the framework.

*   **Dataset Contribution:** The introduction of FormalStep adds value to the community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Overhead:** Formal verification can be computationally expensive, requiring the use of LLMs for auto-formalization and potentially substantial search budgets for automated theorem proving. This could limit the scalability of Safe in some applications. The paper acknowledges this limitation, but further optimization may be needed.

*   **Dependence on Auto-Formalization and ATP Quality:** The performance of Safe is inherently dependent on the quality of the auto-formalization process and the capabilities of the automated theorem provers. Errors in either of these components can lead to incorrect verification results.

*   **Limited Scope of Lean 4:** While Lean 4 is powerful, it may not be suitable for all types of mathematical reasoning or all domains. Some reasoning steps may be difficult or impossible to express in Lean 4, limiting the applicability of Safe.

*   **GSM8K result.** The smaller result on GSM8k indicates a possible limitation on simple tasks, indicating that the auto-formalization pipeline may not be worthwhile.

**Potential Influence:**

Safe has the potential to significantly influence the field of trustworthy AI by providing a more reliable and interpretable approach to verifying the correctness of LLM reasoning. The combination of formal verification with existing techniques like PRMs offers a promising path towards building more robust and dependable AI systems. The FormalStep dataset is also likely to be a valuable resource for future research in this area. The influence and improvements on different datasets are quite impressive.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty and significance of its approach, I assign a score of **8**. The paper addresses a crucial challenge in LLM reasoning (hallucinations), presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated framework (Safe), makes a valuable dataset contribution (FormalStep), and demonstrates strong experimental results. While the computational overhead and dependence on auto-formalization and ATP quality are limitations, they are clearly acknowledged and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. The work lays important groundwork for future development of robust and reliable AI systems.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A MISMATCHED Benchmark for Scientific Natural Language Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04603v1)
- **Authors**: Firoz Shaik, Mobashir Sadat, Nikita Gautam, Doina Caragea, Cornelia Caragea
- **Abstract**: Scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI) is the task of predicting the semantic relation between a pair of sentences extracted from research articles. Existing datasets for this task are derived from various computer science (CS) domains, whereas non-CS domains are completely ignored. In this paper, we introduce a novel evaluation benchmark for scientific NLI, called MISMATCHED. The new MISMATCHED benchmark covers three non-CS domains-PSYCHOLOGY, ENGINEERING, and PUBLIC HEALTH, and contains 2,700 human annotated sentence pairs. We establish strong baselines on MISMATCHED using both Pre-trained Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Our best performing baseline shows a Macro F1 of only 78.17% illustrating the substantial headroom for future improvements. In addition to introducing the MISMATCHED benchmark, we show that incorporating sentence pairs having an implicit scientific NLI relation between them in model training improves their performance on scientific NLI. We make our dataset and code publicly available on GitHub.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MISMATCHED, a new evaluation benchmark for scientific Natural Language Inference (NLI).  Unlike existing scientific NLI datasets, which primarily focus on computer science domains, MISMATCHED covers three non-CS domains: Psychology, Engineering, and Public Health. The dataset consists of 2,700 human-annotated sentence pairs.  The authors establish strong baselines using both pre-trained Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) and show that there is substantial headroom for future improvements. Furthermore, they demonstrate that including implicit relations (adjacent sentences that have scientific NLI relation) helps enhance the performance of scientific NLI models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The introduction of a scientific NLI dataset that explicitly targets non-CS domains is a valuable contribution. It directly addresses a significant gap in the existing resources, which are heavily skewed towards computer science. Prior datasets like SciNLI and MSCINLI, while significant, were limited in their domain diversity. MISMATCHED introduces a challenging new testbed for evaluating the out-of-domain robustness of scientific NLI models. The exploration of *implicit* relations is also interesting, offering a new way to augment existing datasets by leveraging contextual information. The core idea, broadening the scope of scientific NLI datasets, is novel and timely.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in several aspects:
    *   **Dataset Creation:** It provides a well-constructed dataset using a rigorous annotation process and addresses class imbalances, preventing data leakage.
    *   **Baseline Performance:** The thorough baseline evaluation using various SLMs and LLMs highlights the difficulty of the task and sets a clear benchmark for future research. Showing the effectiveness of incorporating *implicit* relations demonstrates a clear path for improving performance on this task, and suggests this should be adopted.
    *   **Impact on Scientific Understanding:** It enables the development of more robust and generalizable scientific NLI models, ultimately contributing to improved scientific text understanding and related downstream applications like topic classification and citation intent classification.
    *   **Out-of-Domain Evaluation:** The paper provides a practical framework for out-of-domain evaluation in the specific context of Scientific NLI, which is useful to understand the robustness of approaches developed in the computer science domain.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dataset is well-motivated and addresses a clear gap in existing resources.
    *   The data collection and annotation process is clearly explained and rigorous.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive set of baseline results using a variety of established models.
    *   The experiments on implicit relations are insightful and provide a promising direction for future research.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While covering three non-CS domains, the paper acknowledges that many other scientific disciplines remain unexplored. The dataset, while a good starting point, is limited in its overall scale.
    *   The *implicit* relation extraction process relies on SCIBERT for pseudo-labeling, which introduces potential bias. Exploring other methods or combining multiple methods might improve the quality of implicit relations.
    *   It could explore fine-tuning LLMs as well, which may close some of the gap between SLMs and LLMs.
    *   The paper doesn't explore the utility of MISMATCHED to other downstream tasks to demonstrate the benefit of increasing the diversity of Scientific NLI datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to have a moderate influence on the field. The MISMATCHED dataset will serve as a valuable benchmark for researchers working on scientific NLI and will encourage the development of more generalizable models. The findings regarding implicit relations offer a promising direction for future research. However, its impact is somewhat limited by the relatively small scale of the dataset and the fact that only 3 non-CS domains are covered.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper introduces a valuable, novel dataset and tackles an important problem by addressing the CS domain bias in existing scientific NLI datasets, and shows an interesting approach to improving model performance through the incorporation of implicit relations.  It includes rigorous experimentation and strong baselines, setting a solid foundation for future work.  While the limited scale and coverage of the MISMATCHED dataset are weaknesses, the paper's strengths outweigh its limitations. It will encourage further research and drive progress in the field of scientific NLI and potentially improve the robustness of models employed in downstream scientific text mining applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SmartAvatar: Text- and Image-Guided Human Avatar Generation with VLM AI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04606v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Huang-Menders, Xinhang Liu, Andy Xu, Yuyao Zhang, Chi-Keung Tang, Yu-Wing Tai
- **Abstract**: SmartAvatar is a vision-language-agent-driven framework for generating fully rigged, animation-ready 3D human avatars from a single photo or textual prompt. While diffusion-based methods have made progress in general 3D object generation, they continue to struggle with precise control over human identity, body shape, and animation readiness. In contrast, SmartAvatar leverages the commonsense reasoning capabilities of large vision-language models (VLMs) in combination with off-the-shelf parametric human generators to deliver high-quality, customizable avatars. A key innovation is an autonomous verification loop, where the agent renders draft avatars, evaluates facial similarity, anatomical plausibility, and prompt alignment, and iteratively adjusts generation parameters for convergence. This interactive, AI-guided refinement process promotes fine-grained control over both facial and body features, enabling users to iteratively refine their avatars via natural-language conversations. Unlike diffusion models that rely on static pre-trained datasets and offer limited flexibility, SmartAvatar brings users into the modeling loop and ensures continuous improvement through an LLM-driven procedural generation and verification system. The generated avatars are fully rigged and support pose manipulation with consistent identity and appearance, making them suitable for downstream animation and interactive applications. Quantitative benchmarks and user studies demonstrate that SmartAvatar outperforms recent text- and image-driven avatar generation systems in terms of reconstructed mesh quality, identity fidelity, attribute accuracy, and animation readiness, making it a versatile tool for realistic, customizable avatar creation on consumer-grade hardware.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SmartAvatar paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmartAvatar, a novel framework for generating fully rigged and animation-ready 3D human avatars from a single image or text prompt. The key innovation lies in using a vision-language-agent (VLM) driven approach to iteratively refine avatar generation.  SmartAvatar leverages VLMs for semantic parsing, code synthesis (generating Blender-compatible Python code), scene rendering, and auto-verification. The auto-verification loop assesses facial similarity, anatomical plausibility, and prompt alignment, iteratively adjusting generation parameters until the output aligns with user intent. This allows for conversational avatar editing using natural language and provides detailed control for both novice and expert users. The paper demonstrates that SmartAvatar outperforms existing methods in mesh quality, identity fidelity, attribute accuracy, and animation readiness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the specific integration of a VLM-driven agentic workflow for avatar creation, particularly the closed-loop auto-verification process. Combining VLMs with parametric human generators is not entirely novel, as previous work has explored similar directions. However, the iterative refinement, AI-guided parameter tuning, and focus on animation readiness represent a significant advancement.  The use of chain-of-thought prompting within each agent is also a valuable contribution. While individual components exist, the combination and specifically orchestrated workflow are new.

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact of SmartAvatar is significant. The ability to generate controllable, high-quality avatars from simple inputs could greatly lower the barrier to entry for creating virtual characters in gaming, VR/AR, and digital fashion. The conversational editing feature enhances accessibility and user control.  The framework's emphasis on animation readiness addresses a crucial limitation of many existing avatar generation methods. The integration of the pipeline with Blender using open source models also increases the impact of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Framework:**  The paper provides a clear explanation of the SmartAvatar pipeline, including the roles of each LLM agent. The modular design improves explainability.
    *   **Emphasis on Controllability and Editability:** The iterative refinement loop and natural language editing capabilities provide users with fine-grained control over the avatar creation process.
    *   **Animation Readiness:** The focus on generating fully rigged avatars that are ready for animation is a significant advantage.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents both qualitative and quantitative results, comparing SmartAvatar to state-of-the-art methods.  The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component.
    *   **Use of Open-Source Tools:** Leveraging Blender and HumGen3D fosters community engagement and reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on HumGen3D:**  The reliance on HumGen3D, while practical, inherently limits the realism and detail of the generated avatars. The authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest exploring more expressive human modeling tools in future work.
    *   **Quantitative Metric Limitations:** The similarity scores reported can be misleading, as they can be influenced by texture alignment rather than accurate 3D geometry.  The paper acknowledges this but requires readers to be critical of the reported numbers.
    *   **Generalization of VLMs:** The LLaMA 4 model did not converge in this specific pipeline. The authors report issues with LLaMA4 being usable within the framework. While the work is only tested on a small subset of models, it potentially introduces concerns about how the design generalizes beyond high-performance VLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** SmartAvatar has the potential to influence research in controllable avatar generation, VLM-driven content creation, and AI-assisted design tools. The auto-verification loop provides a valuable framework for bridging the gap between symbolic control and perceptual fidelity. Further exploration of this pipeline with other parametric models, generative frameworks, and broader user studies has the potential to further solidify the value of this type of work.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, SmartAvatar represents a significant advance in controllable avatar generation. The use of a VLM-driven agentic workflow with an iterative refinement loop is a notable innovation. While the reliance on HumGen3D is a limitation, the framework's strengths in controllability, editability, and animation readiness outweigh this drawback. The clear presentation, comprehensive evaluation, and use of open-source tools contribute to the paper's impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring bidirectional bounds for minimax-training of Energy-based models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04609v1)
- **Authors**: Cong Geng, Jia Wang, Li Chen, Zhiyong Gao, Jes Frellsen, Søren Hauberg
- **Abstract**: Energy-based models (EBMs) estimate unnormalized densities in an elegant framework, but they are generally difficult to train. Recent work has linked EBMs to generative adversarial networks, by noting that they can be trained through a minimax game using a variational lower bound. To avoid the instabilities caused by minimizing a lower bound, we propose to instead work with bidirectional bounds, meaning that we maximize a lower bound and minimize an upper bound when training the EBM. We investigate four different bounds on the log-likelihood derived from different perspectives. We derive lower bounds based on the singular values of the generator Jacobian and on mutual information. To upper bound the negative log-likelihood, we consider a gradient penalty-like bound, as well as one based on diffusion processes. In all cases, we provide algorithms for evaluating the bounds. We compare the different bounds to investigate, the pros and cons of the different approaches. Finally, we demonstrate that the use of bidirectional bounds stabilizes EBM training and yields high-quality density estimation and sample generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the difficulty of training Energy-Based Models (EBMs) by proposing a novel approach based on bidirectional bounds.  Instead of just minimizing a variational lower bound on the negative log-likelihood (as in adversarial training), the authors suggest *simultaneously* maximizing a lower bound and minimizing an upper bound. They explore four different bounds: two lower bounds based on the generator Jacobian's singular values and mutual information, and two upper bounds derived from a gradient penalty-like term and diffusion processes. Algorithms for evaluating these bounds are presented, and the performance of the resulting minimax training strategy is evaluated empirically on toy data and standard image datasets. The paper demonstrates that bidirectional bounds can stabilize EBM training and lead to high-quality density estimation and sample generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the introduction of *bidirectional bounds* for training EBMs. While the connection between EBMs and GANs through variational lower bounds has been explored previously, the idea of explicitly employing *both* upper and lower bounds is a significant departure. Using both upper and lower bounds is akin to sandwiching the actual value of negative log-likelihood, thereby stabilizing training, something which the results seem to point out. The specific bounds themselves (singular values, mutual information, gradient penalties, diffusion) have precedents in other contexts, but their combination and application within this minimax framework is new.
* **Significance:**  EBMs offer a compelling framework for density estimation, but their training is notoriously difficult. The proposed bidirectional bound approach has the potential to address these training challenges, making EBMs more practical and competitive with other generative models like GANs and diffusion models. By stabilizing training and addressing mode collapse, the paper contributes towards making EBMs more reliable for density estimation and generative tasks, which is an important objective in the field. The results show that EBMs using the proposed method perform either better or at-par with other contemporary models.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problems with minimizing only a lower bound and provides a compelling rationale for the bidirectional approach.
    * **Comprehensive Exploration:** The paper explores a variety of different bounds, providing insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on toy data and image datasets support the effectiveness of the proposed method, demonstrating improved stability and performance.
    * **Thoroughness:** The paper delves into related theoretical analyses such as derivation of tightest bounds to back up the results.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:**  Some of the bounds, particularly those involving singular value calculations and diffusion processes, can be computationally expensive. The paper mitigates this to some extent by providing efficient algorithms but could provide clearer guidance on the trade-offs between computational cost and performance for each bound.
    * **Heuristic Elements:**  The diffusion-based upper bound relies on a specific choice of energy function that, while effective in practice, lacks a strict theoretical guarantee about bound validity. This aspect of the approach remains somewhat heuristic.
    * **Limited Comparison:** While comparisons are made to standard GANs and existing EBM methods, more extensive comparisons to recent advances in diffusion models or other state-of-the-art generative approaches would further strengthen the paper's impact.
    * **Practicality:** Even with proposed optimizations to the algorithm, the complexity of calculation and implementation might be a limiting factor.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by offering a more stable and effective way to train EBMs. The bidirectional bound approach could inspire further research into alternative bounds and training strategies for generative models. The study of the properties of those bounds such as tightness and the method to find them, could prove fruitful as well.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach (bidirectional bounds) to a significant problem (training EBMs), supported by solid empirical evidence. While some components (individual bounds, network architecture) are not entirely new, the combination and application are innovative. There are some limitations in terms of computational cost and the heuristic nature of certain aspects, but the paper's contributions outweigh these weaknesses. For this, it earns a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Revisiting Test-Time Scaling: A Survey and a Diversity-Aware Method for Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04611v1)
- **Authors**: Ho-Lam Chung, Teng-Yun Hsiao, Hsiao-Ying Huang, Chunerh Cho, Jian-Ren Lin, Zhang Ziwei, Yun-Nung Chen
- **Abstract**: Test-Time Scaling (TTS) improves the reasoning performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) by allocating additional compute during inference. We conduct a structured survey of TTS methods and categorize them into sampling-based, search-based, and trajectory optimization strategies. We observe that reasoning-optimized models often produce less diverse outputs, which limits TTS effectiveness. To address this, we propose ADAPT (A Diversity Aware Prefix fine-Tuning), a lightweight method that applies prefix tuning with a diversity-focused data strategy. Experiments on mathematical reasoning tasks show that ADAPT reaches 80% accuracy using eight times less compute than strong baselines. Our findings highlight the essential role of generative diversity in maximizing TTS effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of improving the reasoning performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) using Test-Time Scaling (TTS), which involves allocating more compute during inference. The paper presents a survey of TTS methods, categorizing them into sampling-based, search-based, and trajectory optimization strategies. A key observation is that reasoning-optimized models often lack generative diversity, limiting the effectiveness of TTS. To counter this, the authors propose ADAPT (A Diversity Aware Prefix fine-Tuning), a lightweight method that uses prefix tuning with a diversity-focused data strategy. Experiments on mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate that ADAPT achieves comparable accuracy with significantly less compute compared to strong baselines, highlighting the importance of generative diversity for maximizing TTS effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its structured survey of TTS methods with a focus on the role of generative diversity, and the proposal of ADAPT to explicitly address this issue. While prefix tuning is not a new technique *per se*, the application of prefix tuning *specifically to improve diversity for test-time scaling in reasoning models* is a significant contribution. The insight linking reasoning-optimized model design to reduced diversity, hindering TTS, is also novel. The paper clearly outlines the ADAPT approach.

* **Significance:** The finding that generative diversity is crucial for the effectiveness of TTS is a significant contribution. Reasoning models are often trained with objectives that encourage determinism and correctness, which can unintentionally reduce diversity. The results showing that ADAPT can achieve competitive accuracy with significantly reduced computational cost are highly valuable, suggesting a practical way to improve the efficiency of LLM inference.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-structured Survey:** The categorization of TTS methods provides a useful framework for understanding the landscape.
    * **Clear Hypothesis:** The paper formulates a specific hypothesis about the role of generative diversity.
    * **Effective Method:** ADAPT is a relatively simple and efficient method that yields significant improvements.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments on mathematical reasoning tasks provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of ADAPT.
    * **Well-written:** The paper is well-written, with a clear and logical flow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to a single domain (mathematical reasoning), and the generalizability to other tasks is not fully explored.
    * **Model Size:** Evaluating on larger models would strengthen the results. The 1.5B parameter range may not be fully representative of modern LLMs.
    * **Indirect Diversity Measurement:** The paper does not directly measure or optimize for diversity. The effect of ADAPT is inferred from improvements in accuracy.
    * **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The sensitivity of ADAPT to prefix length and data mixture ratio is not extensively investigated.

* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of reasoning models and TTS strategies. The insight that diversity is important could lead to new training objectives and fine-tuning methods that explicitly promote it. ADAPT provides a practical approach for improving the efficiency of reasoning models, which could be widely adopted.

* **Rigorous Rationale** The paper delivers on a rigorous analysis of current scaling methods and provides a targeted solution with a clear hypothesis. The method is lightweight which allows a focus on implementation. Although the scope of the paper is limited to a single domain, the clear presentation and results offer a promising and easily extendable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by identifying the importance of generative diversity in TTS for reasoning models and proposing a practical method for addressing this issue. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of ADAPT in a mathematical reasoning setting. While the limitations discussed above prevent a higher score, the paper's insights and practical method have the potential to significantly impact the design and use of LLMs for reasoning tasks. The approach is straightforward to implement, well tested, and shows promising impact making it a well-formed and impactful paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Perfecting Depth: Uncertainty-Aware Enhancement of Metric Depth
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04612v1)
- **Authors**: Jinyoung Jun, Lei Chu, Jiahao Li, Yan Lu, Chang-Su Kim
- **Abstract**: We propose a novel two-stage framework for sensor depth enhancement, called Perfecting Depth. This framework leverages the stochastic nature of diffusion models to automatically detect unreliable depth regions while preserving geometric cues. In the first stage (stochastic estimation), the method identifies unreliable measurements and infers geometric structure by leveraging a training-inference domain gap. In the second stage (deterministic refinement), it enforces structural consistency and pixel-level accuracy using the uncertainty map derived from the first stage. By combining stochastic uncertainty modeling with deterministic refinement, our method yields dense, artifact-free depth maps with improved reliability. Experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness across diverse real-world scenarios. Furthermore, theoretical analysis, various experiments, and qualitative visualizations validate its robustness and scalability. Our framework sets a new baseline for sensor depth enhancement, with potential applications in autonomous driving, robotics, and immersive technologies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Perfecting Depth: Uncertainty-Aware Enhancement of Metric Depth":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel two-stage framework called "Perfecting Depth" for enhancing sensor-captured depth maps. The first stage, "stochastic estimation," uses a diffusion model trained on clean synthetic data but applied to noisy real-world data. This training-inference domain gap allows the model to identify unreliable depth measurements and infer geometric cues while simultaneously estimating pixel-wise uncertainty.  The second stage, "deterministic refinement," uses a refinement network to enforce structural consistency and pixel-level accuracy, focusing on regions identified as uncertain in the first stage. By combining global uncertainty estimation via stochastic sampling with precise local refinement, the framework aims to generate dense, artifact-free, and reliable depth maps for real-world applications like autonomous driving and robotics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novelty:** The core idea of leveraging the stochastic nature of diffusion models to *automatically* detect unreliable depth regions by exploiting a training-inference gap is relatively novel.  Previous methods often rely on hand-crafted priors or heuristics to identify and correct depth errors. Combining this with a deterministic refinement stage is also a well-motivated approach to balance global consistency and local accuracy.  Using the variance of multiple diffusion samples as an uncertainty measure is a clever technique.
    * **Technical Soundness:**  The approach appears technically sound. The two-stage framework is logically structured. The use of a masked autoencoder (MAE) and spatial propagation network is justified by their suitability for depth reconstruction and refinement. The mathematical formulation appears correct.
    * **Experimental Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across diverse real-world datasets (DIODE-Indoor, NYUv2, ScanNet).  The comparisons against existing methods (especially relative depth estimators) and ablation studies (Diff. only vs. Proposed) provide evidence for the benefits of the full framework.  Qualitative results also effectively showcase the ability to remove noise and preserve edges.
    * **Generalization:** The paper makes a convincing case for its ability to generalize from synthetic training data to real-world scenarios. The training-inference gap strategy is effective in real noisy data.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework, its motivations, and the experimental setup.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** While the authors demonstrate good generalization, the method still relies on synthetic data for the initial diffusion model training. The success of the framework depends heavily on the realism and diversity of the synthetic dataset and on the specific architecture choice. If the synthetic domain is not carefully designed, then the entire system may fail.
    * **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving a diffusion model, a refinement network, and a series of intermediate steps. This complexity might make it harder to train and deploy than simpler, end-to-end approaches. Also, having to run multiple diffusion samples significantly increases computational complexity.
    * **Limited Comparisons:** The direct comparisons against more recent state-of-the-art depth completion or depth enhancement methods seem limited.  More extensive comparisons would strengthen the claims. For example, comparing against alternative uncertainty estimation techniques in neural networks would be useful.
    * **Zero-Depth handling:** The paper briefly mentions the need for an additional mask for infinite-depth regions. Addressing this limitation or at least discussing it more thoroughly would enhance the practical utility of the method. Adaptive normalization may be useful.

* **Significance:**

    * **Impact:** The potential impact of this work lies in enabling more robust and accurate depth perception for various applications, particularly in autonomous driving, robotics, and augmented reality.  The approach addresses a critical need for handling noisy or incomplete depth data, which is common in real-world deployments. The potential for reduced reliance on real-world training data is a plus.
    * **Future research:** This paper could inspire further research on using diffusion models for uncertainty estimation and depth map enhancement. The approach may lead to more robust and reliable depth perception systems.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound framework for sensor depth enhancement. The use of a training-inference gap with a diffusion model is a clever way to detect unreliable depth regions without hand-crafted priors. The experimental results demonstrate strong performance and good generalization ability. While the reliance on synthetic data and the complexity of the framework are limitations, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field and has the potential to enable more robust and accurate depth perception systems.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Look Before You Leap: A GUI-Critic-R1 Model for Pre-Operative Error Diagnosis in GUI Automation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04614v1)
- **Authors**: Yuyang Wanyan, Xi Zhang, Haiyang Xu, Haowei Liu, Junyang Wang, Jiabo Ye, Yutong Kou, Ming Yan, Fei Huang, Xiaoshan Yang, Weiming Dong, Changsheng Xu
- **Abstract**: In recent years, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have been extensively utilized for multimodal reasoning tasks, including Graphical User Interface (GUI) automation. Unlike general offline multimodal tasks, GUI automation is executed in online interactive environments, necessitating step-by-step decision-making based on real-time status of the environment. This task has a lower tolerance for decision-making errors at each step, as any mistakes may cumulatively disrupt the process and potentially lead to irreversible outcomes like deletions or payments. To address these issues, we introduce a pre-operative critic mechanism that provides effective feedback prior to the actual execution, by reasoning about the potential outcome and correctness of actions. Specifically, we propose a Suggestion-aware Gradient Relative Policy Optimization (S-GRPO) strategy to construct our pre-operative critic model GUI-Critic-R1, incorporating a novel suggestion reward to enhance the reliability of the model's feedback. Furthermore, we develop a reasoning-bootstrapping based data collection pipeline to create a GUI-Critic-Train and a GUI-Critic-Test, filling existing gaps in GUI critic data. Static experiments on the GUI-Critic-Test across both mobile and web domains reveal that our GUI-Critic-R1 offers significant advantages in critic accuracy compared to current MLLMs. Dynamic evaluation on GUI automation benchmark further highlights the effectiveness and superiority of our model, as evidenced by improved success rates and operational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Look Before You Leap: A GUI-Critic-R1 Model for Pre-Operative Error Diagnosis in GUI Automation" introduces a pre-operative critic mechanism for improving the reliability and efficiency of GUI automation agents.  Instead of correcting errors *after* they've been made (which can lead to irreversible consequences like deleting files), the proposed `GUI-Critic-R1` model *evaluates actions before they are executed*. The model reasons about potential outcomes, assesses correctness, and provides real-time feedback to the agent. The core components are:

*   **GUI-Critic-R1 Model:** A specialized 7B language model trained for GUI pre-critic tasks.
*   **Suggestion-aware Gradient Relative Policy Optimization (S-GRPO):** A RL strategy incorporating a novel suggestion reward to improve feedback reliability.
*   **Reasoning-Bootstrapping Data Collection Pipeline:**  A method to create `GUI-Critic-Train` and `GUI-Critic-Test` datasets addressing the lack of dedicated critic data for GUI automation.

The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of its approach through experiments on both static critic evaluation benchmarks and a dynamic GUI automation benchmark, showing improved success rates and operational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of a *pre-operative* critic in GUI automation is a valuable contribution.  Most existing error-correction mechanisms in GUI automation operate *post-hoc*.  The paper directly addresses the problem of irreversible errors and the need for optimal path planning. The S-GRPO with the suggestion reward is a solid novel technique. Creating the train/test sets is also a positive contribution.

*   **Significance:**  GUI automation is a rapidly growing area, and improving the robustness and efficiency of agents has significant practical implications. A pre-operative critic has the potential to reduce failure rates and optimize the number of steps required to complete tasks, making GUI automation more reliable and user-friendly. The data set they are creating is important as well.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly identifies a critical challenge in GUI automation.
    *   **Technical Approach:**  The proposed solution is well-engineered. S-GRPO and the reasoning-bootstrapping data collection pipeline are important.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of `GUI-Critic-R1` both in static settings (critic accuracy) and dynamic settings (GUI automation success rate and efficiency).
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** Releasing the code and datasets will encourage further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Size:** While the paper emphasizes efficiency, a 7B model might still be considered computationally expensive for real-time operation on resource-constrained devices (e.g., smartphones).  Further exploration of model distillation or other techniques to reduce model size without sacrificing performance would be beneficial. The paper discusses lighter models but more detail would be helpful.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** The dynamic evaluation is limited to AndroidWorld, which is a simulated environment.  While useful for initial validation, testing on real-world devices and a broader range of applications would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Dependency on MLLMs:** The pre-critic hinges on the reasoning capabilities of LLMs to produce outputs, and this can impact performance.

* **Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem in GUI automation. The pre-operative critic is a valuable contribution, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness. The creation of new datasets is very important for the community. However, the reliance on a relatively large model, the limited evaluation environment, and the dependence on MLLM reasoning accuracy prevent it from receiving a higher score. With further research and improvements, the `GUI-Critic-R1` model has the potential to have a significant impact on the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Advancing Tool-Augmented Large Language Models via Meta-Verification and Reflection Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04625v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Ma, Jiayu Liu, Xianzhen Luo, Zhenya Huang, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che
- **Abstract**: Empowering large language models (LLMs) with effective tool utilization capabilities is crucial for enabling AI agents to solve complex problems. However, current models face two major limitations: (1) unreliable tool planning and invocation due to low-quality instruction datasets (e.g., widespread hallucinated API calls), and (2) weak tool reflection abilities (over 90% of errors cannot be corrected) resulting from static imitation learning. To address these critical limitations, we propose Tool-MVR, a novel Tool-Augmented LLM that achieves comprehensive System 2 reasoning through two key innovations. Specifically, we first introduce Multi-Agent Meta-Verification (MAMV), a systematic pipeline that rigorously validates APIs, queries, and reasoning trajectories to construct ToolBench-V, a new high-quality instruction dataset that addresses the limitation of unreliable tool planning and invocation. Second, we propose Exploration-based Reflection Learning (EXPLORE), which enhances tool reflection capabilities by leveraging tool feedback through a dynamic "Error -> Reflection -> Correction" learning paradigm, resulting in our reflection dataset ToolBench-R and addressing the critical weakness in tool reflection. Finally, we obtain Tool-MVR by finetuning open-source LLMs (e.g., Qwen-7B) on both ToolBench-V and ToolBench-R. Our experiments demonstrate that Tool-MVR achieves state-of-the-art performance on StableToolBench, surpassing both ToolLLM (by 23.9%) and GPT-4 (by 15.3%) while reducing API calls by 31.4%, with strong generalization capabilities across unseen tools and scenarios. Additionally, on our proposed RefineToolBench, the first benchmark specifically designed to evaluate tool reflection capabilities, Tool-MVR achieves a 58.9% error correction rate, significantly outperforming ToolLLM's 9.1%.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and rigorous critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tool-MVR, a novel tool-augmented large language model (LLM) designed to improve tool utilization capabilities. It addresses two key limitations of existing models: unreliable tool planning and invocation (often due to low-quality instruction datasets and hallucinated API calls) and weak tool reflection abilities (difficulty in correcting errors based on tool feedback). Tool-MVR achieves this through two innovations: Multi-Agent Meta-Verification (MAMV), a pipeline to create a high-quality instruction dataset (ToolBench-V) by rigorously validating APIs, queries, and reasoning trajectories, and Exploration-based Reflection Learning (EXPLORE), which enhances tool reflection by learning from tool feedback in an "Error → Reflection → Correction" paradigm, resulting in the ToolBench-R dataset.  Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on StableToolBench, surpassing ToolLLM and GPT-4 while reducing API calls. Additionally, it shows a significant error correction rate on a new benchmark (RefineToolBench) designed to evaluate tool reflection capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, MAMV and EXPLORE, which, while not entirely orthogonal to existing work, are integrated in a novel way to address a specific problem in tool-augmented LLMs.  The novelty primarily lies in the *systematic approach to data curation and error-driven learning*. The development of RefineToolBench is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** Tool learning is a crucial area for expanding the capabilities of LLMs.  The paper directly addresses key weaknesses in existing approaches, particularly the reliance on noisy datasets and the lack of effective error correction. The improvements in performance, efficiency, and generalization suggest that Tool-MVR could significantly advance the field. The reduction of API calls is also practically significant for real-world deployments.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing tool-augmented LLMs.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The combination of MAMV and EXPLORE provides a holistic solution addressing both data quality and learning methodologies.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are thorough, with comparisons to strong baselines (GPT-4, ToolLLM) and ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of each component.
*   **New Benchmark:** The introduction of RefineToolBench is a valuable contribution, enabling more targeted evaluation of tool reflection capabilities.
*   **Significant Results:** The reported performance gains are substantial, especially in error correction and API call reduction.
*   **Generalization:** The paper includes statements highlighting the model's generalization performance across diverse tools and scenarios.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-4:** While the authors demonstrate improvement over GPT-4, the dataset curation heavily relies on GPT-4-powered agents for validation and generation, which might introduce biases and limitations. This should be addressed in detail.
*   **Limited Discussion of Scalability:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the scalability of MAMV and EXPLORE to larger datasets and a wider range of tools.
*   **Ablation Study Granularity:** The ablation study highlights the necessity of Stages 1 and 2. It would strengthen the claims if a more fine-grained component ablation were presented.
*   **Appendix Heavy:** A lot of important details are pushed to the appendix (evaluation details and protocols). Keeping such important aspects in the main body of the paper would improve the readability and impact.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Highlighting the importance of data quality in tool learning.
*   Promoting the use of error-driven learning for improving tool reflection.
*   Providing a new benchmark for evaluating tool reflection capabilities.
*   Guiding future research towards more efficient and robust tool-augmented LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of tool-augmented LLMs.  It addresses critical weaknesses in existing approaches with a well-designed and empirically validated solution. The novel combination of techniques and the creation of a new benchmark demonstrate a good level of innovation. However, the strong reliance on GPT-4 for data curation and lack of sufficient discussion on scalability and ablation experiments are limitations that slightly temper the overall assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unfolding Spatial Cognition: Evaluating Multimodal Models on Visual Simulations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04633v1)
- **Authors**: Linjie Li, Mahtab Bigverdi, Jiawei Gu, Zixian Ma, Yinuo Yang, Ziang Li, Yejin Choi, Ranjay Krishna
- **Abstract**: Spatial cognition is essential for human intelligence, enabling problem-solving through visual simulations rather than solely relying on verbal reasoning. However, existing AI benchmarks primarily assess verbal reasoning, neglecting the complexities of non-verbal, multi-step visual simulation. We introduce STARE(Spatial Transformations and Reasoning Evaluation), a benchmark designed to rigorously evaluate multimodal large language models on tasks better solved through multi-step visual simulation. STARE features 4K tasks spanning foundational geometric transformations (2D and 3D), integrated spatial reasoning (cube net folding and tangram puzzles), and real-world spatial reasoning (perspective and temporal reasoning), reflecting practical cognitive challenges like object assembly, mechanical diagram interpretation, and everyday spatial navigation. Our evaluations show that models excel at reasoning over simpler 2D transformations, but perform close to random chance on more complex tasks like 3D cube net folding and tangram puzzles that require multi-step visual simulations. Humans achieve near-perfect accuracy but take considerable time (up to 28.9s) on complex tasks, significantly speeding up (down by 7.5 seconds on average) with intermediate visual simulations. In contrast, models exhibit inconsistent performance gains from visual simulations, improving on most tasks but declining in specific cases like tangram puzzles (GPT-4o, o1) and cube net folding (Claude-3.5, Gemini-2.0 Flash), indicating that models may not know how to effectively leverage intermediate visual information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unfolding Spatial Cognition: Evaluating Multimodal Models on Visual Simulations":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STARE (Spatial Transformations and Reasoning Evaluation), a new benchmark designed to evaluate the spatial cognition abilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). STARE focuses on tasks that humans solve through visual simulation, such as geometric transformations (2D and 3D), cube net folding, tangram puzzles, and real-world spatial reasoning tasks like perspective and temporal reasoning. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art MLLMs on STARE and find that while models perform well on simpler tasks like 2D transformations, their performance is significantly lower on tasks requiring multi-step visual simulations, often approaching random chance.  The paper also investigates the impact of providing intermediate visual simulations, finding that models don't consistently benefit, suggesting a lack of effective utilization of visual guidance.  Finally, the authors analyze model errors, linking them to difficulties in 3D spatial reasoning and integrating visual information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of STARE is a significant contribution. Existing benchmarks tend to focus on either linguistic reasoning or static visual recognition, leaving a gap in evaluating the dynamic visual simulation capabilities crucial for spatial cognition.
