- **Score**: 6/10

### Lifelong Evolution: Collaborative Learning between Large and Small Language Models for Continuous Emergent Fake News Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04739v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyi Zhou, Xiaoming Zhang, Litian Zhang, Yibo Zhang, Zhenyu Guan, Chaozhuo Li, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: The widespread dissemination of fake news on social media has significantly impacted society, resulting in serious consequences. Conventional deep learning methodologies employing small language models (SLMs) suffer from extensive supervised training requirements and difficulties adapting to evolving news environments due to data scarcity and distribution shifts. Large language models (LLMs), despite robust zero-shot capabilities, fall short in accurately detecting fake news owing to outdated knowledge and the absence of suitable demonstrations. In this paper, we propose a novel Continuous Collaborative Emergent Fake News Detection (C$^2$EFND) framework to address these challenges. The C$^2$EFND framework strategically leverages both LLMs' generalization power and SLMs' classification expertise via a multi-round collaborative learning framework. We further introduce a lifelong knowledge editing module based on a Mixture-of-Experts architecture to incrementally update LLMs and a replay-based continue learning method to ensure SLMs retain prior knowledge without retraining entirely. Extensive experiments on Pheme and Twitter16 datasets demonstrate that C$^2$EFND significantly outperforms existed methods, effectively improving detection accuracy and adaptability in continuous emergent fake news scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Continuous Collaborative Emergent Fake News Detection (C2EFND) for addressing the challenge of detecting fake news in a continuous and evolving news environment. C2EFND leverages the strengths of both large language models (LLMs) and small language models (SLMs) through a multi-round collaborative learning approach.  It incorporates a lifelong knowledge editing module for LLMs based on a Mixture-of-Experts architecture, enabling incremental updates without extensive retraining.  Additionally, a replay-based continue learning method is employed for SLMs to retain prior knowledge.  The framework also includes a two-stage active learning strategy to minimize the reliance on large labeled datasets. Experiments on Pheme and Twitter16 datasets demonstrate that C2EFND outperforms existing methods in terms of detection accuracy and adaptability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a relatively good level of novelty. The combination of LLMs and SLMs in a multi-round collaborative learning framework for continuous fake news detection is a strong point. The lifelong knowledge editing mechanism for LLMs using a Mixture-of-Experts approach and the replay-based continual learning for SLMs are also notable.  The two-stage active learning module adds another layer of novelty, addressing the practical issue of limited labeled data. However, some components, like active learning, are inspired by existing techniques but are well-integrated into the overall framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical real-world problem: the spread of fake news in dynamic online environments. The C2EFND framework's ability to adapt to emergent news events and retain past knowledge is highly valuable.  The improvements in detection accuracy and adaptability demonstrated in the experiments are significant.  The framework's ability to function with limited labeled data is also a considerable advantage, making it practical for real-world deployment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive framework:** C2EFND provides a holistic approach to continuous fake news detection by addressing challenges related to data scarcity, knowledge updating, and effective model collaboration.
    *   **Effective collaboration:** The multi-round collaborative learning approach allows the model to combine the comprehensive reasoning capabilities of LLMs with the specialized classification skills of SLMs.
    *   **Adaptability:** The proposed framework is able to keep up with emergent news events because of its LLM editing abilities.
    *   **Robust performance:** The experimental results on two real-world datasets (Pheme and Twitter16) demonstrate the effectiveness of C2EFND in improving detection accuracy and adaptability.
    *   **Practicality:** The framework can function with a lower amount of labels compared to existing models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving multiple modules and training stages. While this complexity contributes to its effectiveness, it also increases the implementation and tuning effort.
    *   **Computational cost:** The use of LLMs and iterative training procedures can be computationally expensive, especially for large-scale deployments.
    *   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** Fine-tuning LLMs and balancing collaborative learning between different model sizes requires expert and expensive hyperparameter tuning.

*   **Impact and potential influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and adaptable fake news detection systems. The C2EFND framework's ability to continuously learn and adapt to evolving news environments could be valuable for social media platforms, news organizations, and fact-checking initiatives. The lifelong learning component and collaborative learning strategy offer new directions for research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-designed and comprehensive framework that addresses a significant problem with practical relevance. The novelty lies in the integration of several existing techniques, as well as the new LLM and SLM continue learning and adaptation components, into a cohesive system. The experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods. The limitations related to computational cost and complexity are acknowledged, but they do not significantly detract from the paper's overall value. The paper's potential influence on the field is considerable, but the true impact will depend on its adoption and adaptation in real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Layer GRPO: Enhancing Reasoning and Self-Correction in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04746v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Ding, Baiqiao Wang, Zijian Zeng, Youwei Wang
- **Abstract**: The Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm has demonstrated considerable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), as evidenced by DeepSeek-R1. However, the absence of intermediate supervision in GRPO frequently leads to inefficient exploration dynamics. A single error in a complex reasoning chain can invalidate the entire solution, resulting in abrupt reward vanishing and compromising training stability.To address these challenges, we propose MGRPO (Multi-layer GRPO). MGRPO operates in two layers: the first layer employs standard GRPO to generate an initial response. This response, along with the original query, is then fed into a second-layer GRPO process. This second layer is specifically trained to identify and correct errors in the initial response, effectively creating a self-correction loop. This mechanism provides implicit process-level supervision by rewarding successful error correction, without requiring an explicit, densely-annotated reward model. Experimental results on several mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MGRPO significantly outperforms standard GRPO, achieving superior performance by fostering both reasoning and self-correction abilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multi-Layer GRPO (MGRPO), an extension of the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm, aimed at enhancing reasoning and self-correction in large language models (LLMs). MGRPO employs a two-layer structure: the first layer generates initial responses using standard GRPO, and the second layer is trained to identify and correct errors in the initial response, effectively creating a self-correction loop.  The second layer leverages the output of the first layer to construct a correction-focused prompt. The approach uses a correction-augmentation-selection mechanism to focus on successfully corrected responses for training. Experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MGRPO outperforms standard GRPO and other baselines, showing improved performance in both reasoning and self-correction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a structured multi-layer learning paradigm that enables LLMs to simultaneously enhance reasoning capabilities and error correction abilities, allowing the model to learn from its own mistakes. Combining GRPO with a self-correction mechanism is a reasonable step. The use of successful corrections to augment the training data adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in reinforcement learning for LLMs: the need for dense process rewards without relying on explicit, densely-annotated reward models. The implicit process self-supervision approach provides a practical solution. Experiments on multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MGRPO significantly outperforms standard GRPO and other relevant baselines, suggesting the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The MGRPO framework presents a clear and structured approach to improve both reasoning and self-correction abilities in LLMs.
    *   The correction-augmentation-selection mechanism is a valuable contribution for focusing training on successful corrections.
    *   The experimental results show the significant advantages of MGRPO over existing methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The impact of the two stage framework relies on the ability of second stage to *identify* and *correct* the initial step. What if it can't even identify a bad step in the initial attempt? The correction step might not always improve and might actually worsen the solution. The authors mention the use of a selection mechanism to focus training on successful corrections, it still might be a limitation of this paradigm.
    *   The evaluation, while comprehensive, primarily focuses on mathematical reasoning tasks.  Generalizability to other types of reasoning or language tasks is not explicitly demonstrated.
    *   It is unclear how sensitive MGRPO is to the hyperparameters and the specific prompts used, and some analysis about sensitivity to hyperparamters would be beneficial.
*   **Impact:** The MGRPO approach has the potential to influence the design of future RL algorithms for LLMs, especially in scenarios where dense reward signals are difficult to obtain. It could also inspire new research directions in self-correction and error detection mechanisms in LLMs. The idea of recycling data generated during the training process is particularly attractive from a computational efficiency perspective.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. The MGRPO method offers a practical approach to improving reasoning and self-correction in LLMs without the need for expensive dense annotation. The experimental results are promising, showing clear improvements over standard GRPO and other baselines. The potential for computational efficiency by recycling data is also a significant advantage. However, the evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks, and the lack of generalizability, a core limitation of two stage framework where second stage must correct any wrong step makes it from being a higher scored research paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Truth in the Few: High-Value Data Selection for Efficient Multi-Modal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04755v1)
- **Authors**: Shenshen Li, Kaiyuan Deng, Lei Wang, Hao Yang, Chong Peng, Peng Yan, Fumin Shen, Heng Tao Shen, Xing Xu
- **Abstract**: While multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in complex reasoning tasks via reinforcement learning, it is commonly believed that extensive training data is necessary for improving multi-modal reasoning ability, inevitably leading to data redundancy and substantial computational costs. However, can smaller high-value datasets match or outperform full corpora for multi-modal reasoning in MLLMs? In this work, we challenge this assumption through a key observation: meaningful multi-modal reasoning is triggered by only a sparse subset of training samples, termed cognitive samples, whereas the majority contribute marginally. Building on this insight, we propose a novel data selection paradigm termed Reasoning Activation Potential (RAP), which identifies cognitive samples by estimating each sample's potential to stimulate genuine multi-modal reasoning by two complementary estimators: 1) Causal Discrepancy Estimator (CDE) based on the potential outcome model principle, eliminates samples that overly rely on language priors by comparing outputs between multi-modal and text-only inputs; 2) Attention Confidence Estimator (ACE), which exploits token-level self-attention to discard samples dominated by irrelevant but over-emphasized tokens in intermediate reasoning stages. Moreover, we introduce a Difficulty-aware Replacement Module (DRM) to substitute trivial instances with cognitively challenging ones, thereby ensuring complexity for robust multi-modal reasoning. Experiments on six datasets show that our RAP method consistently achieves superior performance using only 9.3% of the training data, while reducing computational costs by over 43%. Our code is available at https://github.com/Leo-ssl/RAP.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a rigorous evaluation of the paper "Truth in the Few: High-Value Data Selection for Efficient Multi-Modal Reasoning," based on the provided OCR text.

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of data redundancy and computational costs associated with training multi-modal large language models (MLLMs). The core idea is that only a small subset of training samples, termed "cognitive samples," significantly contributes to multi-modal reasoning ability. The authors propose a data selection paradigm called Reasoning Activation Potential (RAP) to identify these cognitive samples. RAP employs two estimators: Causal Discrepancy Estimator (CDE) to eliminate samples overly reliant on language priors, and Attention Confidence Estimator (ACE) to discard samples dominated by irrelevant tokens. Furthermore, a Difficulty-aware Replacement Module (DRM) replaces trivial instances with more challenging ones. Experiments on six datasets show that RAP achieves superior performance using only a small fraction of the training data while reducing computational costs. The authors claim a "truth in the few" phenomenon where smaller, high-quality datasets outperform full corpora.

**Rigorous Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of focusing on "cognitive samples" is a reasonable and potentially impactful observation. The novelty lies in the specific approach of identifying these samples using CDE and ACE. While the individual components (causal discrepancy estimation and attention-based confidence) might draw inspiration from existing techniques in causal inference and attention analysis, their combination and application to multi-modal data selection is a novel contribution. The DRM module adds a further layer of refinement to ensure sufficient data complexity. A potential limitation is the reliance on self-attention, which is already a common ingredient in transformer-based models. To emphasize novelty, the authors could more clearly articulate the differences between their CDE and ACE from existing methods within causal inference and attention analysis, respectively.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant practical problem: the high cost of training MLLMs. By demonstrating that a small fraction of data can achieve comparable or even superior performance, the paper offers a compelling solution for reducing computational overhead and improving efficiency. The claim of "truth in the few" is strong and highlights the potential for a paradigm shift in MLLM training. A valuable contribution is the analysis of why smaller, curated datasets outperform full corpora, specifically focusing on language priors and token attention issues. This helps guide future research efforts. Also, the DRM module is useful for handling some potential negative effects for the dataset reduction.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a critical issue in MLLM training: data redundancy and computational cost.
    *   The RAP framework provides a concrete and implementable solution for data selection.
    *   The experimental results across six datasets are compelling, demonstrating consistent performance gains.
    *   The analysis of language priors and attention biases provides valuable insights into MLLM behavior.
    *   The DRM module is effective for improving the MLLM ability to handle complex tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the components are well-motivated, the specific thresholds and hyperparameters used in CDE and ACE might require careful tuning and might not generalize well across all datasets and models. The paper could provide more guidance on setting these parameters.
    *   The novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon existing techniques in causal inference and attention analysis. A more detailed comparison with existing methods in these areas would strengthen the paper.
    *   The reliance on the initial model is critical to the effectiveness of the CDE and ACE. This initial model's performance may affect the data selected and, therefore, the final trained model.
    *   The DRM module's effectiveness relies on proper definition of the "challenging" samples, which can be complex.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the results are reproducible and generalize well to other MLLMs and datasets, the paper could have a significant impact on how MLLMs are trained. It could lead to more efficient and effective training procedures, reducing computational costs and promoting wider adoption of MLLMs. The insights into language priors and attention biases could also inform the design of new MLLM architectures and training objectives. The result that smaller high-quality datasets can perform much better than larger datasets has the potential to shift the focus from "scale everything" to "smart selection."

**Justification for Score**
The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to data selection for MLLMs. The idea of identifying and leveraging "cognitive samples" is insightful, and the RAP framework provides a concrete methodology for achieving this. While the novelty is somewhat incremental, the combination of CDE, ACE, and DRM, as well as the strong experimental results, warrant a positive assessment. However, some issues of hyperparameter tuning, potential biases from the initial model, and the sensitivity of DRM to challenging sample definition limit the paper's impact, preventing it from achieving a very high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exp4Fuse: A Rank Fusion Framework for Enhanced Sparse Retrieval using Large Language Model-based Query Expansion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04760v1)
- **Authors**: Lingyuan Liu, Mengxiang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown potential in generating hypothetical documents for query expansion, thereby enhancing information retrieval performance. However, the efficacy of this method is highly dependent on the quality of the generated documents, which often requires complex prompt strategies and the integration of advanced dense retrieval techniques. This can be both costly and computationally intensive. To mitigate these limitations, we explore the use of zero-shot LLM-based query expansion to improve sparse retrieval, particularly for learned sparse retrievers. We introduce a novel fusion ranking framework, Exp4Fuse, which enhances the performance of sparse retrievers through an indirect application of zero-shot LLM-based query expansion. Exp4Fuse operates by simultaneously considering two retrieval routes-one based on the original query and the other on the LLM-augmented query. It then generates two ranked lists using a sparse retriever and fuses them using a modified reciprocal rank fusion method. We conduct extensive evaluations of Exp4Fuse against leading LLM-based query expansion methods and advanced retrieval techniques on three MS MARCO-related datasets and seven low-resource datasets. Experimental results reveal that Exp4Fuse not only surpasses existing LLM-based query expansion methods in enhancing sparse retrievers but also, when combined with advanced sparse retrievers, achieves SOTA results on several benchmarks. This highlights the superior performance and effectiveness of Exp4Fuse in improving query expansion for sparse retrieval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Exp4Fuse, a novel rank fusion framework designed to enhance sparse retrieval by leveraging Large Language Model (LLM)-based query expansion.  Instead of directly using LLM-generated text in the query, Exp4Fuse indirectly applies this knowledge by creating two retrieval routes: one with the original query and the other with an LLM-augmented query.  The results from these two routes are then fused using a modified reciprocal rank fusion method. The authors conduct experiments on several datasets (MS MARCO, TREC-DL, and BEIR) to compare Exp4Fuse against existing LLM-based query expansion techniques, advanced retrieval methods, and multi-stage retrieval systems.  The results demonstrate that Exp4Fuse outperforms other LLM-based query expansion methods for sparse retrievers and achieves competitive, sometimes SOTA, performance when combined with advanced learned sparse retrievers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a moderately novel approach. The idea of using rank fusion isn't entirely new in information retrieval. However, the specific application of rank fusion with LLM-based query expansion in *this indirect way* (using LLM-generated documents to augment a retrieval route rather than directly rewriting the query) for sparse retrievers is a valuable and novel exploration. Using LLMs to generate "hypothetical documents" is similar to existing approaches like HyDE, but the fusion methodology and targeting learned sparse retrievers makes it a unique contribution. The modifications to the reciprocal rank fusion method are also a smaller, but tangible contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its ability to improve the performance of sparse retrievers, especially learned sparse retrievers, in a cost-effective manner. Learned sparse retrievers are important because they offer a good balance between performance and computational cost.  The fact that Exp4Fuse can be effectively combined with these retrievers to achieve competitive or even SOTA results is significant. This is particularly relevant as dense retrievers often require significant computational resources. The paper addresses the challenges of directly applying LLM-based QE to strong sparse retrievers. The consistent improvement across various datasets and metrics lends credibility to the approach. The success on the BEIR datasets also shows the approach can generalize to out-of-domain datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of suboptimal performance when directly applying LLM-based query expansion to learned sparse retrievers.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** Exp4Fuse is presented as a well-defined and easily implementable solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, using multiple datasets and comparing against a wide range of baselines, including strong dense retrievers and multi-stage retrieval systems. The ablation study provides insights into the importance of different components of the Exp4Fuse framework.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The focus on improving sparse retrieval and offering a cost-effective solution makes the research practically relevant to real-world information retrieval systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:** Like all LLM-based methods, Exp4Fuse's performance is dependent on the quality of the LLM used. The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't fully explore the impact of different LLMs or prompt engineering strategies on the framework's performance.
    *   **Reciprocal Rank Fusion Tuning:** The parameters of the modified reciprocal rank fusion method (particularly the *k* constant) are determined through a pilot study. A more systematic approach to parameter tuning could potentially improve the framework's performance further.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks deep theoretical analysis explaining *why* the proposed fusion strategy is effective. While the experiments provide empirical evidence, a more theoretical justification would strengthen the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** Exp4Fuse has the potential to influence the design of future information retrieval systems, especially those that prioritize efficiency and cost-effectiveness. The work could encourage further research into indirect methods for incorporating LLM-based knowledge into retrieval systems.

*   **Missing Details**: It would be nice if the code or appendix would provide further details about the prompt used for generating the hypothetical documents.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's moderate novelty and significant practical impact. Exp4Fuse provides a valuable and effective method for enhancing sparse retrieval using LLMs. It effectively addresses the problem of suboptimal performance when directly applying LLM-based query expansion to strong sparse retrievers. The limitations, such as LLM dependency and limited theoretical analysis, prevent a higher score. However, the comprehensive experimental evaluation and practical relevance of the approach make it a significant contribution to the field. It builds on existing methods, such as rank fusion and LLM-based hypothetical document generation, but the specific combination, targeting of learned sparse retrievers, and the observed performance gains warrant a solid score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Log-Linear Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04761v1)
- **Authors**: Han Guo, Songlin Yang, Tarushii Goel, Eric P. Xing, Tri Dao, Yoon Kim
- **Abstract**: The attention mechanism in Transformers is an important primitive for accurate and scalable sequence modeling. Its quadratic-compute and linear-memory complexity however remain significant bottlenecks. Linear attention and state-space models enable linear-time, constant-memory sequence modeling and can moreover be trained efficiently through matmul-rich parallelization across sequence length. However, at their core these models are still RNNs, and thus their use of a fixed-size hidden state to model the context is a fundamental limitation. This paper develops log-linear attention, an attention mechanism that balances linear attention's efficiency and the expressiveness of softmax attention. Log-linear attention replaces the fixed-size hidden state with a logarithmically growing set of hidden states. We show that with a particular growth function, log-linear attention admits a similarly matmul-rich parallel form whose compute cost is log-linear in sequence length. Log-linear attention is a general framework and can be applied on top of existing linear attention variants. As case studies, we instantiate log-linear variants of two recent architectures -- Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet -- and find they perform well compared to their linear-time variants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Log-Linear Attention":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "log-linear attention," a novel attention mechanism designed to bridge the gap between the efficiency of linear attention and the expressiveness of softmax attention.  Traditional attention mechanisms, particularly in Transformers, suffer from quadratic compute complexity, limiting their scalability.  Linear attention offers linear time and constant memory but often lacks the expressiveness needed for tasks requiring associative recall or long-range dependencies. The core idea is to replace the fixed-size hidden state of linear attention with a logarithmically growing set of hidden states, achieved through a Fenwick tree-based hierarchical partitioning of the input sequence.  This allows queries to attend to context at multiple temporal scales.  The authors demonstrate that, with a specific growth function, log-linear attention can be parallelized for training in a manner similar to linear attention, albeit with a compute cost that's log-linear in sequence length.  They instantiate log-linear variants of Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet, showing performance gains over their linear-time counterparts in various benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central contribution is the introduction of the log-linear attention mechanism itself. The Fenwick tree-based partitioning and the hierarchical attention masking scheme are genuinely novel elements. The adaptation of linear attention principles to incorporate a logarithmic set of hidden states is a clever way to address the expressiveness limitations of existing linear attention variants. While building on prior work on linear attention, state-space models, and structured matrices, the *specific combination* of these elements and their realization in the proposed algorithm are novel.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Potential Impact:** The potential impact is primarily in scaling sequence models to significantly longer contexts with more manageable compute costs compared to full attention. This has implications for tasks like long document summarization, complex reasoning, and processing high-resolution video. The modularity of the framework (being applicable to existing linear attention models) is also a significant advantage.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The empirical results, while demonstrating improvements over linear attention baselines, are not overwhelmingly convincing and still leave a substantial performance gap compared to Transformers. The experiments on academic-scale language modeling are compelling, but the need for further experiments on real-world applications with long sequences is notable. The benefits on tasks like LongBench suggest the value is in modelling longer context effectively.
    *   **Theoretical Underpinnings:** The connection to hierarchical matrices and the theoretical analysis of the algorithm's complexity provide a strong foundation for the approach.
    *   **Limitations:** The major weakness lies in the increased complexity of the algorithm and implementation, which the authors acknowledge. The bespoke implementation of intra-chunk operations and backward pass is a barrier to wider adoption. The fact that the method still fails to reach Transformer level performance is also concerning.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized. The structured matrix view of efficient attention variants is a useful framing. The explanation of the Fenwick tree-based partitioning is clear, and the algorithm descriptions are reasonably detailed. However, some aspects of the implementation (specifically the details of the level fusion and gradient computation optimization) could benefit from further elaboration.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   The core idea of log-linear attention is novel and well-motivated. The use of a Fenwick tree for hierarchical partitioning is an interesting and potentially powerful technique.
*   The paper demonstrates tangible improvements over linear attention baselines in certain tasks, particularly those requiring long-range context understanding (LongBench).
*   The modular design, which allows log-linear attention to be applied to existing linear attention architectures, is a valuable contribution.
*   However, the empirical results are not uniformly positive, and there remains a significant gap in performance compared to full attention mechanisms. The engineering complexity is considerable, and the limitations in implementation details provided in the paper, hamper immediate adoption. The novelty also relies on building on already established techniques (e.g. linear attention). Future research is required to assess the full potential of the approach and address the challenges associated with its implementation and scaling. Furthermore, the evaluation would be strengthened with tests that truly isolate the benefits for much longer sequence lengths than currently performed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GOLFer: Smaller LM-Generated Documents Hallucination Filter & Combiner for Query Expansion in Information Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04762v1)
- **Authors**: Lingyuan Liu, Mengxiang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs)-based query expansion for information retrieval augments queries with generated hypothetical documents with LLMs. However, its performance relies heavily on the scale of the language models (LMs), necessitating larger, more advanced LLMs. This approach is costly, computationally intensive, and often has limited accessibility. To address these limitations, we introduce GOLFer - Smaller LMs-Generated Documents Hallucination Filter & Combiner - a novel method leveraging smaller open-source LMs for query expansion. GOLFer comprises two modules: a hallucination filter and a documents combiner. The former detects and removes non-factual and inconsistent sentences in generated documents, a common issue with smaller LMs, while the latter combines the filtered content with the query using a weight vector to balance their influence. We evaluate GOLFer alongside dominant LLM-based query expansion methods on three web search and ten low-resource datasets. Experimental results demonstrate that GOLFer consistently outperforms other methods using smaller LMs, and maintains competitive performance against methods using large-size LLMs, demonstrating its effectiveness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GOLFer, a novel method for query expansion in information retrieval that leverages smaller open-source large language models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based query expansion techniques, which often rely on computationally expensive and less accessible large-scale LLMs. GOLFer consists of two main modules: a hallucination filter to detect and remove non-factual or inconsistent sentences generated by the smaller LLM, and a document combiner that combines the filtered content with the original query using a weighted approach. The method is evaluated on several web search and low-resource datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to other methods using smaller LMs and competitive performance against methods using large LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the architecture of GOLFer, specifically the hallucination filter designed for smaller LLMs and the weighted combiner. Previous works have addressed query expansion and hallucination detection separately, but GOLFer integrates these into a unified framework optimized for use with smaller models. The paper distinguishes itself by not simply employing an LLM and hoping it performs well, but by actively addressing the known limitations of smaller LLMs in terms of factual accuracy. The weighted combination of query and expanded documents is fairly standard, but its application here is tailored to the specific challenges of working with smaller LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to democratize LLM-based query expansion. By demonstrating that smaller, more accessible LMs can be effectively used for this task, the paper opens up possibilities for institutions and researchers with limited resources to improve their information retrieval systems. The method is lightweight and versatile, integrating without retraining, which is an advantage. The experimental results, showcasing consistently improved performance on multiple datasets, bolster the paper's claims of practical significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a clear and important problem: the dependence on large, costly LLMs for query expansion.
    *   Presents a well-defined and modular architecture (hallucination filter & combiner).
    *   Provides thorough experimental evaluation across diverse datasets, including web search and low-resource scenarios.
    *   Offers a competitive performance comparison against state-of-the-art methods, including those using larger LLMs.
    *   Detailed ablation study revealing the importance of each component
    *   Clear description of the methodology
    *   Code is provided

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty of the components is incremental, particularly the weighting scheme in the combiner, which, while effective, isn't radically new.
    *   The hallucination filter relies on self-attention, which isn't available through some APIs, potentially limiting its applicability. While stated as a limitation, it is a real limitation that has not been tackled in the current study.
    *   There may be some fine tuning, prompt engineering or parameter adjustments for the datasets, but it is not stated in the paper.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the approach can be easily adopted and replicated, GOLFer could have a measurable impact on the IR field by enabling more widespread adoption of LLM-based query expansion. It offers a practical solution for scenarios where access to massive LLMs is limited.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a solid, well-executed method that improves on existing approaches and addresses an important practical challenge. While the novelty of individual components is not ground-breaking, the combination and adaptation to smaller LLMs, along with the thorough experimental evaluation, make this a valuable contribution. Therefore, the paper warrants a score reflecting its practical value and methodological soundness.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### OpenGT: A Comprehensive Benchmark For Graph Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04765v1)
- **Authors**: Jiachen Tang, Zhonghao Wang, Sirui Chen, Sheng Zhou, Jiawei Chen, Jiajun Bu
- **Abstract**: Graph Transformers (GTs) have recently demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse domains. By leveraging attention mechanisms, GTs are capable of modeling long-range dependencies and complex structural relationships beyond local neighborhoods. However, their applicable scenarios are still underexplored, this highlights the need to identify when and why they excel. Furthermore, unlike GNNs, which predominantly rely on message-passing mechanisms, GTs exhibit a diverse design space in areas such as positional encoding, attention mechanisms, and graph-specific adaptations. Yet, it remains unclear which of these design choices are truly effective and under what conditions. As a result, the community currently lacks a comprehensive benchmark and library to promote a deeper understanding and further development of GTs. To address this gap, this paper introduces OpenGT, a comprehensive benchmark for Graph Transformers. OpenGT enables fair comparisons and multidimensional analysis by establishing standardized experimental settings and incorporating a broad selection of state-of-the-art GNNs and GTs. Our benchmark evaluates GTs from multiple perspectives, encompassing diverse tasks and datasets with varying properties. Through extensive experiments, our benchmark has uncovered several critical insights, including the difficulty of transferring models across task levels, the limitations of local attention, the efficiency trade-offs in several models, the application scenarios of specific positional encodings, and the preprocessing overhead of some positional encodings. We aspire for this work to establish a foundation for future graph transformer research emphasizing fairness, reproducibility, and generalizability. We have developed an easy-to-use library OpenGT for training and evaluating existing GTs. The benchmark code is available at https://github.com/eaglelab-zju/OpenGT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenGT, a comprehensive benchmark for Graph Transformers (GTs). Recognizing the growing importance of GTs for graph learning and the lack of a standardized evaluation framework, OpenGT aims to provide a unified and reproducible platform for comparing different GT architectures and implementations. The benchmark includes a diverse set of datasets (covering node-level and graph-level tasks with varying properties like size, homophily, and sparsity), implementations of 16 representative GT and GNN models, and a systematic evaluation pipeline.  Through extensive experiments, the paper identifies several critical insights regarding the effectiveness of positional encodings, the limitations of local attention, and the efficiency trade-offs in different GT designs. The authors hope OpenGT will foster future GT research by emphasizing fairness, reproducibility, and generalizability. They make the OpenGT library publicly available.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the graph learning community by addressing the critical need for a standardized benchmark for Graph Transformers. While individual GT models are being rapidly developed, their comparison is often inconsistent due to varying experimental setups. OpenGT helps to solve this. The contributions are as follows:

*   **Unified Benchmark Library:** The release of a comprehensive library with implementations of both GNNs and GTs under a unified framework is a significant step. It allows for fair comparisons and promotes reproducible research.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The extensive evaluation conducted across different task types, graph structures, and dataset scales offers valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different GT designs.
*   **Empirical Observations:** The identified limitations of local attention, the effectiveness of degree-based positional encodings in dense graphs, and the preprocessing overhead of certain positional encodings provide valuable guidelines for future research.
*   **Future Research Directions:** The actionable suggestions based on the findings are also beneficial.

However, there are also some limitations to consider in assessing the paper's novelty:

*   **Incremental Nature:** While the framework is valuable, many components (datasets, some models) are already available or adapted from existing libraries (e.g., torch\_geometric, graphgym). The core novelty lies in the *integration* and *systematic analysis*, rather than groundbreaking algorithmic contributions.
*   **Scope of Datasets:** While diverse, the number of datasets is not exceptionally large compared to other benchmarks. The conclusions might be limited to the types of graphs and tasks considered.
*   **Some Conclusions May Be Preliminary**: While the insights are helpful, several results (e.g. observations) can be quite specific and need validation for different architectures in the future.

Despite these limitations, the creation of OpenGT provides a crucial infrastructure for future GT research. The careful analysis and the open availability of the code will benefit the community and lead to a better understanding of GTs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's strong contribution to the graph learning community while acknowledging the limitations regarding groundbreaking algorithmic novelty.  The paper's strength lies in providing a unified and standardized evaluation platform for GTs, filling a significant gap in the field. The systematic evaluation reveals valuable insights that can guide future GT development and deployment. The insights on positional embeddings and partitioning are especially beneficial. However, the paper's incremental nature (building upon existing datasets and model implementations) and the limitations regarding the scope of dataset must be considered in the assessment of its significance. The quality and effort associated with implementation of the benchmark is apparent and well done, and the usefulness to the field is certain, meriting a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fine-Grained Interpretation of Political Opinions in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04774v1)
- **Authors**: Jingyu Hu, Mengyue Yang, Mengnan Du, Weiru Liu
- **Abstract**: Studies of LLMs' political opinions mainly rely on evaluations of their open-ended responses. Recent work indicates that there is a misalignment between LLMs' responses and their internal intentions. This motivates us to probe LLMs' internal mechanisms and help uncover their internal political states. Additionally, we found that the analysis of LLMs' political opinions often relies on single-axis concepts, which can lead to concept confounds. In this work, we extend the single-axis to multi-dimensions and apply interpretable representation engineering techniques for more transparent LLM political concept learning. Specifically, we designed a four-dimensional political learning framework and constructed a corresponding dataset for fine-grained political concept vector learning. These vectors can be used to detect and intervene in LLM internals. Experiments are conducted on eight open-source LLMs with three representation engineering techniques. Results show these vectors can disentangle political concept confounds. Detection tasks validate the semantic meaning of the vectors and show good generalization and robustness in OOD settings. Intervention Experiments show these vectors can intervene in LLMs to generate responses with different political leanings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of existing methods for analyzing political opinions in Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that previous approaches relying on single-axis (left-right) evaluations and open-ended responses neglect the complexity of political concepts and the potential misalignment between LLMs' internal intentions and their outputs. To overcome these limitations, the authors propose a fine-grained political learning framework based on interpretable representation engineering. This framework involves: (1) designing a four-dimensional political space (economic, diplomatic, civil, and society) to disentangle conceptual confounds, (2) creating a corresponding dataset of left/right-leaning statements across these dimensions, and (3) applying representation engineering techniques (CAA, RepE, Linear Probing) to learn disentangled political concept vectors from LLM internals. The effectiveness of these vectors is evaluated through detection and intervention tasks. Results demonstrate that these vectors can disentangle political concepts, accurately detect LLMs' internal states, and steer LLM outputs towards different political leanings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper introduces a more nuanced approach to understanding political bias in LLMs.  The extension from a single-axis to a multi-dimensional framework is a significant step forward, addressing the limitations of existing methodologies and moving beyond simple left/right binaries. The application of representation engineering within the political domain is relatively unexplored and provides a valuable contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:** While representation engineering techniques are themselves not novel, the application and adaptation to the specifically nuanced context of political bias assessment is. However, the usage of the standard approaches CAA, RepE, and linear probing might limit the novel contribution to the application.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper offers a practical framework and associated methodology that can be applied to more transparently explore and even potentially mitigate political biases in LLMs. The ability to detect and intervene in LLMs' internal states, rather than solely relying on output evaluations, could lead to the development of more robust and trustworthy AI systems.
*   **Weaknesses:** The scope of the evaluation could be broadened. While the experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach within the controlled setting, the paper could be strengthened by evaluating the framework's applicability to real-world scenarios where biases might be more subtle and complex. The impact of intervention experiments needs further investigation as the potential existence of guardrails raises ethical concerns about influencing the opinions of users interacting with the LLM.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing a critical limitation in existing approaches to analyzing political bias in LLMs. The multi-dimensional framework and application of representation engineering offer a novel perspective. While the core techniques are not fundamentally new, their adaptation to the political domain and the framework proposed demonstrate originality. A robust evaluation using detection and intervention tasks provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.

However, the relatively standard application of representation engineering and the limited scope of the evaluation prevent it from achieving the highest possible score. The exploration of only open-source models also restricts the scope. Further work is needed to investigate the applicability and ethical implications of intervention in more complex scenarios and to extend the analysis to a wider range of LLMs.

### MMSU: A Massive Multi-task Spoken Language Understanding and Reasoning Benchmark
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04779v1)
- **Authors**: Dingdong Wang, Jincenzi Wu, Junan Li, Dongchao Yang, Xueyuan Chen, Tianhua Zhang, Helen Meng
- **Abstract**: Speech inherently contains rich acoustic information that extends far beyond the textual language. In real-world spoken language understanding, effective interpretation often requires integrating semantic meaning (e.g., content), paralinguistic features (e.g., emotions, speed, pitch) and phonological characteristics (e.g., prosody, intonation, rhythm), which are embedded in speech. While recent multimodal Speech Large Language Models (SpeechLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in processing audio information, their ability to perform fine-grained perception and complex reasoning in natural speech remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we introduce MMSU, a comprehensive benchmark designed specifically for understanding and reasoning in spoken language. MMSU comprises 5,000 meticulously curated audio-question-answer triplets across 47 distinct tasks. To ground our benchmark in linguistic theory, we systematically incorporate a wide range of linguistic phenomena, including phonetics, prosody, rhetoric, syntactics, semantics, and paralinguistics. Through a rigorous evaluation of 14 advanced SpeechLLMs, we identify substantial room for improvement in existing models, highlighting meaningful directions for future optimization. MMSU establishes a new standard for comprehensive assessment of spoken language understanding, providing valuable insights for developing more sophisticated human-AI speech interaction systems. MMSU benchmark is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ddwang2000/MMSU. Evaluation Code is available at https://github.com/dingdongwang/MMSU_Bench.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMSU, a new benchmark for evaluating spoken language understanding and reasoning (SLU) in Speech Large Language Models (SpeechLLMs).  MMSU comprises 5,000 meticulously curated audio-question-answer triplets across 47 distinct tasks, grounded in linguistic theory and covering phonetics, prosody, rhetoric, syntactics, semantics, and paralinguistics. The authors evaluate 14 advanced SpeechLLMs, revealing significant room for improvement and highlighting directions for future research. The MMSU benchmark aims to standardize SLU assessment and provide insights for developing more sophisticated human-AI speech interaction systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the **comprehensive and theoretically grounded nature of the benchmark.** While individual tasks within SLU exist, MMSU's integration of a diverse range of linguistic phenomena, from low-level phonetics to high-level pragmatics, is a significant contribution. Previous benchmarks have often focused on speech recognition or content-level dialogue, neglecting the nuances of spoken language. The inclusion of real-world audio samples, rather than relying solely on synthesized speech, further enhances the benchmark's ecological validity. This is a noticeable improvement in the field.

**Significance:** The significance of this benchmark lies in its potential to **drive progress in SLU research.** The benchmark makes the existing models to tackle a more practical problems rather than just focusing on general audio performance. By providing a standardized framework with diverse tasks and clear metrics, MMSU facilitates fair comparison of different SpeechLLMs. The paper's evaluation highlights the current limitations of existing models, particularly in interpreting paralinguistic and prosodic cues, providing valuable guidance for future model development.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive coverage:** MMSU covers a wide range of linguistic and paralinguistic phenomena.
*   **Theoretical grounding:** The benchmark is rooted in established linguistic principles.
*   **Real-world data:** The use of authentic audio samples enhances ecological validity.
*   **Rigorous evaluation:** The evaluation of 14 SpeechLLMs provides a clear understanding of current capabilities and limitations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Data construction Bias:** There is potential bias and overfitting due to the data used during the data construction process.
*   **Human annotations dependency:** There is a reliance on high-quality human annotations for expert curated questions in MMSU.
*   **Scope of the dataset:** Limited speaker diversity and controlled audio condition within the MMSU dataset.

**Overall Impact:** MMSU has the potential to become a valuable resource for the SpeechLLM community. It provides a needed standardized and comprehensive assessment tool that should foster innovation in SLU model development. However, the limitations regarding the reliance on expert knowledge, potential data bias, and scope need to be considered.

### Towards LLM-Centric Multimodal Fusion: A Survey on Integration Strategies and Techniques
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04788v1)
- **Authors**: Jisu An, Junseok Lee, Jeoungeun Lee, Yongseok Son
- **Abstract**: The rapid progress of Multimodal Large Language Models(MLLMs) has transformed the AI landscape. These models combine pre-trained LLMs with various modality encoders. This integration requires a systematic understanding of how different modalities connect to the language backbone. Our survey presents an LLM-centric analysis of current approaches. We examine methods for transforming and aligning diverse modal inputs into the language embedding space. This addresses a significant gap in existing literature. We propose a classification framework for MLLMs based on three key dimensions. First, we examine architectural strategies for modality integration. This includes both the specific integration mechanisms and the fusion level. Second, we categorize representation learning techniques as either joint or coordinate representations. Third, we analyze training paradigms, including training strategies and objective functions. By examining 125 MLLMs developed between 2021 and 2025, we identify emerging patterns in the field. Our taxonomy provides researchers with a structured overview of current integration techniques. These insights aim to guide the development of more robust multimodal integration strategies for future models built on pre-trained foundations.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Towards LLM-Centric Multimodal Fusion: A Survey on Integration Strategies and Techniques."

**Summary**

The paper presents a survey of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), focusing on the integration strategies and techniques used to fuse different modalities (e.g., images, audio, video) with Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors propose a classification framework based on three key dimensions: (1) architectural strategies for modality fusion (including mechanisms and fusion levels), (2) representation learning paradigms (joint or coordinate), and (3) training methodologies (training strategies and objective functions). They analyze 125 MLLMs developed between 2021 and 2025, identifying emerging patterns and providing a structured overview of current integration techniques. The stated goal is to guide the development of more robust multimodal integration strategies for future models built on pre-trained foundations.  They also provide tables comparing and classifying various architectures.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty*: The paper's novelty lies primarily in its specific focus on LLM-centric multimodal fusion and the proposed three-dimensional classification framework. While previous surveys have covered MLLMs, this one carves out a niche by explicitly analyzing how modalities are integrated *into* LLMs, rather than just broadly surveying MLLM components. The proposed taxonomy of architectural strategies (Abstraction, Projection, Semantic Embedding, Cross-attention) is reasonably novel in the way it aims to disambiguate the *contextual intention* of different layers, which can be somewhat obscured.  However, the individual components themselves (e.g., cross-attention, projection layers) are well-established. The idea of fusion levels (early, intermediate, hybrid) is not entirely new, but the application of it within this LLM-centric framework adds value.  The attention to different *training* paradigms is also a strong point. The comparative tables summarizing a large number of recent papers are useful.

*Significance*: The paper's significance stems from the increasing importance of MLLMs in AI.  A structured overview of integration strategies is valuable for researchers and practitioners working in this rapidly evolving field. By highlighting emerging patterns and design principles, the survey can potentially accelerate the development of more effective MLLMs. The explicit attempt to clarify the functional roles of different architectural components is particularly valuable, addressing a practical challenge faced by many working in this area.

*Strengths*:
*   **Clear Focus:** The LLM-centric perspective provides a valuable and well-defined scope.
*   **Structured Framework:** The three-dimensional classification framework offers a systematic way to analyze and compare different MLLMs.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The analysis of 125 models provides a broad and well-supported basis for the conclusions.
*   **Practical Value:** The paper addresses a real challenge in the field by attempting to clarify the roles of different architectural components.
*   **Excellent Organization and Presentation:** The paper is well-written, clearly organized, and easy to follow. Tables provide excellent comparitive analysis.

*Weaknesses*:
*   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the breadth is good, the depth of analysis of *each* model is limited by the survey nature. The survey provides more of a categorized overview than a deep dive into the specific design choices and rationales behind them.
*   **Subjectivity in Categorization:** Categorizing architectural components and fusion mechanisms can be somewhat subjective, as components can have multiple functions depending on the context. While the paper acknowledges this, the taxonomy is still somewhat rigid.  The authors do a good job of trying to account for this ambiguity.
*   **Descriptive vs. Prescriptive:**  The paper is primarily descriptive, summarizing existing approaches.  It offers less in the way of concrete prescriptive guidelines for designing *better* MLLMs, beyond identifying patterns. While the analysis provides insight, there is less emphasis on novel research problems and challenges.
*   **Future Prediction is Difficult:** Given the pace of innovation in the field, some aspects of the survey may become outdated relatively quickly.

*Potential Influence*: The paper has the potential to be widely cited and used as a reference by researchers and practitioners in the MLLM field. It provides a valuable starting point for understanding the landscape of integration strategies and can inform the design of future models. It could serve as a useful reference for anyone entering this field.

*Justification for Score*:

The paper is a well-executed survey with a clear focus, a valuable classification framework, and a comprehensive analysis of a large number of MLLMs. It addresses a relevant challenge in the field and provides practical value for researchers and practitioners. While the survey nature limits the depth of analysis and the categorization can be somewhat subjective, the overall contribution is significant.  It's more of an excellent survey than a groundbreaking paper presenting a new technique, which influences the score.

### Dissecting Logical Reasoning in LLMs: A Fine-Grained Evaluation and Supervision Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04810v1)
- **Authors**: Yujun Zhou, Jiayi Ye, Zipeng Ling, Yufei Han, Yue Huang, Haomin Zhuang, Zhenwen Liang, Kehan Guo, Taicheng Guo, Xiangqi Wang, Xiangliang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Logical reasoning is a core capability for many applications of large language models (LLMs), yet existing benchmarks often rely solely on final-answer accuracy, failing to capture the quality and structure of the reasoning process. We propose FineLogic, a fine-grained evaluation framework that assesses logical reasoning across three dimensions: overall benchmark accuracy, stepwise soundness, and representation-level alignment. In addition, to better understand how reasoning capabilities emerge, we conduct a comprehensive study on the effects of supervision format during fine-tuning. We construct four supervision styles (one natural language and three symbolic variants) and train LLMs under each. Our findings reveal that natural language supervision yields strong generalization even on out-of-distribution and long-context tasks, while symbolic reasoning styles promote more structurally sound and atomic inference chains. Further, our representation-level probing shows that fine-tuning primarily improves reasoning behaviors through step-by-step generation, rather than enhancing shortcut prediction or internalized correctness. Together, our framework and analysis provide a more rigorous and interpretable lens for evaluating and improving logical reasoning in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FineLogic, a fine-grained evaluation framework designed to dissect and assess logical reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs). It moves beyond simple final-answer accuracy by incorporating stepwise soundness (validity, relevance, atomicity of each reasoning step) and representation-level probing (examining how well LLMs internally align reasoning structures with their hidden state).  The authors also perform a comprehensive study comparing different supervision formats (natural language vs. symbolic logic) during fine-tuning (SFT). They found that natural language supervision leads to better generalization (even to out-of-distribution and long-context tasks), while symbolic reasoning promotes more structurally sound and atomic inference chains. Representation-level probing indicates that fine-tuning primarily improves the stepwise generation process rather than shortcut prediction or internalized correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Evaluation Framework (FineLogic):** The core contribution is the introduction of FineLogic. This framework is a significant step up from existing binary accuracy evaluations for logical reasoning. The decomposition into stepwise soundness and representational probing adds considerably more depth and insight.
    *   **Comprehensive Study of Supervision Formats:**  The systematic comparison of different supervision strategies (natural language vs. symbolic) is valuable. The findings that different supervision styles lead to different strengths and weaknesses in LLM reasoning abilities are insightful and potentially actionable.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology. The datasets, experimental setup, and analysis appear to be carefully considered and executed. The use of GPT-4.1-mini for assessing step validity and atomicity helps to enhance the rigor of the stepwise soundness evaluation.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper effectively answers two research questions: How to rigorously evaluate LLMs' step-by-step correctness in logical reasoning tasks, beyond the binary evaluation of the final answer? and What style of training data, natural language or formal logical symbols, better facilitates the learning of multi-step logical reasoning through SFT?

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While FineLogic is modular, the study relies on a finite set of datasets for evaluation. This might limit the generalizability of the findings and suggests a need for evaluation across more diverse benchmark datasets.
    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Data for SFT:**  The supervised fine-tuning primarily uses synthetic datasets (FLD and ProntoQA). This could create a bias towards problems formatted in a way similar to the training data. This could potentially limit general reasoning ability on more diverse problem set that are not similar to the training data.
    *   **Lack of Integration Strategies:** While the paper analyzes different supervision styles, it doesn't explore systematic integration of these into more comprehensive SFT datasets. The authors acknowledge this as a limitation, and future work should investigate hybrid approaches to address the trade-offs identified.
    *   **Model Scale:** It would have been valuable to extend the representation-level probing beyond the LLaMA and Qwen families, to assess if the findings hold at larger model scales.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The fine-grained evaluation framework is a valuable contribution that directly addresses the limitations of existing binary accuracy assessments in logical reasoning. The analysis of different supervision formats has practical implications for improving LLM training. However, the limitations of the data may pose a challenge for direct applications in the field.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The FineLogic framework can potentially become a standard for more detailed analysis of reasoning in LLMs. The insights into supervision strategies should influence future research on SFT for reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by introducing a novel and rigorous evaluation framework and providing valuable insights into the effects of supervision format on LLM reasoning. The weaknesses (dataset limitations, exclusive use of synthetic data for SFT, and lack of integration strategies) prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the insights from this work should influence SFT strategies for LLMs, more work must be done before it is widely applicable. It is important to acknowledge that more LLM datasets should be added to test against. Therefore, a score of 7 is assigned.

### Design of intelligent proofreading system for English translation based on CNN and BERT
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04811v1)
- **Authors**: Feijun Liu, Huifeng Wang, Kun Wang, Yizhen Wang
- **Abstract**: Since automatic translations can contain errors that require substantial human post-editing, machine translation proofreading is essential for improving quality. This paper proposes a novel hybrid approach for robust proofreading that combines convolutional neural networks (CNN) with Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT). In order to extract semantic information from phrases and expressions, CNN uses a variety of convolution kernel filters to capture local n-gram patterns. In the meanwhile, BERT creates context-rich representations of whole sequences by utilizing stacked bidirectional transformer encoders. Using BERT's attention processes, the integrated error detection component relates tokens to spot translation irregularities including word order problems and omissions. The correction module then uses parallel English-German alignment and GRU decoder models in conjunction with translation memory to propose logical modifications that maintain original meaning. A unified end-to-end training process optimized for post-editing performance is applied to the whole pipeline. The multi-domain collection of WMT and the conversational dialogues of Open-Subtitles are two of the English-German parallel corpora used to train the model. Multiple loss functions supervise detection and correction capabilities. Experiments attain a 90% accuracy, 89.37% F1, and 16.24% MSE, exceeding recent proofreading techniques by over 10% overall. Comparative benchmarking demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in identifying and coherently rectifying mistranslations and omissions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper "Design of intelligent proofreading system for English translation based on CNN and BERT."

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel hybrid approach combining Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) and Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT) for English translation proofreading. The CNN extracts local n-gram patterns, while BERT captures context-rich representations of whole sequences. An integrated error detection component uses BERT's attention to identify translation irregularities (word order, omissions). The correction module then uses parallel English-German alignment and GRU decoders with translation memory to suggest meaning-preserving modifications. The model is trained end-to-end on WMT and OpenSubtitles corpora. Experiments show improvements in accuracy, F1 score, and MSE compared to existing proofreading techniques. The authors claim that this synergistic CNN-BERT approach significantly enhances translator outputs, reduces post-editing needs, and has implications for seamless cross-lingual communication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The hybrid CNN-BERT architecture is a sensible approach. Combining local and global context is likely beneficial for proofreading.
    *   The use of readily available datasets like WMT and OpenSubtitles makes the research reproducible.
    *   The end-to-end training approach and the integrated error detection and correction modules are well-designed.
    *   The reported results are promising and suggest improvement over existing methods based on the chosen metrics.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the *combination* of CNN and BERT for *proofreading* might be considered novel, both technologies are independently well-established. The originality lies in the specific architecture and training regime for this *particular task*, but the fundamental building blocks are not innovative.
    *   **Insufficient Depth of Analysis:** The paper lacks a deeper analysis of *why* the proposed approach works. It provides empirical results, but there isn't much investigation into which component (CNN or BERT) contributes most, or how the interaction between them leads to the observed improvements. Error analysis, beyond overall metrics, is superficial.
    *   **Limited Scope:** Focusing solely on English-German translation limits the generalizability of the findings. The architecture may not translate (pun intended) well to other language pairs with different linguistic properties.
    *   **Missing Baseline Comparison Details:**  The paper mentions exceeding "recent proofreading techniques by over 10% overall."  The specifications of those techniques is not as extensive as the current one. Additionally, the specific implementations and training regimes of the baseline techniques are not sufficiently described. The absence of a clear and equitable benchmark is a point of criticism.
    *   **Vague Future Work:**  The discussion of future work is general and lacks concrete details. Simply stating "multilingual expansion" is not a contribution.
    *   **Poor Presentation and Writing:** There are numerous grammatical errors and formatting issues throughout the paper, undermining its credibility. The frequent bolding of text segments for emphasis is not something found in most journal articles or conference papers. The references are also not formatted as a single style. This all reduces the credibility of the paper.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The research has the potential to improve the quality and efficiency of machine translation workflows. By automating proofreading, it can reduce the burden on human translators and potentially make machine translation more widely adopted.
    *   The proposed hybrid architecture demonstrates the benefits of combining different neural network architectures for specific NLP tasks.
    *   It contributes to the growing body of research on using transformer models (like BERT) for downstream applications.
* **Reason for Assigned Score:** The paper presents a reasonable approach with promising experimental results. However, it suffers from a lack of deep analysis, limited novelty, narrow scope, and inadequate justification of the significance. While potentially valuable to practitioners in the field, its theoretical contributions are weak and the presentation is subpar.  The core concepts are well-known and the empirical results, while positive, lack thorough investigation. Overall the novelty of the topic is questionable, with lack of specific definitions about which baseline techniques are being evaluated.
