### LogicPuzzleRL: Cultivating Robust Mathematical Reasoning in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04821v1)
- **Authors**: Zhen Hao Wong, Jingwen Deng, Runming He, Zirong Chen, Qijie You, Hejun Dong, Hao Liang, Chengyu Shen, Bin Cui, Wentao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at many supervised tasks but often struggle with structured reasoning in unfamiliar settings. This discrepancy suggests that standard fine-tuning pipelines may instill narrow, domain-specific heuristics rather than fostering general-purpose thinking strategies. In this work, we propose a "play to learn" framework that fine-tunes LLMs through reinforcement learning on a suite of seven custom logic puzzles, each designed to cultivate distinct reasoning skills such as constraint propagation, spatial consistency, and symbolic deduction. Using a reinforcement learning setup with verifiable rewards, models receive binary feedback based on puzzle correctness, encouraging iterative, hypothesis-driven problem solving. We demonstrate that this training approach significantly improves out-of-distribution performance on a range of mathematical benchmarks, especially for mid-difficulty problems that require multi-step reasoning. Analyses across problem categories and difficulty levels reveal that puzzle training promotes transferable reasoning routines, strengthening algebraic manipulation, geometric inference, and combinatorial logic, while offering limited gains on rote or highly specialized tasks. These findings show that reinforcement learning over logic puzzles reshapes the internal reasoning of LLMs, enabling more robust and compositional generalization without relying on task-specific symbolic tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "LogicPuzzleRL," a framework that uses reinforcement learning (RL) on a set of seven custom logic puzzles to improve the mathematical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is that these puzzles, designed to cultivate specific reasoning skills (constraint propagation, spatial consistency, etc.), encourage LLMs to develop generalizable problem-solving strategies through iterative hypothesis generation and verifiable rewards.  The authors demonstrate that training on these puzzles improves LLM performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) mathematical benchmarks, particularly on mid-difficulty problems requiring multi-step reasoning. They analyze the models' reasoning processes, concluding that the gains result from enhanced reasoning routines rather than the memorization of domain-specific heuristics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of using RL with logic puzzles to improve LLM reasoning has some novelty, but the individual components have been explored before. Game-based RL and math-based RL are established fields. The novelty lies in *combining* these elements to explicitly target the *internal reasoning mechanisms* of LLMs rather than solely focusing on achieving high scores on benchmarks. However, the construction of specific puzzles tailored to specific reasoning skills is a contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the *potential* to address a key limitation of LLMs: their brittleness in unfamiliar settings. The work *suggests* that we can move beyond solely relying on supervised fine-tuning and pretraining datasets and explore methods for teaching LLMs to "think" more effectively. The empirical results, while showing improvements on some benchmarks, are not overwhelmingly conclusive, but they are promising. The analysis of reasoning traces is a strength, providing insights into *how* the puzzle training changes the model's internal workings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined framework (LogicPuzzleRL).
    *   Carefully designed set of logic puzzles with verifiable rewards.
    *   Emphasis on fostering generalizable reasoning skills rather than task-specific heuristics.
    *   Analysis of reasoning traces provides qualitative insights.
    *   Demonstrated improvements on OOD mathematical benchmarks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvements, while present, are not uniformly large across all benchmarks and difficulty levels.
    *   Some results are statistically insignificant.
    *   The conclusions regarding the qualitative changes in reasoning mechanisms are based on interpretations of reasoning traces, which can be subjective.
    *   The "play-to-learn" paradigm, while a good metaphor, isn't fundamentally new.
    *   The benefits depend on carefully constructed puzzles and reward systems, requiring significant effort in design and implementation.
    * The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of the computational resources required for RL fine-tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in two ways: 1) It encourages the development of more targeted and structured training methods for LLMs, moving beyond massive datasets towards curricula that focus on specific cognitive skills. 2) It provides a framework for analyzing the internal reasoning processes of LLMs, which could lead to a better understanding of how these models learn and reason.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents an interesting approach to improve LLM reasoning, with some evidence suggesting that targeted training through logic puzzles enhances generalizable problem-solving skills. However, the empirical gains are not overwhelming, and the conclusions about the qualitative changes in reasoning mechanisms rely on interpretation. While there is a clear and well-defined framework with carefully designed puzzles and the use of verifiable rewards is a strength, the paper could be strengthened with additional quantitative analyses and a larger-scale study with more definitive findings.

### Evaluating Vision-Language and Large Language Models for Automated Student Assessment in Indonesian Classrooms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04822v1)
- **Authors**: Nurul Aisyah, Muhammad Dehan Al Kautsar, Arif Hidayat, Raqib Chowdhury, Fajri Koto
- **Abstract**: Although vision-language and large language models (VLM and LLM) offer promising opportunities for AI-driven educational assessment, their effectiveness in real-world classroom settings, particularly in underrepresented educational contexts, remains underexplored. In this study, we evaluated the performance of a state-of-the-art VLM and several LLMs on 646 handwritten exam responses from grade 4 students in six Indonesian schools, covering two subjects: Mathematics and English. These sheets contain more than 14K student answers that span multiple choice, short answer, and essay questions. Assessment tasks include grading these responses and generating personalized feedback. Our findings show that the VLM often struggles to accurately recognize student handwriting, leading to error propagation in downstream LLM grading. Nevertheless, LLM-generated feedback retains some utility, even when derived from imperfect input, although limitations in personalization and contextual relevance persist.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of vision-language models (VLMs) and large language models (LLMs) for automated student assessment in Indonesian classrooms, focusing specifically on grade 4 mathematics and English subjects. The authors created a dataset of handwritten exam responses from both rural and urban schools in Indonesia. They evaluated the performance of GPT-4o, Llama-3.1-Instruct (70B), Qwen2.5-Instruct (72B), and Deepseek-Chat (671B) in grading the responses and generating personalized feedback. They found that GPT-4o with vision achieved the best accuracy and feedback quality, but performance was affected by OCR accuracy and geographical location. Specifically, the authors highlight challenges related to noisy input (handwriting recognition), multilingual content, and the limitations imposed by low-tech environments. The study also highlights the importance of contextual relevance and adaptation for non-English speaking and under-resourced settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-world Data and Context:** The paper's primary strength lies in its focus on a real-world application within an under-resourced context (Indonesian primary schools). This is a significant departure from much of the existing research that centers on English-speaking, high-resource settings. This provides valuable insights that are often overlooked.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The creation and release of a handwritten student answer dataset is a valuable contribution. Such datasets are crucial for advancing research on educational applications of AI in diverse contexts. This dataset fills an important gap.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation, comparing multiple state-of-the-art models across various question types (multiple choice, short answer, essay). The evaluation includes both quantitative (MAE) and qualitative (human evaluation of feedback) metrics.
    *   **Multimodal Pipeline:** The integration of VLM for handwriting recognition and LLM for grading and feedback is a practical approach for addressing the issue of a lack of resources and digital tools.
    *   **Analysis of the Role of OCR:** The analysis of how errors in the OCR stage propagate through to the downstream LLM process is also an important contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Generalization:** While the focus on Indonesian classrooms is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings. The specific challenges related to handwriting styles, local language nuances, and educational curricula mean that the results may not directly translate to other regions or educational systems.
    *   **Dataset Size Disparity:** The dataset size discrepancy between urban and rural schools introduces a potential bias. While the authors acknowledge this and offer an explanation, it's still a factor that could influence the results.
    *   **Moderate Improvement Over Human:** The accuracy improvements aren't overwhelmingly significant which can lead to questions around the cost of introducing AI into this process.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper highlights steps to protect student privacy, but more discussion of the broader ethical implications of using AI in education (particularly in resource-constrained environments) would be beneficial. Consider also issues around bias amplification, data security, and the potential for deskilling of educators.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper is novel in its focus on automated student assessment in an Indonesian context, using real-world handwritten data. The study addresses a critical gap in research by exploring the challenges and opportunities of applying AI in under-resourced educational settings, where these tools could have the most significant impact.
    *   The dataset created will certainly contribute to further research within educational evaluation.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    While the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by focusing on a previously underexplored context and providing a novel dataset, its limited scope of generalizability and modest accuracy improvements mean it doesn't represent a transformative breakthrough. The work clearly advances understanding of deploying VLM and LLM techniques in education, specifically in low-resource, multi-lingual settings, but it also identifies key challenges for future research and highlights the importance of careful contextual adaptation and a focus on equitable access to AI-powered educational tools. More effort should be considered for ethical considerations.

### DualX-VSR: Dual Axial Spatial$\times$Temporal Transformer for Real-World Video Super-Resolution without Motion Compensation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04830v1)
- **Authors**: Shuo Cao, Yihao Liu, Xiaohui Li. Yuanting Gao. Yu Zhou, Chao Dong
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based models like ViViT and TimeSformer have advanced video understanding by effectively modeling spatiotemporal dependencies. Recent video generation models, such as Sora and Vidu, further highlight the power of transformers in long-range feature extraction and holistic spatiotemporal modeling. However, directly applying these models to real-world video super-resolution (VSR) is challenging, as VSR demands pixel-level precision, which can be compromised by tokenization and sequential attention mechanisms. While recent transformer-based VSR models attempt to address these issues using smaller patches and local attention, they still face limitations such as restricted receptive fields and dependence on optical flow-based alignment, which can introduce inaccuracies in real-world settings. To overcome these issues, we propose Dual Axial Spatial$\times$Temporal Transformer for Real-World Video Super-Resolution (DualX-VSR), which introduces a novel dual axial spatial$\times$temporal attention mechanism that integrates spatial and temporal information along orthogonal directions. DualX-VSR eliminates the need for motion compensation, offering a simplified structure that provides a cohesive representation of spatiotemporal information. As a result, DualX-VSR achieves high fidelity and superior performance in real-world VSR task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DualX-VSR, a novel video super-resolution (VSR) approach using a dual axial spatial×temporal transformer.  The key innovation lies in its dual axial attention mechanism that integrates spatial and temporal information along orthogonal directions (vertical-temporal and horizontal-temporal), eliminating the need for explicit motion compensation. The authors argue that this simplifies the architecture and provides a more cohesive representation of spatiotemporal information, leading to improved performance and fidelity in real-world VSR tasks compared to existing transformer-based and CNN-based methods. They present quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating DualX-VSR's superiority on various datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The dual axial spatial×temporal attention mechanism is the core novelty. Existing video transformers often process spatial and temporal information sequentially, or rely on complex alignment mechanisms using optical flow. DualX-VSR's approach of projecting spatiotemporal information along orthogonal axes for integrated modeling is a conceptually simple yet potentially effective way to overcome these limitations. It cleverly leverages separate attention modules to capture horizontal and vertical motion patterns, which the authors empirically show differ in amplitude. The shift from using optical flow for alignment to direct spatiotemporal attention is a significant architectural change, especially for real-world VSR where optical flow estimation can be unreliable.

*   **Significance:** Video super-resolution is a critical task with applications in various domains. Real-world VSR, in particular, is challenging due to the complexity of degradations. If DualX-VSR consistently outperforms existing methods, it would be a significant contribution. The paper highlights a trend towards utilizing transformer architecture for video super-resolution, but also the challenge of adapting pure transformer design for low-level task. DualX-VSR is an important attempt to address this challenge. The paper's claim about surpassing alignment-based methods in real-world scenarios is significant, as it challenges a long-standing reliance on motion estimation. However, this needs to be carefully validated across diverse and realistic datasets.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and motivation, providing a good background of existing VSR methods and highlighting their limitations.
    *   A well-defined and explained architecture.
    *   Empirical evaluation on multiple datasets, including both synthetic and real-world scenarios. The use of both quantitative and qualitative results strengthens the evaluation.
    *   The design choices are well-justified by a series of ablation studies.
    *   The approach retains the simplicity of ViViT's architecture.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the concept of modeling horizontal and vertical motion independently is insightful, the justification might not fully capture all the complexities of real-world video content.
    *   The improvements reported might be specific to the datasets used, and the generalization ability to more diverse and complex real-world scenarios needs further investigation.
    *   The reliance on a ViViT-based architecture limits the exploration of other potential transformer backbones, and the impact of the dual axial attention mechanism on different transformer architectures should be further studied.
    *   The ablation studies are fairly comprehensive, more results and discussion of temporal consistancy would be very helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dual axial attention mechanism could inspire further research in video processing tasks beyond super-resolution, specifically for tasks where motion and spatial information are crucial. By decoupling the reliance on explicit motion estimation, the approach can be more robust to noisy or degraded video inputs.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

Despite its strengths, the paper also has certain limitations. Although it presents an innovative approach to spatial and temporal data fusion, the results do not overwhelmingly outperform the state-of-the-art (SOTA) in every single benchmark. The complexity of the approach might hinder its immediate adoption if the improvements are marginal compared to significantly simpler alignment-based methods. The absence of a detailed complexity study against a broader range of SOTA limits a rigorous judgement of advantages of this approach.

*Score: 7*

*Justification:* While the paper presents a novel and potentially impactful method with strong experimental support, the margin of improvement compared to existing state-of-the-art methods may not be substantial enough to immediately revolutionize the field. It is solid contribution to the current literature, and can spark interest and further improvements in video super-resolution techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Joint Evaluation of Answer and Reasoning Consistency for Hallucination Detection in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04832v1)
- **Authors**: Changyue Wang, Weihang Su, Qingyao Ai, Yiqun Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) extend large language models with explicit, multi-step reasoning traces to enhance transparency and performance on complex tasks. However, these reasoning traces can be redundant or logically inconsistent, making them a new source of hallucination that is difficult to detect. Existing hallucination detection methods focus primarily on answer-level uncertainty and often fail to detect hallucinations or logical inconsistencies arising from the model's reasoning trace. This oversight is particularly problematic for LRMs, where the explicit thinking trace is not only an important support to the model's decision-making process but also a key source of potential hallucination. To this end, we propose RACE (Reasoning and Answer Consistency Evaluation), a novel framework specifically tailored for hallucination detection in LRMs. RACE operates by extracting essential reasoning steps and computing four diagnostic signals: inter-sample consistency of reasoning traces, entropy-based answer uncertainty, semantic alignment between reasoning and answers, and internal coherence of reasoning. This joint analysis enables fine-grained hallucination detection even when the final answer appears correct. Experiments across datasets and different LLMs demonstrate that RACE outperforms existing hallucination detection baselines, offering a robust and generalizable solution for evaluating LRMs. Our code is available at: https://github.com/bebr2/RACE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RACE (Reasoning and Answer Consistency Evaluation), a novel framework for detecting hallucinations in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs).  RACE goes beyond simply checking answer-level consistency, which is common in hallucination detection, and also incorporates the consistency, coherence, and alignment of the reasoning traces produced by LRMs. RACE breaks down hallucination detection into four components: reasoning path consistency (across multiple generations), answer uncertainty (using a refined semantic entropy), reasoning-answer alignment (measuring if the reasoning supports the answer), and reasoning internal coherence (assessing speculative content). A CoT Extraction module is used to distill key reasoning steps. The authors demonstrate that RACE outperforms existing hallucination detection methods on several datasets and LRMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The primary novelty of the paper lies in its explicit focus on the reasoning traces of LRMs for hallucination detection.  While answer-level consistency checks have been extensively explored, explicitly evaluating the reasoning process is less common, particularly in a black-box setting. The decomposition of hallucination detection into four components and the CoT Extraction module provide a structured approach to tackling this problem. It addresses a clear limitation in existing methods, which often overlook inconsistencies within the reasoning process itself.  The information-theoretic motivation for combining reasoning consistency, answer uncertainty, and reasoning-answer alignment is sound and helps to justify the structure of the framework.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the development and deployment of LRMs. As LRMs become more prevalent, ensuring the reliability and trustworthiness of their reasoning processes is crucial.  Hallucinations arising from flawed reasoning, even when answers appear correct, can have serious consequences. The performance gains demonstrated by RACE over existing baselines on various datasets suggest its practical value.  The code release further enhances its potential impact by enabling other researchers and practitioners to adopt and extend the framework.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of reasoning-based hallucinations in LRMs.
*   **Comprehensive Framework:** RACE provides a comprehensive framework for addressing the problem.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate that RACE consistently outperforms existing baselines.
*   **Generalizability:** The experiments encompass both general-purpose LLMs and LRMs, and various output types.
*   **Black-box Approach:** The framework operates in a black-box setting, making it applicable to a wide range of models without requiring access to internal parameters.
*   **Information Theoretic Motivation**: The decomposition of the joint entropy provides a clear framework for thinking about the problem.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Linear Combination of Scores:**  The final score aggregation uses a simple linear combination with equal weights. While justified by its simplicity, exploring more sophisticated weighting schemes might improve performance. Some discussion about the optimality of equal weights would be useful.
*   **Reliance on NLI Classifier:** The reasoning consistency module relies on an NLI classifier. The accuracy of the NLI classifier will affect performance. There should be an analysis on how the performance of the NLI model affects the performance of RACE.
*   **CoT extraction introduces overhead**: As noted in the paper, CoT extraction is an additional overhead in this model. There is no direct comparison with the original LRM without CoT extraction.
*   **Dataset Limitations:** The experiments are conducted on question answering datasets. It would be beneficial to evaluate RACE on other tasks where LRMs are used, such as code generation or summarization, to assess its broader applicability.

**Potential Influence:**

RACE has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Shifting the focus of hallucination detection towards evaluating reasoning traces in addition to answers.
*   Providing a practical and effective framework for detecting reasoning-based hallucinations.
*   Inspiring further research into more sophisticated methods for analyzing and improving the reasoning processes of LRMs.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

RACE makes a substantial contribution by offering a novel framework tailored to the specific problem of hallucination in large reasoning models.  Its comprehensive design, incorporating reasoning consistency, answer uncertainty, reasoning-answer alignment, and internal coherence, represents a significant step forward from existing answer-level consistency methods. The experimental results strongly support the framework's effectiveness, demonstrating consistent outperformance across a range of models and datasets. While the choice of a linear combination for score aggregation and reliance on external models like the NLI classifier are potential limitations, they do not detract significantly from the paper's overall contribution. The explicit acknowledgement of runtime overhead is balanced by the accuracy gains, which is acceptable for applications where high reliability is paramount.

### On Automating Security Policies with Contemporary LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04838v1)
- **Authors**: Pablo Fernández Saura, K. R. Jayaram, Vatche Isahagian, Jorge Bernal Bernabé, Antonio Skarmeta
- **Abstract**: The complexity of modern computing environments and the growing sophistication of cyber threats necessitate a more robust, adaptive, and automated approach to security enforcement. In this paper, we present a framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) for automating attack mitigation policy compliance through an innovative combination of in-context learning and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We begin by describing how our system collects and manages both tool and API specifications, storing them in a vector database to enable efficient retrieval of relevant information. We then detail the architectural pipeline that first decomposes high-level mitigation policies into discrete tasks and subsequently translates each task into a set of actionable API calls. Our empirical evaluation, conducted using publicly available CTI policies in STIXv2 format and Windows API documentation, demonstrates significant improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score when employing RAG compared to a non-RAG baseline.
- **Summary**: Okay, I have analyzed the provided paper. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a framework for automating attack mitigation policy compliance using Large Language Models (LLMs). It leverages a combination of in-context learning and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to translate high-level security policies into executable API calls. The system collects and manages tool and API specifications in a vector database, decomposes policies into discrete tasks, and translates each task into actionable API calls. The authors conducted experiments using STIXv2 formatted CTI policies and Windows API documentation, demonstrating improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score when using RAG compared to a non-RAG baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Problem:** Addresses a significant challenge in cybersecurity: the difficulty in translating high-level security policies into concrete, executable actions, especially considering the complexity and scale of modern systems.
*   **Clear Architecture:** The dual-LLM pipeline architecture is well-defined. The decomposition of the problem into policy analysis/task decomposition and API generation stages is a logical and effective approach.
*   **RAG Integration:** The use of RAG is a smart move to improve the accuracy of API call generation. The vector database approach for storing and retrieving API documentation is a well-established and efficient technique. The focus on Windows APIs provides a degree of focus to the study, and is well-justified.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The experimental methodology is reasonably sound. The use of publicly available CTI policies and the comparison with a non-RAG baseline provide empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The choice of metrics (precision, recall, F1-score) is appropriate.
*   **Clear Results:** The results demonstrating the improvements achieved by RAG are clearly presented and discussed.
*   **Implications Discussed:** The paper discusses the implications of the approach for dependable security, highlighting benefits such as improved consistency, responsiveness to new threats, and scalability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to Windows APIs and a specific set of attack mitigation policies. While this targeted approach allows for a detailed analysis, it raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other platforms, operating systems, and types of security policies. This is acknowledged by the authors.
*   **Ground Truth Creation:** The manual creation of the ground truth API calls is a potential source of bias and error. It's unclear how rigorously this process was validated and whether inter-rater reliability was assessed. The complexity involved in this process likely limits the experiments.
*   **LLM Choice Dependence:** The performance of the system is heavily dependent on the chosen LLMs. The paper does a fair job of testing multiple LLMs, but it's important to acknowledge that future improvements in LLM capabilities could significantly impact the results.
*   **Novelty in Implementation:** The *idea* of using LLMs for automation is not entirely novel in cybersecurity, and the use of RAG is becoming increasingly common. The novelty largely lies in the specific architecture and application to attack mitigation policies, but this is arguably an incremental contribution on existing research.
*   **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** While the paper acknowledges the Windows-specific focus, it could provide a more in-depth discussion of other limitations, such as the challenges in handling complex conditional logic within policies or the potential for generating incorrect or harmful API calls due to LLM errors. The discussion of limitations is too brief.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes an incremental but worthwhile contribution. While LLMs and RAG have been applied to various cybersecurity tasks, this paper offers a systematic and practical framework for automating attack mitigation policy enforcement. The combination of a dual-LLM pipeline with RAG, along with the detailed empirical evaluation, provides valuable insights into the potential of this approach. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the reliance on manual ground truth creation limit the broader impact of the work.

**Score:**

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a practical solution to a real-world cybersecurity problem, with a well-defined architecture and empirical evidence supporting its effectiveness. The use of RAG improves the performance of LLMs, the experiments are well constructed. But, it's incremental work, and several significant gaps remain. The Windows-specific focus limits generalizability. The need for manual creation of ground truth is a bottleneck. It is a well-engineered approach that addresses a critical area, making a useful contribution that builds on existing work. Future work should address the generalizability concerns and explore ways to automate the ground truth creation process to enable more extensive evaluations.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multiple-Choice Question Generation Using Large Language Models: Methodology and Educator Insights
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04851v1)
- **Authors**: Giorgio Biancini, Alessio Ferrato, Carla Limongelli
- **Abstract**: Integrating Artificial Intelligence (AI) in educational settings has brought new learning approaches, transforming the practices of both students and educators. Among the various technologies driving this transformation, Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for creating educational materials and question answering, but there are still space for new applications. Educators commonly use Multiple-Choice Questions (MCQs) to assess student knowledge, but manually generating these questions is resource-intensive and requires significant time and cognitive effort. In our opinion, LLMs offer a promising solution to these challenges. This paper presents a novel comparative analysis of three widely known LLMs - Llama 2, Mistral, and GPT-3.5 - to explore their potential for creating informative and challenging MCQs. In our approach, we do not rely on the knowledge of the LLM, but we inject the knowledge into the prompt to contrast the hallucinations, giving the educators control over the test's source text, too. Our experiment involving 21 educators shows that GPT-3.5 generates the most effective MCQs across several known metrics. Additionally, it shows that there is still some reluctance to adopt AI in the educational field. This study sheds light on the potential of LLMs to generate MCQs and improve the educational experience, providing valuable insights for the future.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating Multiple Choice Questions (MCQs). It compares the performance of Llama 2, Mistral, and GPT-3.5 in creating MCQs based on a given text passage.  The study emphasizes a prompt-engineering approach that injects the knowledge directly from a provided text into the prompt, giving educators control over the test content and reducing hallucinations. An experiment involving 21 educators evaluated the generated MCQs using metrics like compliance, clarity, distractor quality, answer soundness, and learning utility.  The results suggest that GPT-3.5 generally produces the most effective MCQs, and highlight both opportunities and potential reluctance from educators in adopting AI-driven question generation tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is moderate.  While LLMs have been used for question answering, this study focuses specifically on *generating* MCQs with careful prompt engineering, injecting information and comparing multiple LLMs in this scenario. The approach of using the LLM as a tool and injecting the information to test the abilities to generate the right MCQ can be seen as original. However, the field of LLM application is rapidly evolving, and the core idea of generating questions is not entirely new. This distinguishes it from some previous work that merely uses the LLM as a source of knowledge.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its practical application and educator-centric approach.  MCQ generation is a time-consuming task for educators, and the paper explores a potential solution. The inclusion of a user study involving educators provides valuable insights into the perceived quality and usefulness of LLM-generated MCQs, as well as the barriers to adoption. This helps to ground the research in the real needs of educators. However, the study sample size of 21 is relatively small, which limits the generalizability of the results.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly describes its prompt engineering approach, the LLMs used, the evaluation metrics, and the experimental setup.
    *   **Educator Involvement:** The user study provides practical insights into educator perceptions.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:**  Comparing Llama 2, Mistral, and GPT-3.5 offers valuable comparative data.
    *   **Focus on Control:**  The deliberate design to rely on injected text provides a level of control to educators lacking in general knowledge based question generation
    *   The limitations are acknowledged with honesty.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** The study involved only 21 educators, which is a small sample size for drawing broad conclusions.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on one subject (Second World War) and a single prompt structure. While this allows for controlled comparison, it doesn't demonstrate generalizability across different topics and prompt variations.
    *   **Missing Baselines:** The paper lacks a direct comparison to existing human-generated MCQs or other automated MCQ generation methods. This makes it difficult to assess the true value added by the LLMs.
    *   **Lack of Demographic Data:** Without demographic data of the participants, it is difficult to understand the different behaviours in the answers.
    *   The paper doesn't explore the reasons for the poorer performance of Llama and Mistral in details, making it difficult to understand how to improve the prompt to make it suitable for these LLMs.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by encouraging further research into LLM-based MCQ generation, particularly with a focus on prompt engineering, educator feedback, and the development of user-friendly tools. It also highlights the need to address educator concerns and build trust in AI-driven educational solutions. It can also be used to understand the performance of LLMs given the constraints of a specific prompt for educational task.

**Score:** 6/10

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a moderately novel and significant contribution. The application of LLMs to MCQ generation is not entirely new, but the specific focus on prompt engineering, injecting text and educator feedback adds some value. The relatively small sample size, the focus on only one subject, lack of direct comparisons with other generation method and lack of demographic information limit the wider impact of the research. The study is valuable, well-executed, and provides valuable directions for future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Improving AI-generated music with user-guided training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04852v1)
- **Authors**: Vishwa Mohan Singh, Sai Anirudh Aryasomayajula, Ahan Chatterjee, Beste Aydemir, Rifat Mehreen Amin
- **Abstract**: AI music generation has advanced rapidly, with models like diffusion and autoregressive algorithms enabling high-fidelity outputs. These tools can alter styles, mix instruments, or isolate them. Since sound can be visualized as spectrograms, image-generation algorithms can be applied to generate novel music. However, these algorithms are typically trained on fixed datasets, which makes it challenging for them to interpret and respond to user input accurately. This is especially problematic because music is highly subjective and requires a level of personalization that image generation does not provide. In this work, we propose a human-computation approach to gradually improve the performance of these algorithms based on user interactions. The human-computation element involves aggregating and selecting user ratings to use as the loss function for fine-tuning the model. We employ a genetic algorithm that incorporates user feedback to enhance the baseline performance of a model initially trained on a fixed dataset. The effectiveness of this approach is measured by the average increase in user ratings with each iteration. In the pilot test, the first iteration showed an average rating increase of 0.2 compared to the baseline. The second iteration further improved upon this, achieving an additional increase of 0.39 over the first iteration.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Improving AI-generated music with user-guided training" explores a human-computation approach to refine AI music generation, specifically using diffusion models. The core idea is to iteratively improve the model by incorporating user feedback into the training process.  Instead of relying solely on fixed datasets and text prompts, the system uses songs provided by users as conditioning inputs for the diffusion model. Human ratings and listening times for generated music are then used to update the training dataset, favoring songs that users prefer. The system uses Spherical Linear Interpolation (SLERP) to merge multiple song encodings.  A pilot study showed that with each iteration of this feedback loop, the average user rating of generated songs increased, suggesting an improvement in the model's ability to produce appealing music. The authors discuss the architecture of their system, which includes a user interface, system logic, music generator, and database.  They also classify their system under the human computation framework, detailing aspects like motivation, quality control, and aggregation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating user feedback into training generative models isn't entirely new; there are existing approaches in other domains like image generation. However, the *specific* application to music generation using *diffusion models* and a *human-computation framework* with weighted losses derived from listening time *is novel*. The use of SLERP for audio encoding aggregation is also a valuable contribution. The integration of listening time into the loss function to optimize for preference is a good idea. What is missing is a comparison with other techniques for personalization. It would also be interesting to know if users are aware if they are listening to the base model or an iteration of the human feedback-driven algorithm.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a crucial limitation of current AI music generation: the inability to personalize music based on individual tastes. The paper provides a practical approach to bridge this gap. While the pilot study is small, the positive results suggest that the approach has potential. Scaling the approach for more sophisticated merging of songs (multiple conditioning inputs) seems problematic as discussed by the authors. How to handle stylistic drift as user tastes change is also not addressed. A more comprehensive quantitative study with larger cohorts and more metrics to evaluate novelty, diversity, and overall quality is needed.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear articulation of the problem (lack of personalization in AI music generation).
    *   Well-defined system architecture and human computation framework.
    *   A practical approach that integrates user feedback into the training loop.
    *   Demonstration of the approach with a pilot study (although small).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Small pilot study with a limited number of users. The results need to be validated with a larger dataset and more rigorous statistical analysis. The metrics for measuring song quality are not well defined.
    *   Limited comparison with existing approaches to music personalization.
    *   The reliance on average ratings might be too simplistic. Individual differences in musical taste are not adequately addressed.
    *   The combination of multiple songs is problematic and abandoned in the last stage of the study.
    * The paper does not contain an ablation study or hyperparameter exploration
* **Potential Impact**:
    The ideas introduced have the potential to influence future research and development in AI music generation. Human preference should be an element in all such systems. As user expectations increase, this area will grow in importance.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and promising approach to personalizing AI music generation, the small-scale pilot study and lack of comparison with other personalization techniques limit its current impact. The problematic song mixing issue is also a concern. The lack of a formal ablation study and tuning experiments is also a factor.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Prompting LLMs: Length Control for Isometric Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04855v1)
- **Authors**: Dávid Javorský, Ondřej Bojar, François Yvon
- **Abstract**: In this study, we explore the effectiveness of isometric machine translation across multiple language pairs (En$\to$De, En$\to$Fr, and En$\to$Es) under the conditions of the IWSLT Isometric Shared Task 2022. Using eight open-source large language models (LLMs) of varying sizes, we investigate how different prompting strategies, varying numbers of few-shot examples, and demonstration selection influence translation quality and length control. We discover that the phrasing of instructions, when aligned with the properties of the provided demonstrations, plays a crucial role in controlling the output length. Our experiments show that LLMs tend to produce shorter translations only when presented with extreme examples, while isometric demonstrations often lead to the models disregarding length constraints. While few-shot prompting generally enhances translation quality, further improvements are marginal across 5, 10, and 20-shot settings. Finally, considering multiple outputs allows to notably improve overall tradeoff between the length and quality, yielding state-of-the-art performance for some language pairs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores length control for isometric machine translation using prompting with large language models (LLMs). It investigates the effectiveness of different prompting strategies, the impact of few-shot examples, and the influence of demonstration selection across multiple language pairs (En->De, En->Fr, En->Es). The study focuses on achieving translations that adhere to strict length constraints, specifically ±10% of the source text's character count, as required in applications like dubbing. The paper evaluates eight open-source LLMs of varying sizes. The core finding is that the phrasing of instructions and their alignment with the properties of the provided demonstrations plays a crucial role in controlling output length. Presenting extreme examples of short translations helps produce shorter outputs, but isometric demonstrations can lead to models disregarding length constraints.  The paper also finds that generating multiple outputs can improve the trade-off between length compliance and translation quality, allowing for competitive performance with specialized isometric MT systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation of multiple prompting strategies, LLMs, and language pairs. The systematic comparison of different settings provides valuable insights into the behavior of LLMs for length-constrained MT.
*   **Focus on a Practical Problem:** Isometric MT is a niche but highly relevant problem for media applications. The paper addresses a practical need for accurate and concise translations that respect length constraints.
*   **Clear Findings:** The paper identifies the crucial role of aligning instructions and demonstrations, which is a key contribution to understanding how to effectively prompt LLMs for constrained translation. The finding that extreme examples are needed to encourage shorter translations is also notable.
*   **Reproducible Setup:** The paper uses open-source LLMs and provides details on the experimental setup, promoting reproducibility and further research in this area. The public release of data further helps in this regard.
*   **Use of Multiple Outputs for Improvement**: The authors provide insightful result that generating multiple outputs allows for a better trade-off between length and quality, which is a cost effective approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Prompting Strategies:** While the study investigates various prompting approaches, the specific techniques used are relatively standard. The prompts themselves are quite simple.  The novelty primarily lies in applying these strategies to the specific task of isometric MT and analyzing their impact.
*   **Incremental Improvements:** While the paper demonstrates that LLMs can achieve reasonable performance on isometric MT, the improvements over baselines are not always substantial.  The best results are obtained by generating many different translations and selecting the best one, which is computationally expensive.
*   **Lack of Comparison with State-of-the-Art Isometric MT Systems:**  The paper compares against IWSLT 2022 submissions. This is good. The organizers of IWSLT22 are the authors as well. The organizers provided two baselines (weak and strong). The performance improvement is not impressive. Comparison against more sophisticated, recent isometric MT systems that use length tokens, auxiliary tasks, or fine-tuning would strengthen the analysis and provide a clearer picture of the competitiveness of LLMs in this task. The system might not have state-of-the-art performance in isometric machine translation but the goal of the paper is to use LLMs and the task is simply employed as a tool to explore LLMs limitations.
*   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed error analysis of the generated translations.  Understanding the types of errors made by LLMs, and how these errors relate to length constraints, would provide valuable insights.
*   **Computational Cost:** The strategy of generating multiple outputs, although effective, could lead to the high computational costs. However, authors highlighted as it would not be necessary for every sample in practice and the cost can be amortized.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on using LLMs for machine translation, particularly in constrained settings. The insights into prompting strategies and demonstration selection provide valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners working on length-controlled MT and other constrained MT tasks.  The focus on isometric MT addresses a practical need in media applications. The work could also inspire further research on leveraging LLMs for synthetic data generation to train more specialized MT models. Although, the novelty could be higher the paper is well-structured and has good results. The paper will serve as a solid guide for prompting LLMs.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper provides a thorough and well-executed study on length control for isometric MT using LLMs. The insights into instruction-demonstration alignment and the effectiveness of extreme examples are valuable contributions. The paper could be significantly enhanced by more innovative prompting techniques, comparisons to state-of-the-art isometric MT systems and more detailed error analysis. The paper is certainly very useful but I can't give it a high score because the novelty and significance is not exceptional.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Sparse Autoencoders, Again?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04859v1)
- **Authors**: Yin Lu, Tong He, Xuening Zhu, David Wipf
- **Abstract**: Is there really much more to say about sparse autoencoders (SAEs)? Autoencoders in general, and SAEs in particular, represent deep architectures that are capable of modeling low-dimensional latent structure in data. Such structure could reflect, among other things, correlation patterns in large language model activations, or complex natural image manifolds. And yet despite the wide-ranging applicability, there have been relatively few changes to SAEs beyond the original recipe from decades ago, namely, standard deep encoder/decoder layers trained with a classical/deterministic sparse regularizer applied within the latent space. One possible exception is the variational autoencoder (VAE), which adopts a stochastic encoder module capable of producing sparse representations when applied to manifold data. In this work we formalize underappreciated weaknesses with both canonical SAEs, as well as analogous VAEs applied to similar tasks, and propose a hybrid alternative model that circumvents these prior limitations. In terms of theoretical support, we prove that global minima of our proposed model recover certain forms of structured data spread across a union of manifolds. Meanwhile, empirical evaluations on synthetic and real-world datasets substantiate the efficacy of our approach in accurately estimating underlying manifold dimensions and producing sparser latent representations without compromising reconstruction error. In general, we are able to exceed the performance of equivalent-capacity SAEs and VAEs, as well as recent diffusion models where applicable, within domains such as images and language model activation patterns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Sparse Autoencoders, Again?" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper revisits sparse autoencoders (SAEs) and argues that, despite their longevity and wide use, there's room for improvement beyond the standard recipe.  It identifies limitations in both traditional SAEs (hyperparameter sensitivity, potential non-convexity) and variational autoencoders (VAEs) when applied to sparse autoencoding tasks (tendency towards fixed sparsity patterns).  The authors propose a hybrid approach called VAEase, which modifies the VAE architecture by using the encoder variance to gate the latent representation before it enters the decoder. This allows for adaptive sparsity, similar to SAEs, while retaining VAE's benefits like hyperparameter-free training and a smoother loss landscape. The paper provides theoretical justifications (showing VAEase can recover manifold structure), and empirical results demonstrating improved performance in manifold dimension estimation and sparse representation learning compared to SAEs and VAEs, including in the context of large language model (LLM) activation patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using the encoder variance from a VAE as a gating mechanism for adaptive sparsity is, arguably, the most novel contribution. While conditional VAEs have explored similar concepts, the straightforward application within the standard VAE framework is new and surprisingly effective. The theoretical analysis, although focused on specific data distributions, provides a valuable foundation.
*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in addressing a practical problem: limitations of VAEs for sparse representation learning, especially in scenarios with complex manifold structure. By retaining the benefits of VAEs (simpler training, fewer hyperparameters) while enabling adaptive sparsity, VAEase offers a compelling alternative. The experiments are well-designed, and the results clearly demonstrate an advantage over existing methods, particularly in estimating manifold dimensions and in sparse representation learning of LLM activations. The demonstration regarding LLM activation data has direct applicability in an active area of research which contributes to its impact. The theoretical contribution also raises more important questions regarding the limitations of the original VAE structure and opens further room for investigation, which is highly valuable for the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem statement:**  The paper clearly articulates the shortcomings of existing SAE and VAE approaches.
    *   **Simple and elegant solution:** VAEase is a relatively minor modification with significant performance gains.
    *   **Theoretical support:**  The theoretical analysis, albeit in simplified settings, provides a solid foundation for the method.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The experiments are comprehensive and cover a range of datasets, demonstrating the efficacy of VAEase.
    *   **Relevant applications:** The demonstration on LLM activation data adds to the significance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited theoretical scope:** The theoretical analysis focuses on specific data distributions (union of manifolds) and linear decoders in some cases, restricting the generality of the results. The theorems also rely on asymptotic behaviour, which is not fully representative of realistic training scenarios.
    *   **Incremental improvement:**  While effective, VAEase can be viewed as an incremental improvement rather than a radical departure from existing methods.
    *   **Limited discussion on limitations:**  The paper has relatively little discussion on limitations of the proposed methods beyond what it improves, such as its applicability to different model types. This creates an impression that it is better than both SAE and VAE in every area, which is likely untrue.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in sparse representation learning and autoencoders. VAEase is easy to implement and use, which could lead to its adoption in various applications. The findings regarding the limitations of VAEs for sparse encoding could also spur further research into addressing these limitations.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 7 is justified due to the well-articulated problem, the straightforward and effective solution (VAEase), supportive theoretical arguments, and compelling empirical evaluations. However, the somewhat incremental nature of the contribution, along with the limited theoretical scope and a relative lack of discussion regarding the method's own limitations, prevents a higher score. The work provides a solid improvement and insight in the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMs for sensory-motor control: Combining in-context and iterative learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04867v1)
- **Authors**: Jônata Tyska Carvalho, Stefano Nolfi
- **Abstract**: We propose a method that enables large language models (LLMs) to control embodied agents by directly mapping continuous observation vectors to continuous action vectors. Initially, the LLMs generate a control strategy based on a textual description of the agent, its environment, and the intended goal. This strategy is then iteratively refined through a learning process in which the LLMs are repeatedly prompted to improve the current strategy, using performance feedback and sensory-motor data collected during its evaluation. The method is validated on classic control tasks from the Gymnasium library and the inverted pendulum task from the MuJoCo library. In most cases, it successfully identifies optimal or high-performing solutions by integrating symbolic knowledge derived through reasoning with sub-symbolic sensory-motor data gathered as the agent interacts with its environment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for sensory-motor control of embodied agents. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on predefined motor primitives, this method allows LLMs to directly map continuous observation vectors to continuous action vectors, eliminating the need for pre-programmed actions. The LLM is initially prompted with a textual description of the agent, environment, and task, and then iteratively refined through interaction with the environment. This refinement process involves prompting the LLM to improve its control strategy based on performance feedback and sensory-motor data collected during evaluation episodes. The approach is evaluated on classic control tasks from the Gymnasium library and the inverted pendulum task from MuJoCo. The results show that the method can successfully identify optimal or high-performing solutions by integrating symbolic knowledge with sub-symbolic sensory-motor data gathered through experience.  Critically, the method does not depend on human demonstrations, relying solely on autonomous experience.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the **combination** of several existing ideas into a cohesive and autonomous learning framework for LLM-based control. While individual components, such as in-context learning and iterative refinement with LLMs, have been explored previously, the integrated architecture and its application to continuous control tasks with direct action vector generation are relatively novel.  The elimination of reliance on human demonstrations in the learning process also represents a significant step towards autonomous skill acquisition.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to simplify and automate the development of control strategies for embodied agents. By leveraging the reasoning capabilities of LLMs and combining them with a trial-and-error learning process, the method offers a promising alternative to traditional reinforcement learning approaches, which often require extensive training and can be difficult to tune. If successfully scaled, this approach could democratize the development of robotic and virtual agents capable of performing complex tasks. The ability to integrate symbolic knowledge (through the prompts) and sub-symbolic sensory-motor data is a significant advantage.

**Strengths:**

*   **Autonomous Learning:** The method's independence from human demonstrations is a significant advantage, allowing agents to learn skills through self-exploration and experience.
*   **Integration of Symbolic and Sub-symbolic Knowledge:** The method successfully combines the reasoning capabilities of LLMs with sensory-motor data to derive effective control strategies.
*   **Structured Prompting:** The step-by-step prompting strategy promotes structured reasoning and helps the LLM generate effective control policies.
*   **Validation on Benchmark Tasks:** The evaluation on classic control tasks provides a solid foundation for comparison with existing methods.
*   **Comprehensive reporting**: The authors have provided ample data (code, logs, videos) for other researchers to verify their results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Sensory-Motor Data:** The sub-sampling of sensory-motor data is a significant limitation, potentially hindering the LLM's ability to identify critical failure points in the control strategy. Only 20 past steps of the episode are sent for each new prompting, limiting the view the model has of the problem it is trying to solve.
*   **Reliance on Hand-Crafted Prompts:** The performance of the method is likely to be sensitive to the design of the initial prompts. Automating the prompt engineering process would make the approach more robust and accessible. While the step-by-step prompting approach helps, this remains an area for potential improvement.
*   **Limited Scale:** While the performance on the CartPole tasks is impressive, more complex and high-dimensional control tasks need to be explored to determine the scalability and generalizability of the approach. Furthermore, tasks requiring longer term reasoning/planning/memory may be more suitable for demonstrating the benefits of using LLMs over other methods.
*   **Poor performance on Pendulum task:** The inability to achieve high performance on the Pendulum task highlights limitations in handling tasks without explicit failure termination conditions.
*   **Computational Cost:** Executing LLMs for real-time control can be computationally expensive, limiting the method's applicability to resource-constrained platforms.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of embodied AI by:

*   Inspiring further research on LLM-based control strategies for embodied agents.
*   Providing a framework for integrating symbolic and sub-symbolic knowledge in autonomous learning systems.
*   Motivating the development of more efficient LLM architectures for real-time control applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty (combination of existing ideas with a valuable autonomous element), significance (potential to simplify control development), strengths (autonomous learning, knowledge integration, structured prompting), and weaknesses (limited data, prompt dependence, limited scope), I would assign this paper a score of **7**. It represents a significant step in leveraging LLMs for embodied control and offers a promising alternative to existing methods. However, the identified weaknesses need to be addressed to realize the full potential of the approach and to demonstrate its broader applicability and scalability.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Invisible Backdoor Triggers in Image Editing Model via Deep Watermarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04879v1)
- **Authors**: Yu-Feng Chen, Tzuhsuan Huang, Pin-Yen Chiu, Jun-Cheng Chen
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable progress in both image generation and editing. However, recent studies have revealed their vulnerability to backdoor attacks, in which specific patterns embedded in the input can manipulate the model's behavior. Most existing research in this area has proposed attack frameworks focused on the image generation pipeline, leaving backdoor attacks in image editing relatively unexplored. Among the few studies targeting image editing, most utilize visible triggers, which are impractical because they introduce noticeable alterations to the input image before editing. In this paper, we propose a novel attack framework that embeds invisible triggers into the image editing process via poisoned training data. We leverage off-the-shelf deep watermarking models to encode imperceptible watermarks as backdoor triggers. Our goal is to make the model produce the predefined backdoor target when it receives watermarked inputs, while editing clean images normally according to the given prompt. With extensive experiments across different watermarking models, the proposed method achieves promising attack success rates. In addition, the analysis results of the watermark characteristics in term of backdoor attack further support the effectiveness of our approach. The code is available at:https://github.com/aiiu-lab/BackdoorImageEditing
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel backdoor attack framework for image editing diffusion models.  Instead of using visible triggers, the method embeds imperceptible watermarks using off-the-shelf deep watermarking models. These watermarks act as hidden triggers during inference, causing the model to generate a predefined backdoor target when a watermarked image is input, while editing clean images as expected. The authors evaluate the framework's effectiveness using various watermarking models and analyze the watermark properties that contribute to successful attacks. The results show promising attack success rates and robustness to certain real-world distortions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of deep watermarking as a means to inject *invisible* backdoor triggers into *image editing* diffusion models.  Prior work in backdoor attacks on diffusion models has largely focused on image *generation* and often employed *visible* triggers. While ISSBA utilizes watermarking for classification network attacks, this paper is the first to integrate deep watermarking techniques with instruction-based *image editing diffusion* models specifically.  The shift from visible to invisible triggers is important for practical applications where preserving input integrity is key.  Furthermore, the analysis of watermark properties in relation to attack success is a valuable contribution.

**Significance and Impact:**

The significance stems from highlighting a new vulnerability vector in image editing diffusion models, especially concerning applications where image integrity and ownership are paramount. The imperceptible nature of the attack makes it difficult to detect and prevent. The paper provides a proof-of-concept attack, demonstrates its effectiveness across different watermarking techniques, and analyzes contributing factors. This work should raise awareness within the community, prompting further research into robust defenses against such attacks and the development of watermarking methods resilient to use as backdoor triggers.  The analysis of the relationship between watermark properties and attack success also provides insights for future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The use of deep watermarking for invisible backdoor triggers in image editing is innovative.
*   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a real-world concern regarding the security of image editing models.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper offers a decent evaluation with various watermarking models and under different distortions.
*   **Analysis of Watermark Properties:** The analysis of the impact of watermark characteristics on attack success is insightful.
*   **Open Source Code:** The availability of code is commendable and facilitates further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Distortions:** While robustness to certain distortions like erasing and JPEG compression is shown, performance under other common transformations such as rotation, blur, noise, and scaling is relatively poor. A more robust attack might require techniques resilient to a broader range of transformations.
*   **Utility Metrics Could Be Stronger**: While CLIP-based metrics are used, alternatives specifically tailored to image editing might offer better fidelity measures, reflecting the complex nature of modifications.
*   **Reliance on off-the-shelf Watermarking:** The analysis could benefit from exploring customized watermarking algorithms specifically optimized for backdoor injection, potentially yielding superior performance and more control over the trigger properties.
*  **The paper's discussion is limited in terms of real-world applicability, and defense mechanisms.**

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and practically relevant attack, but with limitations in robustness and a dependence on existing watermarking techniques. The analysis provides valuable insights, but there is room for improvement in expanding the scope of distortions and potentially developing customized watermarking strategies. The significance lies in opening up a new attack vector, but the paper does not offer immediate solutions or defensive measures. The paper, though novel and useful, lacks a full-fledged treatment for the vulnerabilities detected, which lowers its overall significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating the Effectiveness of Linguistic Knowledge in Pretrained Language Models: A Case Study of Universal Dependencies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04887v1)
- **Authors**: Wenxi Li
- **Abstract**: Universal Dependencies (UD), while widely regarded as the most successful linguistic framework for cross-lingual syntactic representation, remains underexplored in terms of its effectiveness. This paper addresses this gap by integrating UD into pretrained language models and assesses if UD can improve their performance on a cross-lingual adversarial paraphrase identification task. Experimental results show that incorporation of UD yields significant improvements in accuracy and $F_1$ scores, with average gains of 3.85\% and 6.08\% respectively. These enhancements reduce the performance gap between pretrained models and large language models in some language pairs, and even outperform the latter in some others. Furthermore, the UD-based similarity score between a given language and English is positively correlated to the performance of models in that language. Both findings highlight the validity and potential of UD in out-of-domain tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the effectiveness of integrating Universal Dependencies (UD) into pretrained language models (PLMs) for cross-lingual paraphrase identification (PI). The authors transform UD dependency structures into hypergraphs and then construct a similarity matrix based on lexical alignment and label comparison.  This matrix is then injected into the attention mechanism of PLMs.  The models are evaluated on the PAWS-X dataset. The results demonstrate that incorporating UD information improves the accuracy and F1 scores of PLMs on this task. Furthermore, the UD-based similarity score between languages correlates positively with model performance, suggesting UD captures relevant cross-lingual syntactic information.  The paper concludes that UD can enhance PLM performance and highlights the continued relevance of linguistics in the era of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a gap:** The paper tackles the underexplored area of applying UD to improve PLMs in cross-lingual tasks beyond parsing.
    *   **Clear methodology:** The process of transforming dependencies into hypergraphs and integrating them into the attention mechanism is well-defined and explained.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The use of PAWS-X dataset provides a solid basis for evaluating the proposed method.
    *   **Positive Results**: Demonstrated performance boosts when incorporating UD into pretrained language models
    *   **Interesting Correlation**: The positive correlation between UD similarity and performance offers insights into cross-lingual transfer learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on one dataset (PAWS-X) and a single task (paraphrase identification).  The generalizability to other tasks and datasets is not thoroughly investigated.
    *   **Modest improvements**: The core improvement isn't huge (average gains of 3.85% and 6.08%), and the method's complexity might not justify the performance gain in some cases.
    *   **Black Box nature**: The inner workings of UD-based similarity metric with regard to the nature of UD representation are opaque.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The conversion of dependency trees into hypergraphs for a similarity metric is a somewhat novel engineering approach within the intersection of NLP and graph theory.
    *   The direct integration of UD information into the attention mechanism of PLMs is a relatively novel method.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers evidence for the utility of linguistic knowledge in enhancing PLM performance.
    *   The correlation analysis is helpful for understanding cross-lingual transfer learning and language similarity.
    *   The work provides a framework for exploring other linguistic features and their potential to improve PLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable, well-executed study with a clear methodology and positive results. The integration of UD information is a novel approach, and the findings contribute to our understanding of how linguistic knowledge can be leveraged in PLMs. However, the scope of the study is somewhat limited by the single dataset and the modest performance improvements. Given these strengths and weaknesses, and taking into account that the gains, while positive, are not necessarily groundbreaking, a score of **6.5** is assigned. This score reflects a contribution that is solid and valuable but not radically transformative. The approach shows promise and opens doors for future research directions, but significant work is needed to further generalize and optimize it.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICPC-Eval: Probing the Frontiers of LLM Reasoning with Competitive Programming Contests
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04894v1)
- **Authors**: Shiyi Xu, Yiwen Hu, Yingqian Min, Zhipeng Chen, Wayne Xin Zhao, Ji-Rong Wen
- **Abstract**: With the significant progress of large reasoning models in complex coding and reasoning tasks, existing benchmarks, like LiveCodeBench and CodeElo, are insufficient to evaluate the coding capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in real competition environments. Moreover, current evaluation metrics such as Pass@K fail to capture the reflective abilities of reasoning models. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{ICPC-Eval}, a top-level competitive coding benchmark designed to probing the frontiers of LLM reasoning. ICPC-Eval includes 118 carefully curated problems from 11 recent ICPC contests held in various regions of the world, offering three key contributions: 1) A challenging realistic ICPC competition scenario, featuring a problem type and difficulty distribution consistent with actual contests. 2) A robust test case generation method and a corresponding local evaluation toolkit, enabling efficient and accurate local evaluation. 3) An effective test-time scaling evaluation metric, Refine@K, which allows iterative repair of solutions based on execution feedback. The results underscore the significant challenge in evaluating complex reasoning abilities: top-tier reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1 often rely on multi-turn code feedback to fully unlock their in-context reasoning potential when compared to non-reasoning counterparts. Furthermore, despite recent advancements in code generation, these models still lag behind top-performing human teams. We release the benchmark at: https://github.com/RUCAIBox/Slow_Thinking_with_LLMs
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ICPC-Eval paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ICPC-Eval, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in the context of competitive programming. The benchmark consists of 118 problems curated from recent ICPC (International Collegiate Programming Contest) competitions, designed to be more challenging and representative of real-world competition scenarios than existing benchmarks. A key feature of ICPC-Eval is its focus on enabling local evaluation by providing a robust test case generation method and toolkit. The paper also proposes a novel evaluation metric, Refine@K, which measures an LLM's ability to iteratively refine its solutions based on execution feedback, mirroring the iterative problem-solving process in actual competitions. The paper evaluates a range of state-of-the-art LLMs on ICPC-Eval, demonstrating the significant gap between current model performance and top human competitors and highlighting the effectiveness of Refine@K in capturing model reasoning abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a few significant novelties. First, the benchmark itself, ICPC-Eval, is a valuable contribution, being specifically designed to evaluate LLMs on challenging competitive programming problems. While existing benchmarks exist, this one attempts to better mirror real-world complexity and provide accessible local evaluation tools. The test case generation is also a novel idea.
