By coupling intent differentiation with contextual cues, our approach commendably enhances LLM performance in both detection and explanation tasks, effectively addressing the inherent ambiguity in self-harm signals. The SHINES dataset, CESM-100 and codebase are publicly available at: https://www.iitp.ac.in/~ai-nlp-ml/resources.html#SHINES .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Just a Scratch X: Enhancing LLM Capabilities for Self-harm Detection through Intent Differentiation and Emoji Interpretation" introduces a novel approach to improving self-harm detection on social media using Large Language Models (LLMs). It focuses on distinguishing between casual mentions (CM) and serious intent (SI) in self-harm expressions, incorporating emoji interpretation to enhance LLM comprehension. The paper introduces the Centennial Emoji Sensitivity Matrix (CESM-100), a curated set of emojis with self-harm interpretations, and the SHINES dataset, which is annotated for self-harm labels, CM/SI spans, and emoji interpretations. The proposed framework fine-tunes LLMs for multi-task learning (self-harm detection, CM/SI span detection) and generates explainable rationales. The framework is evaluated across three LLMs (Llama 3, Mental-Alpaca, and MentalLlama) under various settings. Results demonstrate that the approach improves performance in both detection and explanation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components:

    *   The explicit focus on differentiating between casual mentions and serious intent in self-harm detection is a valuable contribution. While previous work has addressed self-harm detection, this nuanced approach is relatively underexplored.
    *   The creation of CESM-100 is significant, providing a curated resource for contextual emoji understanding in self-harm scenarios. This addresses a gap in existing research, which often overlooks the nuanced role of emojis.
    *   The SHINES dataset, with its detailed annotations, provides a valuable benchmark for future research.
    *   The multi-task learning framework, combining self-harm classification, CM/SI span extraction, and rationale generation, is a sound approach to enhance LLM's reasoning capabilities.
*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to make a real-world impact:

    *   Improved self-harm detection can lead to more effective and timely interventions, potentially preventing suicides.
    *   The explainable rationales generated by the framework could improve the transparency and trust in AI-based mental health tools.
    *   The publicly available dataset and codebase will facilitate further research in this critical area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper tackles a challenging and important problem.
    *   The approach is well-designed and incorporates several innovative elements.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough and includes comparisons with strong baselines.
    *   The qualitative analysis provides valuable insights into the LLMs' behavior.
    *   The public release of the dataset, emoji matrix, and code enhances reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The data is collected only from Reddit, which may introduce platform bias. While the authors acknowledge this and explain their rationale, it remains a limitation. Expanding the dataset to other platforms would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The choice of models (while justified given hardware limitations) limits the scope of the findings.  Evaluating the framework on larger, more powerful models (e.g., larger Llama 3 models, GPT-4, Gemini) would provide a better understanding of its potential.
    *   The gains from synthetic data, while present, are not dramatically highlighted in the current evaluation.  A more in-depth analysis of the impact of synthetic data, especially concerning different generation methods and levels of manual revision, would be valuable.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of the ethical implications of self-harm detection, beyond the points briefly outlined in the ethical considerations section. For instance, potential misinterpretations by non-experts, the potential for false positives and their consequences, and the trade-offs between privacy and intervention warrant a more detailed discussion.

* **Conclusion:** The paper makes a strong contribution to the field of self-harm detection by introducing a novel approach, a valuable dataset and emoji matrix, and a comprehensive evaluation. The limitations relating to data source and model size are acknowledged and do not detract significantly from the paper's overall impact.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates a significant advancement in a critical area of mental health support. The novelty of the intent-focused approach, the development of the CESM-100, and the release of the SHINES dataset are all strong points. The limitations are relatively minor and do not overshadow the overall value of the research. Expanding the dataset and evaluating on larger models in future work could potentially elevate this score further. The ethical considerations are mentioned, but could be more in-depth.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SeedEdit 3.0: Fast and High-Quality Generative Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05083v1)
- **Authors**: Peng Wang, Yichun Shi, Xiaochen Lian, Zhonghua Zhai, Xin Xia, Xuefeng Xiao, Weilin Huang, Jianchao Yang
- **Abstract**: We introduce SeedEdit 3.0, in companion with our T2I model Seedream 3.0 [22], which significantly improves over our previous version [27] in both aspects of edit instruction following and image content (e.g., ID/IP) preservation on real image inputs. Additional to model upgrading with T2I, in this report, we present several key improvements. First, we develop an enhanced data curation pipeline with a meta-info paradigm and meta-info embedding strategy that help mix images from multiple data sources. This allows us to scale editing data effectively, and meta information is helpfult to connect VLM with diffusion model more closely. Second, we introduce a joint learning pipeline for computing a diffusion loss and a reward loss. Finally, we evaluate SeedEdit 3.0 on our testing benchmarks, for real image editing, where it achieves a best trade-off between multiple aspects, yielding a high usability rate of 56.1%, compared to SeedEdit 1.6 (38.4%), GPT4o (37.1%) and Gemini 2.0 (30.3%).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SeedEdit 3.0 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SeedEdit 3.0, a generative image editing model built upon the Seedream 3.0 text-to-image model. It focuses on improving both edit instruction following and image content preservation when editing real images. Key improvements include an enhanced data curation pipeline utilizing a meta-information paradigm to combine diverse data sources, and a joint learning pipeline incorporating both diffusion and reward losses. The model demonstrates improved performance on a internal benchmark compared to previous versions (SeedEdit 1.6) and competitor models like GPT-40 and Gemini 2.0, achieving a better trade-off between usability, content preservation, and instruction adherence, as well as improved speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper builds on previous work (SeedEdit 1.6) but introduces several noteworthy improvements. The core novelty lies in the combination of these elements:

*   **Enhanced Data Curation Pipeline with Meta-Information:** Using meta-info to mix datasets and help the model connect VLMs more effectively with diffusion models. While data curation itself isn't entirely new, the structured meta-information approach and its integration into the training pipeline are a contribution.
*   **Joint Learning with Diffusion and Reward Losses:** This addresses the issue of general diffusion losses treating all parts of an image as equally important, allowing the model to focus on preserving high-value attributes like face identity or detailed structures. This is a relatively novel strategy for image editing.
*   **Inference Efficiency Improvements:** Using distillation,  CFG distillation,  unified noise reference, and adaptive timestep sampling, there is a significant speedup in inference time.

**Significance:**

The paper tackles a practical and important problem: creating a robust and high-quality image editing tool that can effectively handle real-world images and user instructions. The improvements in usability and speed are particularly relevant for practical applications. Demonstrating improved performance over commercial products like GPT-40 and Gemini 2.0, even on a internal benchmark, suggests a meaningful advancement in the field. The joint learning approach, if generalizable, can have implications for improving the quality and controllability of other generative models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of instructive image editing, including data curation and content preservation.
*   **Comprehensive approach:** It addresses the problem through multiple angles – data curation, model architecture, training strategy, and inference efficiency.
*   **Demonstrated improvement over existing methods:** Quantitative and qualitative results suggest a tangible improvement in performance and usability.
*   **Emphasis on practical application:** The focus on usability and speed makes the model more suitable for real-world deployment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Internal Benchmark:** Results are primarily evaluated on a internal benchmark, limiting generalizability. While useful for internal comparison, external validation is needed to confirm superiority over publicly available datasets and models.
*   **Limited details on the exact Reward Models used:** More details about the architecture and training of the reward models would increase the reproducibility and understanding of the approach.
*   **Limited discussion of failure cases or limitations:** The paper focuses primarily on successes, a more complete analysis would discuss failure modes and limitations of the approach.
*   **Ablation studies may be limited**: Further ablation studies for each model improvement will improve the paper.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence research in the following areas:

*   **Data curation strategies:** The meta-information approach may inspire other researchers to explore structured data labeling and integration techniques.
*   **Loss functions for generative models:** The joint learning with reward losses may lead to the development of more sophisticated loss functions that prioritize specific attributes.
*   **Efficient inference techniques:** The distillation and adaptive timestep sampling methods may contribute to more efficient generative models in general.

**Conclusion:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of generative image editing, particularly in improving the quality, usability, and efficiency of models for editing real-world images. The enhanced data curation and joint learning approaches are promising directions for future research. While the lack of external benchmarking and some missing details limit its impact somewhat, the overall contribution is significant.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a combination of novel techniques that leads to tangible improvements in image editing quality, usability, and speed. The enhancements to data curation, the introduction of reward losses in training, and efficiency optimizations during inference justify a high score. However, the reliance on a internal benchmark and limited discussion of limitations prevents it from receiving a higher score (8-9) that would indicate a transformative impact on the field. It's a very strong engineering paper, but the science isn't quite as groundbreaking as it could be.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Astraea: A GPU-Oriented Token-wise Acceleration Framework for Video Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05096v1)
- **Authors**: Haosong Liu, Yuge Cheng, Zihan Liu, Aiyue Chen, Yiwu Yao, Chen Chen, Jingwen Leng, Yu Feng, Minyi Guo
- **Abstract**: Video diffusion transformers (vDiTs) have made impressive progress in text-to-video generation, but their high computational demands present major challenges for practical deployment. While existing acceleration methods reduce workload at various granularities, they often rely on heuristics, limiting their applicability. We introduce ASTRAEA, an automatic framework that searches for near-optimal configurations for vDiT-based video generation. At its core, ASTRAEA proposes a lightweight token selection mechanism and a memory-efficient, GPU-parallel sparse attention strategy, enabling linear reductions in execution time with minimal impact on generation quality. To determine optimal token reduction for different timesteps, we further design a search framework that leverages a classic evolutionary algorithm to automatically determine the distribution of the token budget effectively. Together, ASTRAEA achieves up to 2.4x inference speedup on a single GPU with great scalability (up to 13.2x speedup on 8 GPUs) while retaining better video quality compared to the state-of-the-art methods (<0.5% loss on the VBench score compared to the baseline vDiT models).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ASTRAEA: A GPU-Oriented Token-wise Acceleration Framework for Video Diffusion Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ASTRAEA, a novel framework designed to accelerate video diffusion transformers (vDiTs) specifically for text-to-video generation. ASTRAEA tackles the computational bottlenecks inherent in vDiTs by focusing on token-level optimizations. It proposes a lightweight token selection mechanism to identify and prioritize important tokens during the denoising process.  It also features a GPU-friendly sparse attention strategy that reduces computation while minimizing the impact on video quality. An evolutionary algorithm is employed to automatically search for optimal token budget allocation across different denoising timesteps. The authors demonstrate that ASTRAEA achieves significant inference speedups on GPUs (up to 2.4x on a single GPU and 13.2x on 8 GPUs) while maintaining or even improving video quality compared to existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of token-wise acceleration for vDiTs is not entirely new (e.g., ToCa [43]). However, ASTRAEA differentiates itself through its specific combination of techniques and its focus on GPU-friendliness, which addresses the limitations of prior work. The key novelties lie in:

    *   The **lightweight token selection mechanism:** The paper argues that previous token selection methods introduce significant overheads, and ASTRAEA aims to minimize these.  The use of  $S_{penalty}$ term which discourage repeated selection of the same tokens across timesteps and the computation of LSE which are already byproduct of softmax can be considered incremental advancement.
    *   The **GPU-efficient sparse attention strategy:** Unlike naive sparse attention approaches that suffer from computational inaccuracies and high memory overhead, ASTRAEA computes attention only for the selected queries, leading to more efficient parallelization on GPUs. This is a significant contribution, as it makes the framework more practical for real-world deployment.
    *   The **evolutionary search framework:** Automatically determining optimal token budgets for each timestep is a non-trivial problem. While evolutionary algorithms are well-established, their application to fine-tuning token selection in vDiTs, particularly with the described repair and mutation steps, is valuable. This aspect automates a process that often requires manual tuning.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the field of video generation: the high computational cost of vDiTs. By achieving substantial speedups without significantly compromising video quality, ASTRAEA has the potential to make vDiTs more accessible and practical for wider use. The focus on GPU optimization is particularly relevant, as GPUs are the primary hardware platform for deep learning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents thorough evaluations on multiple models and benchmarks (Wan, OpenSora, VBench). The comparisons to state-of-the-art methods are comprehensive, and the reported speedups and quality metrics are convincing. The qualitative results also visually demonstrate the effectiveness of ASTRAEA.
    *   **Well-designed framework:** The architecture of ASTRAEA, with its distinct token selection, sparse attention, and search components, is logically structured and well-explained.
    *   **Clear focus on practical deployment:** The paper consistently emphasizes the importance of GPU efficiency and low overhead, making ASTRAEA a more practical solution than methods that might achieve slightly better quality but at a significantly higher computational cost.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Search cost:** The evolutionary search process requires significant computational resources (82 GPU hours). While this cost is amortized over many uses of the optimized model, it still represents a barrier to entry. A discussion of potential ways to reduce the search cost or make it more efficient would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Incremental improvements:** While ASTRAEA combines several techniques effectively, the individual components can be considered as incremental improvements.  The novelty lies in the design choices and the way these components are combined.
    *   **Limited ablation studies:** While the paper presents results on different token budget percentages and shows results from running with or without *Timestep-Level* and *Fixed-token*, it could have contained ablation study for the *Spenatly* term in the token selection.

*   **Potential Influence:** ASTRAEA has the potential to influence the development of future video generation frameworks by demonstrating the effectiveness of token-level optimizations and GPU-aware design. It provides a valuable framework for researchers and practitioners looking to accelerate vDiT inference. Also, since its token selection uses attention values, It can potentially use other type of features which have similar or better effect.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   **Justification:**
    While the paper addresses an important problem and presents a well-designed, effective framework, the novelty is not groundbreaking. The strength of the work lies in the combination of existing techniques, the careful attention to GPU efficiency, and the comprehensive experimental results. The search cost and incremental advances in particular aspects of the design prevent it from achieving a higher score. However, the improvements in speed and memory usage without much decrease in video quality compared to existing methods are significant, and the potential to influence future research on video generation warrants a rating that reflects real value. ASTRAEA is a practical solution that tackles a key bottleneck in vDiTs and will likely be used as a baseline for future work in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Membership Inference Attacks on Sequence Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05126v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Rossi, Michael Aerni, Jie Zhang, Florian Tramèr
- **Abstract**: Sequence models, such as Large Language Models (LLMs) and autoregressive image generators, have a tendency to memorize and inadvertently leak sensitive information. While this tendency has critical legal implications, existing tools are insufficient to audit the resulting risks. We hypothesize that those tools' shortcomings are due to mismatched assumptions. Thus, we argue that effectively measuring privacy leakage in sequence models requires leveraging the correlations inherent in sequential generation. To illustrate this, we adapt a state-of-the-art membership inference attack to explicitly model within-sequence correlations, thereby demonstrating how a strong existing attack can be naturally extended to suit the structure of sequence models. Through a case study, we show that our adaptations consistently improve the effectiveness of memorization audits without introducing additional computational costs. Our work hence serves as an important stepping stone toward reliable memorization audits for large sequence models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of membership inference attacks (MIAs) on sequence models like Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that existing MIAs, often based on the Likelihood Ratio Attack (LiRA), perform poorly on sequence models because they fail to account for the inherent correlations between sequence elements. They adapt LiRA to explicitly model these correlations, demonstrating significant improvements in attack effectiveness without introducing substantial computational overhead. Their adaptations involve estimating the covariance between sequence elements, using techniques like the Oracle Approximating Shrinkage Estimator (OAS), and studying the impact of different covariance structures (class-wise vs. shared). They conduct experiments on LSTM and transformer-based LLMs, showing consistent performance gains from their adaptations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting MIAs to account for within-sequence correlations is reasonably novel.  While MIAs themselves are not new, the application to sequence models and the specific focus on addressing the independence assumption are contributions. The use of OAS for covariance estimation in this context is also a practical contribution. However, it's important to acknowledge that others have considered sequence-specific vulnerabilities; the paper's value lies in the careful and principled way it tackles this issue, specifically by adapting LiRA.
* **Significance:** The paper is significant because it highlights a critical limitation of standard MIAs when applied to sequence models, a domain of increasing importance. Showing that leveraging sequential correlations can greatly improve attack effectiveness has important implications for privacy auditing and risk assessment of LLMs and other sequence-based generative models. The empirical results support the theoretical arguments and offer practical guidance for building more robust MIAs. Also, the paper’s analysis of the number of shadow models and selection of length-reduction strategies has meaningful implications for real-world implementations of MIAs. However, the presented attack modifications still leverage LiRA, not the novel attacks which could be significantly more performant.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the problem of mismatched assumptions in standard MIAs for sequence models.
    *   Principled approach: The adaptations to LiRA are well-motivated and based on sound statistical reasoning.
    *   Empirical validation: The experiments are thorough and provide strong evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed adaptations.
    *   Practical insights: The paper offers practical guidance on choosing covariance estimators and sequence length reduction strategies.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope: The adaptations are primarily focused on LiRA. While useful, it remains unclear if the exact same techniques generalize to other, potentially more powerful, MIA frameworks.
    *   Dataset limitations: Although PersonaChat mimics a somewhat realistic setting, it might not contain the extreme memorization examples seen with very large real-world datasets (e.g., web scraping data).
    *   Lack of a defense: The paper focuses on improving attacks. A valuable extension would be to explore potential defenses informed by the improved MIA techniques.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of privacy and security for sequence models. It highlights a limitation of current MIA methods and demonstrates a practical approach for improving their effectiveness. Although the core framework relies heavily on LiRA, the adaptations, analysis, and empirical validation are significant and offer insights into effectively measuring memorization in sequence models.  While not a revolutionary new attack, the work substantially improves upon existing attacks and provides key insights. The practical guidance for real-world implementation of these attacks is also a definite plus. The identified limitation of scope concerning the applicability of the presented method to other MIAs and datasets, alongside the lack of defensive measures, prevents the paper from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### PixCell: A generative foundation model for digital histopathology images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05127v1)
- **Authors**: Srikar Yellapragada, Alexandros Graikos, Zilinghan Li, Kostas Triaridis, Varun Belagali, Saarthak Kapse, Tarak Nath Nandi, Ravi K Madduri, Prateek Prasanna, Tahsin Kurc, Rajarsi R. Gupta, Joel Saltz, Dimitris Samaras
- **Abstract**: The digitization of histology slides has revolutionized pathology, providing massive datasets for cancer diagnosis and research. Contrastive self-supervised and vision-language models have been shown to effectively mine large pathology datasets to learn discriminative representations. On the other hand, generative models, capable of synthesizing realistic and diverse images, present a compelling solution to address unique problems in pathology that involve synthesizing images; overcoming annotated data scarcity, enabling privacy-preserving data sharing, and performing inherently generative tasks, such as virtual staining. We introduce PixCell, the first diffusion-based generative foundation model for histopathology. We train PixCell on PanCan-30M, a vast, diverse dataset derived from 69,184 H\&E-stained whole slide images covering various cancer types. We employ a progressive training strategy and a self-supervision-based conditioning that allows us to scale up training without any annotated data. PixCell generates diverse and high-quality images across multiple cancer types, which we find can be used in place of real data to train a self-supervised discriminative model. Synthetic images shared between institutions are subject to fewer regulatory barriers than would be the case with real clinical images. Furthermore, we showcase the ability to precisely control image generation using a small set of annotated images, which can be used for both data augmentation and educational purposes. Testing on a cell segmentation task, a mask-guided PixCell enables targeted data augmentation, improving downstream performance. Finally, we demonstrate PixCell's ability to use H\&E structural staining to infer results from molecular marker studies; we use this capability to infer IHC staining from H\&E images. Our trained models are publicly released to accelerate research in computational pathology.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PixCell, a generative foundation model for digital histopathology images. It's trained on PanCan-30M, a large and diverse dataset derived from 69,184 H&E-stained whole slide images. The authors employ a progressive training strategy and condition PixCell on self-supervised embeddings to scale up training without annotations. PixCell is shown to generate high-quality, diverse images that can be used in place of real data for training self-supervised discriminative models, enabling privacy-preserving data sharing. The paper also demonstrates PixCell's ability for controllable image generation (cell segmentation mask-guided) and stain translation (H&E to IHC).  The trained models are publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in being the *first* diffusion-based generative *foundation* model for histopathology images trained on a massive, *pan-cancer* dataset. While diffusion models have been explored in histopathology before, they were usually limited to smaller datasets or specific cancer types. The pan-cancer training and the sheer scale are significant advancements. The use of UNI-2h embeddings for conditioning and progressive training contributes to the scalability.

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact of this work is considerable:

    *   **Data Augmentation & Overcoming Data Scarcity:**  The ability to generate realistic histopathology images can address the scarcity of annotated data for various tasks, particularly for rare diseases.
    *   **Privacy-Preserving Data Sharing:** Synthetic data sharing bypasses regulatory hurdles associated with real patient data, facilitating collaboration across institutions. This is a major bottleneck in computational pathology.
    *   **Controllable Generation:** The ControlNet integration allows for precise data augmentation for specific tasks (cell segmentation), which is crucial.
    *   **Virtual Staining:** The H&E to IHC translation could significantly reduce the need for expensive and time-consuming molecular marker studies. This aspect has clinical translational value.
    *   **Community Resource:** Public release of the model weights and code is highly significant for promoting reproducibility and furthering research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-scale training:** The PanCan-30M dataset is a major strength, enabling generalization across diverse cancer types.
    *   **Progressive training and self-supervision:** These are crucial for scaling up training effectively.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper uses a variety of metrics to assess image quality, embedding similarity, and downstream task performance.
    *   **Demonstration of utility:** The paper successfully showcases the usefulness of the generated data in downstream tasks.
    *  **Strong results:** SoTA results reported in several generation/ translation tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Potential for Data Leakage:** Although synthetic data sharing is privacy-preserving in principle, generative models can still leak information from the training data. This risk needs to be acknowledged and mitigated. The paper doesn't delve deeply into these mitigation strategies. Further analysis on the data leakage is warranted.
    *   **Reliance on a Pre-trained SSL Model:** The model relies on UNI-2h, thus is bottlenecked by the biases and limitations of that model. If UNI-2h performs poorly on particular types of histology, so will PixCell.
    *   **Limited discussion on limitations of stain translation:** While promising, stain translation is challenging due to complexities in histochemical processes. Further discussion on the potential sources of error, and methods for minimizing them, would strengthen the work.
    *   **Lack of interpretability:** As a generative model, understanding *why* PixCell makes the decisions it does can be very challenging, making it hard to debug and optimize.
    *   **Limited downstream tasks:** SSL encoders are evaluated; a broader evaluation including segmentation or classification task using only PixCell generated data would further show its usability as a drop-in replacement of real data.

*   **Impact:** I anticipate this work will have a significant impact on computational pathology, particularly in areas related to data augmentation, privacy-preserving data sharing, and virtual staining. It sets a new benchmark for generative models in this field. It can also be a core for further research for controllable/ editable histopathological images.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The score of 8 reflects the paper's significant novelty and potential impact. PixCell introduces a valuable resource for the computational pathology community by providing a large, diverse generative foundation model. The ability to generate synthetic data for downstream tasks, to enable controllable generation, and to explore stain translation has far-reaching implications. However, the score is not higher due to the inherent limitations of synthetic data (data leakage risk), some reliance on existing pre-trained models, limited discussions on the error rate and other limitations of stain transfer and limited downstream task testing. With further work on addressing the data leakage issues and increasing the evaluations, the impact and novelty could further increase. The code and the weights being released increase the reproducibility and impact, boosting the score.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### DiCoRe: Enhancing Zero-shot Event Detection via Divergent-Convergent LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05128v1)
- **Authors**: Tanmay Parekh, Kartik Mehta, Ninareh Mehrabi, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng
- **Abstract**: Zero-shot Event Detection (ED), the task of identifying event mentions in natural language text without any training data, is critical for document understanding in specialized domains. Understanding the complex event ontology, extracting domain-specific triggers from the passage, and structuring them appropriately overloads and limits the utility of Large Language Models (LLMs) for zero-shot ED. To this end, we propose DiCoRe, a divergent-convergent reasoning framework that decouples the task of ED using Dreamer and Grounder. Dreamer encourages divergent reasoning through open-ended event discovery, which helps to boost event coverage. Conversely, Grounder introduces convergent reasoning to align the free-form predictions with the task-specific instructions using finite-state machine guided constrained decoding. Additionally, an LLM-Judge verifies the final outputs to ensure high precision. Through extensive experiments on six datasets across five domains and nine LLMs, we demonstrate how DiCoRe consistently outperforms prior zero-shot, transfer-learning, and reasoning baselines, achieving 4-7% average F1 gains over the best baseline -- establishing DiCoRe as a strong zero-shot ED framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DiCoRe: Enhancing Zero-shot Event Detection via Divergent-Convergent LLM Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DICORE, a novel framework for zero-shot Event Detection (ED) using Large Language Models (LLMs). Addressing the limitations of directly prompting LLMs for ED due to the cognitive overload of complex event ontologies and task constraints, DICORE decouples the ED task into two main stages: divergent reasoning (Dreamer) and convergent reasoning (Grounder). The Dreamer encourages open-ended event discovery to boost recall by removing rigid task constraints. The Grounder aligns these free-form predictions with a task-specific event ontology and uses finite-state machine (FSM)-guided constrained decoding to ensure structured output. A final LLM-Judge filters irrelevant predictions to improve precision. The authors demonstrate, through extensive experiments across six datasets and nine LLMs, that DICORE consistently outperforms existing zero-shot, transfer learning, and reasoning-based baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the divergent-convergent approach to zero-shot ED. While the individual components (LLMs, FSMs) are not new, the architecture and specific combination of Dreamer and Grounder for this task is original. Decoupling the task to improve both recall and precision is a compelling idea. Prior methods often struggled balancing these competing objectives, especially in a zero-shot context. The integration of an LLM-judge to ensure task alignment and semantic validity is also a meaningful addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem (the difficulty of zero-shot ED with LLMs) and provides a practical solution. ED is essential for various downstream applications, and the need for zero-shot methods is increasing due to the scarcity of expert-annotated domain-specific data. DICORE's performance gains over established baselines are significant, demonstrating its potential to advance the field. The demonstrated ability of DICORE to outperform fine-tuned baselines, even with significantly fewer inference tokens, emphasizes its potential for practical deployment where computational cost is a constraint.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and clear solution:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of zero-shot ED with LLMs and proposes a coherent and well-explained solution.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** The extensive experiments across diverse datasets and LLMs make a strong case for the generalizability and robustness of DICORE.
    *   **Performance gains:** DICORE consistently outperforms strong baselines (including transfer learning approaches) by a meaningful margin.
    *   **Efficiency:** The reduced inference cost compared to other reasoning-based methods is a significant advantage.
    *   **Detailed implementation and supplementary materials:** The paper includes a detailed methodology section and provides comprehensive implementation details in the appendix. This level of detail ensures reproducibility and facilitates future research.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of each DICORE component to the overall performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Component Synergies - could be stronger.** While the authors show that all components of their model are important, they do not provide a formal analysis of synergies. For example, they allude to that a similar model performs best when one component is removed, but do not rigorously explore when this happens.
    *   **Limited error analysis**: Although the paper demonstrates examples that benefit the model's output, there could be an expansion to the error analysis in this area, rather than primarily relying on quantitative metrics.
    *   **Dependence on prompt engineering:** Like many LLM-based methods, DICORE relies on prompt engineering. While the paper provides details about the prompts, there is the potential for better analysis of the sensitivity of the system's performance to variations in the prompt design.
    *   **Generalization limitations:** While the six datasets are from diverse domains, all of them are English and text-based.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of ED and LLM applications. The divergent-convergent reasoning paradigm is a valuable contribution that can be extended to other tasks where LLMs struggle with complex constraints and limited data. The proposed framework provides a strong foundation for future research on zero-shot ED and can serve as a benchmark for comparing other methods. The insights into balancing recall and precision and reducing the cognitive load on LLMs will be valuable for other researchers.

**Score:** 8/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel, well-evaluated, and efficient framework for zero-shot ED that addresses the limitations of existing methods and achieves significant performance gains. The framework has strong theoretical foundations and is highly practical. However, there are areas of the model's synergies and a deeper error analysis that could be improved. Despite these issues, this paper advances the state of the art and provides valuable insights.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Do Large Language Models Judge Error Severity Like Humans?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05142v1)
- **Authors**: Diege Sun, Guanyi Chen, Fan Zhao, Xiaorong Cheng, Tingting He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as automated evaluators in natural language generation, yet it remains unclear whether they can accurately replicate human judgments of error severity. In this study, we systematically compare human and LLM assessments of image descriptions containing controlled semantic errors. We extend the experimental framework of van Miltenburg et al. (2020) to both unimodal (text-only) and multimodal (text + image) settings, evaluating four error types: age, gender, clothing type, and clothing colour. Our findings reveal that humans assign varying levels of severity to different error types, with visual context significantly amplifying perceived severity for colour and type errors. Notably, most LLMs assign low scores to gender errors but disproportionately high scores to colour errors, unlike humans, who judge both as highly severe but for different reasons. This suggests that these models may have internalised social norms influencing gender judgments but lack the perceptual grounding to emulate human sensitivity to colour, which is shaped by distinct neural mechanisms. Only one of the evaluated LLMs, Doubao, replicates the human-like ranking of error severity, but it fails to distinguish between error types as clearly as humans. Surprisingly, DeepSeek-V3, a unimodal LLM, achieves the highest alignment with human judgments across both unimodal and multimodal conditions, outperforming even state-of-the-art multimodal models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Do Large Language Models Judge Error Severity Like Humans?"

**Summary:**

This paper explores the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to judge the severity of errors in image descriptions in a human-like manner. It compares LLM assessments with human judgments in both unimodal (text-only) and multimodal (text + image) settings. The study extends the experimental framework of van Miltenburg et al. (2020) by evaluating four error types: age, gender, clothing type, and clothing color.  The key findings reveal that while humans assign varying severity levels to different error types (especially with visual context amplifying color and type errors), most LLMs struggle to replicate this nuanced perception.  Specifically, LLMs show a tendency to underestimate the severity of color errors and demonstrate a different sensitivity to gender errors than humans. One exception is the model Doubao, which partially replicates the human-like ranking, but fails to clearly distinguish between error types. Surprisingly, the unimodal model DeepSeek-V3 achieves a high alignment with human judgements across both multimodal and unimodal conditions. The authors conclude that LLMs often lack the perceptual grounding to accurately assess error severity in the same way that humans do, especially concerning visual information, and can also be influenced by social biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of human and LLM assessments of error severity, particularly in the context of both unimodal and multimodal image description evaluation. While prior work has explored LLMs as evaluators, this study's focus on *error severity* and its systematic investigation across different error *types* and *modalities* is a valuable contribution. Investigating potential social bias encoded in language models is also a good addition. The comparison between different LLM architectures is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight the limitations of using LLMs as direct replacements for human evaluators in NLG, especially in tasks involving visual context. Demonstrating the discrepancies between human and LLM error severity judgments reveals crucial areas for improvement in LLM-based evaluation metrics. This study has implications for the design of more robust and human-aligned NLG evaluation systems. Also, this research offers more context for future developments towards more controllable and less biased evaluation metrics.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Controlled Experimentation:** The paper employs a well-designed, controlled experiment to isolate the effects of error type, modality, and model architecture.
    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The comprehensive analysis using LMMs and pairwise comparisons provides strong evidence for the claims made.
    *   **Multiple Models:** The evaluation of several LLMs, including both multimodal and unimodal models, allows for a robust comparison of their capabilities.
    *   **Focus on a specific NLG subtask:** The focus on image description error evaluation offers specific context for results, which is a better design.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Errors:** While the study considers four error types, other relevant errors in image descriptions (e.g., object detection errors, relational errors) are not explored.
    *   **Sample Size:** The sample size of human participants, while meeting the calculated requirements, could potentially be increased to further strengthen the statistical power of the results.
    *   **Closed-Source LLMs:** The use of closed-source LLMs (GPT-4o and Doubao) makes it difficult to fully understand the underlying mechanisms driving the observed differences in error severity judgments. However, the authors address this concern with the argument of practical usability given their ubiquity and high relevance.
    *   **Prompt sensitivity** The study used a specific type of prompt, which may affect how the model would perform in comparison with human judgment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated LLM-based evaluation metrics for NLG. By highlighting the discrepancies between human and LLM judgments, it can guide future research towards incorporating perceptual grounding and bias mitigation strategies into LLM evaluators. Also, these findings can be generalized to other types of applications, like document generation.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

The paper presents a solid experimental design and systematic analysis that uncover interesting and important discrepancies between human and LLM judgments of error severity in image descriptions. The findings are relevant to the NLG community and have the potential to influence the development of more human-aligned evaluation metrics. However, the limitations regarding the scope of error types, sample size and the closed-source nature of some LLMs prevent it from receiving a higher score. It's a well-executed study that adds incrementally to our understanding but doesn't represent a paradigm shift.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Knowledgeable-r1: Policy Optimization for Knowledge Exploration in Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05154v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyu Lin, Yilin Wen, Du Su, Fei Sun, Muhan Chen, Chenfu Bao, Zhonghou Lv
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a mainstream method for improving performance on knowledge-intensive tasks. However,current RAG systems often place too much emphasis on retrieved contexts. This can lead to reliance on inaccurate sources and overlook the model's inherent knowledge, especially when dealing with misleading or excessive information. To resolve this imbalance, we propose Knowledgeable-r1 that using joint sampling and define multi policy distributions in knowledge capability exploration to stimulate large language models'self-integrated utilization of parametric and contextual knowledge. Experiments show that Knowledgeable-r1 significantly enhances robustness and reasoning accuracy in both parameters and contextual conflict tasks and general RAG tasks, especially outperforming baselines by 17.07% in counterfactual scenarios and demonstrating consistent gains across RAG tasks. Our code are available at https://github.com/lcy80366872/ knowledgeable-r1.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Knowledgeable-r1: Policy Optimization for Knowledge Exploration in Retrieval-Augmented Generation" addresses the problem of LLMs' over-reliance on retrieved contexts in RAG systems, particularly when those contexts are inaccurate or conflicting with the model's inherent knowledge.  The paper proposes Knowledgeable-r1, a reinforcement learning framework that uses joint sampling and multi-policy distributions to encourage LLMs to utilize both parametric and contextual knowledge more effectively. The experiments demonstrate improved robustness and accuracy in handling knowledge conflicts and in general RAG tasks, especially under counterfactual conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to improve RAG systems by explicitly encouraging LLMs to leverage their own parametric knowledge in addition to retrieved contextual knowledge.  The use of reinforcement learning to balance these two knowledge sources is a valuable contribution, especially given the increasing reliance on RAG in many applications. While some prior work addresses knowledge conflicts, the systematic exploration and optimization of both parametric and contextual knowledge through a reinforcement learning framework seems relatively unexplored. The emphasis on mitigating the LLM's tendency to blindly trust retrieved contexts is a key strength. The use of joint sampling is interesting, however the incremental improvements over existing methods are sometimes marginal.

*   **Significance:** The problem the paper addresses is highly relevant.  As RAG systems become more widespread, ensuring robustness against inaccurate retrieved contexts is crucial. The experimental results suggest that Knowledgeable-r1 offers a significant improvement over existing RAG approaches. Improvements in handling conflicting knowledge can lead to more reliable and trustworthy LLM-based applications. The ablation study, while concise, provides some insights into the contribution of the different components. The performance comparisons with strong baselines like GRPO demonstrate the competitiveness of the approach. The exploration of different metrics in table 3 is also noteworthy.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined reinforcement learning framework.
    *   Comprehensive experiments with relevant baselines.
    *   Detailed evaluation with metrics that measure distinct knowledge integration capabilities.
    *   Open source code.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance gains, while statistically significant, are sometimes incremental, especially compared to highly optimized baselines.
    *   The ablation study could be more extensive. The impact of each individual component could be clearer.
    *   The explanation of the advantage function transformation T(.) could be more detailed.
    *   Limited experiments on more capable base models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable RAG systems.  It provides a valuable framework for addressing knowledge conflicts and encouraging a balanced use of parametric and contextual knowledge in LLMs. The framework might serve as a basis for future research on knowledge integration and conflict resolution in LLMs.

*   **Overall:** The paper is a solid contribution to the field of RAG. It presents a novel reinforcement learning framework for knowledge exploration and addresses an important problem. While the performance improvements are sometimes incremental and the paper has some minor limitations, the clarity of the presentation, the comprehensive experiments, and the potential influence on future research justify a good score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dissecting Bias in LLMs: A Mechanistic Interpretability Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05166v1)
- **Authors**: Bhavik Chandna, Zubair Bashir, Procheta Sen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are known to exhibit social, demographic, and gender biases, often as a consequence of the data on which they are trained. In this work, we adopt a mechanistic interpretability approach to analyze how such biases are structurally represented within models such as GPT-2 and Llama2. Focusing on demographic and gender biases, we explore different metrics to identify the internal edges responsible for biased behavior. We then assess the stability, localization, and generalizability of these components across dataset and linguistic variations. Through systematic ablations, we demonstrate that bias-related computations are highly localized, often concentrated in a small subset of layers. Moreover, the identified components change across fine-tuning settings, including those unrelated to bias. Finally, we show that removing these components not only reduces biased outputs but also affects other NLP tasks, such as named entity recognition and linguistic acceptability judgment because of the sharing of important components with these tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on a mechanistic interpretability approach to dissecting bias in Large Language Models (LLMs):

**Summary:**

The paper investigates bias in LLMs (GPT-2 and Llama-2) using a mechanistic interpretability (MI) approach.  It aims to identify specific internal edges within the model architecture responsible for demographic and gender biases. The methodology involves employing Edge Attribution Patching (EAP) to pinpoint these crucial edges and subsequently assesses their stability, localization, and generalizability across various datasets, linguistic variations, and fine-tuning settings. The study demonstrates that bias-related computations are often highly localized, primarily concentrated in a small subset of layers. Furthermore, identified components may not always remain consistent across settings.  Finally, ablation studies reveal that removing these biased edges impacts not only biased outputs but also other NLP tasks, suggesting shared components and a trade-off between debiasing and maintaining general NLP task performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in its application of mechanistic interpretability techniques to understand bias in LLMs. While previous work has examined bias, dissecting it at the *edge* level and analyzing the stability and generalizability of these edges across different conditions is relatively novel. Previous approaches using individual neurons/attention heads lack generalizability, something this work attempts to improve upon. However, EAP itself is not a novel method, as it is adopted from existing studies. The exploration of the impact of debiasing on other NLP tasks adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** Identifying the specific edges responsible for bias is significant because it offers the potential for targeted interventions rather than relying on computationally expensive retraining or data augmentation. The finding that bias is localized is crucial for efficient debiasing efforts. Revealing the instability of these "bias edges" suggests that debiasing strategies need to be carefully tailored to specific contexts and that debiasing in one situation may not translate effectively to others.  The observation that removing bias-related edges can impact other NLP tasks is a critical consideration for anyone seeking to debias LLMs, highlighting the trade-off between bias mitigation and general task performance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study covers a broad range of conditions, including different types of bias (gender, demographic), models (GPT-2 small/large, Llama-2), datasets, and linguistic structures.
    *   **Mechanistic Focus:**  The focus on edges provides a more granular understanding of how bias is encoded within the model compared to analyses at the neuron or attention head level.
    *   **Trade-off Analysis:**  The paper effectively highlights the trade-off between bias mitigation and general task performance, a crucial consideration often overlooked in debiasing research.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper explains its methodology well, making it replicable and allowing other researchers to build upon its findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Bias Types:** While the study covers gender and demographic bias, other types of bias (e.g., political, religious) are not explored.
    *   **Dependency on EAP:**  The reliance on EAP means the results are contingent on the effectiveness of this technique. While EAP is more efficient than brute-force ablation, it's an approximation, and the findings may not perfectly represent the true causal relationships.
    *   **Limited Generalization across Models:** While the study used different architectures, GPT-2 and Llama-2 are both still transformer-based. Findings might not generalize perfectly to fundamentally different architectures.
    *   **Interpretability Challenges:** Despite the mechanistic focus, understanding *why* specific edges encode bias remains a challenge. The paper identifies *where* bias is, but doesn't fully explain *why* it exists in those particular locations.
    *   **Practical Debias Applicability**: Showing edge manipulation can decrease bias is interesting, however, its true value relies on actually creating a model that is less bias in production.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's findings have the potential to influence future research on debiasing LLMs by encouraging more targeted and context-aware debiasing strategies. The trade-off analysis is particularly important, as it highlights the need for debiasing techniques that minimize the impact on general task performance. The identification of key edges could lead to the development of more efficient and effective debiasing interventions.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:** The paper makes a solid contribution by applying mechanistic interpretability to bias in LLMs, going beyond previous work by focusing on edges and analyzing stability and generalizability. The study's comprehensive nature and focus on the bias/performance trade-off are commendable. However, the reliance on a single EAP method, the limited scope of bias types, and challenges in fully explaining *why* specific edges encode bias prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the study is important, the true value depends on actually creating a debiased model through edge manipulation. The findings still hold promise for informing future debiasing efforts and fostering a deeper understanding of how bias is encoded in LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ECoRAG: Evidentiality-guided Compression for Long Context RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05167v1)
- **Authors**: Yeonseok Jeong, Jinsu Kim, Dohyeon Lee, Seung-won Hwang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable performance in Open-Domain Question Answering (ODQA) by leveraging external documents through Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). To reduce RAG overhead, from longer context, context compression is necessary. However, prior compression methods do not focus on filtering out non-evidential information, which limit the performance in LLM-based RAG. We thus propose Evidentiality-guided RAG, or \textbf{ECoRAG} framework. ECoRAG improves LLM performance by compressing retrieved documents based on evidentiality, ensuring whether answer generation is supported by the correct evidence. As an additional step, ECoRAG reflects whether the compressed content provides sufficient evidence, and if not, retrieves more until sufficient. Experiments show that ECoRAG improves LLM performance on ODQA tasks, outperforming existing compression methods. Furthermore, ECoRAG is highly cost-efficient, as it not only reduces latency but also minimizes token usage by retaining only the necessary information to generate the correct answer. Code is available at https://github.com/ldilab/ECoRAG.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ECORAG: Evidentiality-guided Compression for Long Context RAG":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ECORAG (Evidentiality-guided Compression for Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a novel framework designed to improve the performance and efficiency of RAG systems, particularly those dealing with long contexts. The core idea is to compress retrieved documents by focusing on evidentiality, determining whether sentences support the correct answer to a question. ECORAG first sorts sentences based on their evidentiality (strong evidence, weak evidence, distractor), then adaptively incorporates more evidence until a language model can generate the correct answer. This avoids including irrelevant information and allows LLMs to focus on the necessary content. The authors train a compressor using evidentiality labels and an evaluator to determine if the compressed content is sufficient. They demonstrate, through experiments on ODQA datasets, that ECORAG outperforms existing compression methods and standard RAG baselines in terms of accuracy and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on evidentiality as a guiding principle for context compression within RAG. While context compression is not a new area, the explicit incorporation of a learned evidentiality signal, along with the adaptive compression scheme, distinguishes this work from prior art like RECOMP and LLMLingua. The evidentiality-guided compression is a useful contribution to filter relevant knowledge for the LLM. The work also provides a novel training approach by training the compressor using evidentiality labels.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of ECORAG stems from its ability to address key challenges in RAG systems: handling long contexts, filtering irrelevant information, and improving efficiency. The experimental results support the claim that ECORAG achieves better accuracy and lower latency compared to other methods. The reduction in token usage is also significant, especially given the cost associated with large language model inference. The work shows substantial performance improvements by integrating the evidentiality to the RAG framework.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   The proposed ECoRAG framework is well-defined and explained, with detailed descriptions of each component (compressor, evaluator, adaptive compression).
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and baselines.
    *   Ablation studies are included to assess the contribution of each component.
    *   The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements in ODQA tasks, highlighting the effectiveness of ECORAG.
    *   The authors include detailed implementation details.
    * The paper includes several experiments, including on multi-hop datasets and using multiple models to validate their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Mining evidentiality labels can be computationally expensive, increasing costs. The paper acknowledges this limitation but suggests potential solutions.
    *   The choice of language models and datasets might limit the generalizability of the findings. Exploring more diverse language models and tasks would strengthen the paper.
    *   Although the adaptive compression method has been proposed, the evaluation of time complexity improvement in adaptive compression is somewhat limited. More extensive analyses are needed to assess the speed and complexity improvement of the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** ECoRAG could influence future research in RAG systems, specifically in the area of context compression. The evidentiality-guided approach could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The framework may also inspire new methods for training compressors and evaluators within RAG systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of RAG systems by introducing a novel and effective approach to context compression. The focus on evidentiality is a valuable contribution, and the experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in accuracy and efficiency. However, the computational cost of mining evidentiality labels and the limited range of language models and datasets used in the experiments prevent it from receiving a higher score. Further exploration of these aspects would solidify the paper's long-term influence and impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Associative Memory and Generative Diffusion in the Zero-noise Limit
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05178v1)
- **Authors**: Joshua Hess, Quaid Morris
- **Abstract**: Connections between generative diffusion and continuous-state associative memory models are studied. Morse-Smale dynamical systems are emphasized as universal approximators of gradient-based associative memory models and diffusion models as white-noise perturbed systems thereof. Universal properties of associative memory that follow from this description are described and used to characterize a generic transition from generation to memory as noise levels diminish. Structural stability inherited by Morse-Smale flows is shown to imply a notion of stability for diffusions at vanishing noise levels. Applied to one- and two-parameter families of gradients, this indicates stability at all but isolated points of associative memory learning landscapes and the learning and generation landscapes of diffusion models with gradient drift in the zero-noise limit, at which small sets of generic bifurcations characterize qualitative transitions between stable systems. Examples illustrating the characterization of these landscapes by sequences of these bifurcations are given, along with structural stability criterion for classic and modern Hopfield networks (equivalently, the attention mechanism).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Associative Memory and Generative Diffusion in the Zero-noise Limit," along with a reasoned score.

**Summary:**

This paper explores connections between associative memory models and generative diffusion models, particularly in the context of a vanishing noise limit. It emphasizes Morse-Smale dynamical systems as universal approximators for gradient-based associative memory models and diffusion models as white-noise perturbed versions. The authors characterize the transition from generation to memory as noise levels decrease, describe the universal properties of associative memory, and relate structural stability of Morse-Smale flows to stability in diffusions at vanishing noise. The paper also provides examples, structural stability criteria for classic and modern Hopfield networks, and analyzes bifurcations in one- and two-parameter families of gradients.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Synthesis:** The paper brings together two active areas of research – associative memory and diffusion models – through the lens of dynamical systems theory. This synthesis is insightful and provides a new perspective on both fields.
    *   **Rigorous Mathematical Framework:** The authors leverage concepts from dynamical systems, Morse theory, and large deviation theory to provide a formal framework for analyzing the behavior of these models. This rigor is a significant strength.
    *   **Universal Approximation and Stability:**  The paper highlights the universal approximation capabilities of Morse-Smale gradients and connects structural stability to robustness in both associative memory and diffusion models.  This provides a valuable theoretical understanding.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The analysis offers concrete implications for understanding the learning and generation processes in models like Hopfield networks and denoising diffusion models, making it relevant to practitioners.
    *   **Clear Examples:** The paper includes specific examples that demonstrate the theoretical concepts, making them more accessible and intuitive.
    *   **Structural Stability Criteria:** Providing structural stability criteria for Hopfield networks and their modern counterparts offers potentially useful tools for model design and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the paper provides theoretical insights and examples, there is a lack of extensive empirical validation. The authors primarily focus on theoretical analysis and leave the extensive experimental testing to future work.
    *   **Assumptions and Genericity:** The reliance on genericity and Morse-Smale assumptions, while useful for establishing theoretical results, might limit the applicability of the findings to real-world models that may not strictly satisfy these conditions. Real neural networks might exhibit behaviors not captured by the Morse-Smale framework.
    *   **Complexity:** The mathematical machinery used is advanced and may be difficult for some practitioners to fully grasp. More explanation or simplification could improve accessibility.
    *   **Zero-Noise Limit Idealization:** While studying the zero-noise limit is valuable, it is an idealization. The behavior of models at very low, but non-zero, noise levels could differ in significant ways.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While insightful, the work relies heavily on existing mathematical theories. The "novel synthesis" lies primarily in *applying* these theories to the machine learning setting rather than inventing wholly new theoretical tools.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty lies in the novel *application* of Morse theory and dynamical systems concepts to understand the convergence of diffusion models to associative memories. It’s a valuable contribution, but it's more a novel interpretation and connection of existing knowledge than the creation of revolutionary new theory.
    *   The significance lies in providing a formal understanding of the relationship between two important classes of machine learning models and offers insights into their stability and learning dynamics. However, the theoretical nature and the need for empirical validation mean the real-world impact isn’t yet definitively established.

**Justification for the Score:**

A score of 7 is warranted.

*   The paper has a good blend of theoretical rigor and practical relevance to machine learning, showing the value of dynamic systems analysis.
*   It establishes novel connections between seemingly disparate fields, provides useful structural stability criteria for popular machine learning models, and offers valuable examples, though the experimental validation remains relatively thin.
*   The theoretical nature and reliance on strong assumptions means it isn't groundbreaking in terms of mathematical theory nor does it revolutionize any specific application domain, but the novel application and synthesis of ideas makes it a solid piece of work that provides useful insight and potentially opens new avenues of research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### On the Comprehensibility of Multi-structured Financial Documents using LLMs and Pre-processing Tools
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05182v1)
- **Authors**: Shivani Upadhyay, Messiah Ataey, Shariyar Murtuza, Yifan Nie, Jimmy Lin
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of complex structured data in hybrid sources, such as PDF documents and web pages, presents unique challenges for current Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in providing accurate answers. Despite the recent advancements of MLLMs, they still often falter when interpreting intricately structured information, such as nested tables and multi-dimensional plots, leading to hallucinations and erroneous outputs. This paper explores the capabilities of LLMs and MLLMs in understanding and answering questions from complex data structures found in PDF documents by leveraging industrial and open-source tools as part of a pre-processing pipeline. Our findings indicate that GPT-4o, a popular MLLM, achieves an accuracy of 56% on multi-structured documents when fed documents directly, and that integrating pre-processing tools raises the accuracy of LLMs to 61.3% for GPT-4o and 76% for GPT-4, and with lower overall cost. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/OGCDS/FinancialQA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to comprehend and answer questions about complex, multi-structured financial documents (e.g., PDFs with nested tables and charts). It investigates whether a pre-processing pipeline, leveraging industrial and open-source tools (Azure Document Intelligence, pypdf, ChartVLM), can improve the performance of LLMs on these tasks.  The study compares the accuracy and cost-effectiveness of using LLMs directly versus using LLMs augmented with pre-processed data. The results suggest that while MLLMs like GPT-4 can achieve moderate accuracy on their own, pre-processing significantly improves accuracy, especially for GPT-4, and also offers substantial cost savings compared to commercial end-to-end solutions. The paper identifies remaining challenges in chart and table interpretation and underscores the importance of preserving hierarchical structures during table parsing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Problem:** The paper addresses a very relevant and practical problem faced by many industries: how to effectively extract information from complex documents using LLMs. Financial document analysis is a key application area.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The study provides a thorough empirical evaluation, comparing different models (GPT-4, GPT-3.5 Turbo) and approaches (direct input vs. pre-processing with RAG).  The inclusion of cost analysis is a definite strength and highlights the real-world implications.
    *   **Use of Industry Tools:** The authors use readily available and relevant industry tools, making the results immediately applicable to practitioners.
    *   **Identification of Challenges:** The discussion section provides a valuable analysis of the specific challenges in interpreting charts and tables, pointing to areas for future research.  The identified failures are well-illustrated in the Appendix.
    *   **Open Source Code:** The public availability of the code enhances reproducibility and encourages further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty (Pipeline):** While the problem is practical, the overall approach (pre-processing + RAG) is relatively standard.  The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of tools and the application to financial documents.  A more thorough analysis of *why* these specific pre-processing tools are superior to alternatives (with proper metrics) would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Dataset:** The dataset, while industry-relevant, is a private one. The lack of a publicly available dataset limits the reproducibility and comparability of the results with other research.  Details about the composition of the private dataset would be more helpful. The size is adequate but could be larger.
    *   **ChartVLM fine-tuning:** The unsuccessful ChartVLM fine-tuning seems like a missed opportunity. The paper notes overfitting and limited data, but a deeper analysis of the data augmentation techniques used and the failure modes would provide valuable insights.
    *   **Lack of comparison to alternatives:** The baseline is GPT without any pre-processing vs GPT after applying a chain of tools for pre-processing. This comparison shows that the pipeline is helpful, but it could be helpful to compare the final method to other available solutions using different pre-processing steps for more novelty.
    *   **Scope of Evaluation Set:** The paper could benefit from expanding on the evaluation set to include a broader variety of document types and industries, to assess the generalizability of the approach.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers valuable practical insights for applying LLMs to financial document analysis.
    *   The cost analysis is significant for companies looking to implement these solutions.
    *   The identified challenges with chart and table interpretation can guide future research directions in the field.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper is valuable for its practical insights and empirical evaluation, making it useful for practitioners. The cost analysis, especially, is rarely seen in academic papers and adds significant value. However, the limited novelty of the overall approach and the use of a private dataset hold it back. The limited exploration of the chart fine-tuning is also a drawback.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### TreeRPO: Tree Relative Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05183v1)
- **Authors**: Zhicheng Yang, Zhijiang Guo, Yinya Huang, Xiaodan Liang, Yiwei Wang, Jing Tang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities through Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) methods. However, a key limitation of existing approaches is that rewards defined at the full trajectory level provide insufficient guidance for optimizing the intermediate steps of a reasoning process. To address this, we introduce \textbf{\name}, a novel method that estimates the mathematical expectations of rewards at various reasoning steps using tree sampling. Unlike prior methods that rely on a separate step reward model, \name directly estimates these rewards through this sampling process. Building on the group-relative reward training mechanism of GRPO, \name innovatively computes rewards based on step-level groups generated during tree sampling. This advancement allows \name to produce fine-grained and dense reward signals, significantly enhancing the learning process and overall performance of LLMs. Experimental results demonstrate that our \name algorithm substantially improves the average Pass@1 accuracy of Qwen-2.5-Math on test benchmarks, increasing it from 19.0\% to 35.5\%. Furthermore, \name significantly outperforms GRPO by 2.9\% in performance while simultaneously reducing the average response length by 18.1\%, showcasing its effectiveness and efficiency. Our code will be available at \href{https://github.com/yangzhch6/TreeRPO}{https://github.com/yangzhch6/TreeRPO}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TREERPO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TREERPO, a novel reinforcement learning method for large language models (LLMs) aimed at improving reasoning capabilities.  TREERPO addresses the limitation of trajectory-level rewards by estimating the mathematical expectations of rewards at various reasoning steps through a tree sampling process.
