Unlike methods that rely on a separate step reward model, TREERPO estimates these rewards directly through sampling and computes rewards based on step-level groups.  Experiments demonstrate that TREERPO significantly improves the performance of the Qwen-2.5-Math model on mathematical benchmarks, outperforming GRPO in accuracy and response length.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using tree sampling to estimate step-level rewards in a *reward model-free* setting is moderately novel. Previous methods either relied on outcome rewards, process rewards (which require labeled data), or trajectory-level rewards. TREERPO aims to bridge this gap by inferring process rewards via sampling.  Building upon GRPO and incorporating tree-based sampling is a reasonable extension, but the innovation level isn't groundbreaking. The group-relative reward computation based on step-level groups within the sampled tree structure is also a relatively novel aspect.

*   **Significance:** The experimental results showing improvements in Pass@1 accuracy on mathematical benchmarks and reduced response length are significant.  The fact that TREERPO outperforms GRPO, while also being more concise, suggests it's a valuable improvement. If TREERPO proves to be consistently beneficial across diverse reasoning tasks and LLM architectures, this method could influence future research in reward shaping for reasoning. The reduced response length also hints at a potential for improved efficiency in LLM deployments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies the problem of insufficient guidance from trajectory-level rewards and proposes a solution.
    *   The description of the method (tree sampling, reward estimation, and advantage computation) is relatively clear.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a tangible improvement in performance.
    *   The reduced response length is a positive side effect of the method.
    *   The use of open source frameworks contributes to replicability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of the step division method based on a fixed token length is overly simplistic and potentially a significant limitation. More sophisticated segmentation strategies might yield better results.
    *   The paper does not deeply explore the computational cost associated with tree sampling compared to trajectory-based methods.
    *   The hyperparameters controlling the tree sampling process (branching factor, depth limit, step length) are not thoroughly investigated.  How sensitive is the performance to these parameters?  Are there optimal settings, and how do they vary across different tasks/models?
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited to the Qwen-2.5-Math model.  Demonstrating the effectiveness of TREERPO on other LLMs would strengthen the generality of the approach.
    *   The paper would benefit from an ablation study to isolate the impact of the key components of TREERPO. For example, what is the effect of the tree sampling *versus* the new advantage computation method?
    *   While the paper motivates the need for finer-grained reward signals, it lacks a clear analysis connecting the *estimated step rewards* and *improved performance*. For instance, showing the distribution of intermediate rewards across successful and failed examples.

*   **Potential Influence:** The method could inspire more research into tree-based search and reward shaping techniques for improving LLM reasoning. The findings highlighting efficiency gains (reduced response length) may also spark interest in resource-constrained environments. However, the real impact will depend on how well TREERPO generalizes and how easy it is to implement and scale.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a moderately novel approach for reward shaping in LLMs. While the core idea of tree sampling is not entirely new, its application in a reward model-free setting for generating dense process-level reward signals, combined with a group-relative computation and variance-preserving advantage estimation, adds some value.  The experimental results are promising, demonstrating improved accuracy and efficiency.

However, several weaknesses limit the impact of the paper. The lack of rigorous hyperparameter tuning, the narrow experimental setup (only one model, relatively small benchmarks), the overly simplistic step division method, and the absence of ablation studies leave open significant questions about the robustness and generalizability of TREERPO.

Therefore, given the moderately novel approach and the promising, though not fully convincing, experimental results, the score is 6.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Counterfactual reasoning: an analysis of in-context emergence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05188v1)
- **Authors**: Moritz Miller, Bernhard Schölkopf, Siyuan Guo
- **Abstract**: Large-scale neural language models (LMs) exhibit remarkable performance in in-context learning: the ability to learn and reason the input context on the fly without parameter update. This work studies in-context counterfactual reasoning in language models, that is, to predict the consequences of changes under hypothetical scenarios. We focus on studying a well-defined synthetic setup: a linear regression task that requires noise abduction, where accurate prediction is based on inferring and copying the contextual noise from factual observations. We show that language models are capable of counterfactual reasoning in this controlled setup and provide insights that counterfactual reasoning for a broad class of functions can be reduced to a transformation on in-context observations; we find self-attention, model depth, and data diversity in pre-training drive performance in Transformers. More interestingly, our findings extend beyond regression tasks and show that Transformers can perform noise abduction on sequential data, providing preliminary evidence on the potential for counterfactual story generation. Our code is available under https://github.com/moXmiller/counterfactual-reasoning.git .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to perform in-context counterfactual reasoning. It focuses on a controlled synthetic setup involving linear regression where the model must infer and copy contextual noise from factual observations to predict outcomes under hypothetical scenarios. The authors demonstrate that LLMs, particularly Transformers, are capable of this type of reasoning. They find that self-attention, model depth, and diversity in pre-training data are crucial for performance.  The study extends beyond regression tasks, showing that Transformers can perform noise abduction on sequential data, hinting at the potential for counterfactual story generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of evaluating counterfactual reasoning in LLMs is not entirely new, but this paper contributes by:

    *   **Controlled Setup:** Provides a more rigorous and interpretable framework than purely natural language-based evaluations. The synthetic data allows for precise measurement of performance against a ground truth. This is particularly valuable given the inherent ambiguity in evaluating reasoning, especially in LLMs where it's difficult to disentangle spurious correlations from genuine understanding.
    *   **Mechanistic Insights:** The paper goes beyond simply demonstrating the capability and tries to understand *how* LLMs achieve counterfactual reasoning. The exploration of self-attention, model depth, and data diversity provides valuable insights into the inner workings of these models. The reduction of counterfactual reasoning to transformations on observed values (Lemma 1) is a valuable, though perhaps conceptually straightforward, finding.
    *   **Connection to causality:** Explicitly framing the problem within the causal inference framework is valuable. While not deeply innovative in causal theory, it clarifies the paper's motivations and contributes to a growing body of literature connecting LLMs to causal reasoning.
    *   **Sequential Data Extension:** Showing potential for story generation, even in a limited context, is a promising extension.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have several potential implications:

    *   **Understanding LLMs:** Helps to unpack the "black box" of LLMs by identifying key architectural features and training data characteristics that enable reasoning abilities.
    *   **Causal AI:** Contributes to the development of more robust and reliable AI systems by exploring the connection between language models and causal reasoning. This is especially crucial given concerns about hallucination and brittleness in LLMs.
    *   **Applications:** Could lead to improved LLM-based applications in areas such as decision support, scientific discovery, and creative content generation (e.g., story generation with greater consistency).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data:** The reliance on synthetic data, while enabling precise control and measurement, limits the generalizability of the findings.  It remains to be seen how well these results translate to real-world scenarios involving messy, ambiguous natural language. The transition to Lotka-Volterra models is a positive step, but is still far removed from real stories.
    *   **Limited Model Scale:** The experiments primarily use smaller GPT-2 models.  It's not clear if the findings hold true for much larger models like GPT-3, GPT-4, or PaLM, though it's reasonable to assume that the trend would continue or improve.
    *   **Incremental Advances:** While the findings are interesting, they could be viewed as incremental advances on existing work demonstrating in-context learning and the role of attention.  The theoretical contribution (Lemma 1) provides a useful framing but is not a breakthrough.

*   **Overall:**

    The paper is a solid contribution to the field. It provides a well-defined setup for evaluating counterfactual reasoning in LLMs and provides mechanistic insights into the underlying processes. The use of synthetic data, while a limitation, allows for rigorous evaluation. The findings are potentially significant for understanding and improving the reasoning abilities of LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper deserves a 7 because it represents a well-executed and insightful study of counterfactual reasoning in LLMs, within a *controlled* setting. The mechanistic analysis adds value beyond simply demonstrating the *ability* to perform counterfactual inference. The paper would be more impactful if it successfully made the jump to natural language/real-world scenarios, or demonstrated that the effect *scales* to modern large LLMs (which is likely true). The limitation regarding the degree of novelty and incrementality given the large body of work on in-context learning slightly holds the score back.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quantifying Cross-Modality Memorization in Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05198v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxin Wen, Yangsibo Huang, Tom Goldstein, Ravi Kumar, Badih Ghazi, Chiyuan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Understanding what and how neural networks memorize during training is crucial, both from the perspective of unintentional memorization of potentially sensitive information and from the standpoint of effective knowledge acquisition for real-world, knowledge-intensive tasks. While previous studies primarily investigate memorization within a single modality, such as text memorization in large language models or image memorization in diffusion models, unified multimodal models are becoming increasingly prevalent in practical applications. In this work, we focus on the unique characteristics of cross-modality memorization and conduct a systematic study centered on vision-language models. To facilitate controlled experiments, we first introduce a synthetic persona dataset comprising diverse synthetic person images and textual descriptions. We quantify factual knowledge memorization and cross-modal transferability by training models on a single modality and evaluating their performance in the other. Our results reveal that facts learned in one modality transfer to the other, but a significant gap exists between recalling information in the source and target modalities. Furthermore, we observe that this gap exists across various scenarios, including more capable models, machine unlearning, and the multi-hop case. At the end, we propose a baseline method to mitigate this challenge. We hope our study can inspire future research on developing more robust multimodal learning techniques to enhance cross-modal transferability.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation with a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Quantifying Cross-Modality Memorization in Vision-Language Models" investigates how vision-language models (VLMs) memorize and transfer factual knowledge across image and text modalities. The authors introduce a synthetic persona dataset with both images and descriptions to control experiments. They find that knowledge transfers from one modality to another but with an asymmetry (image-to-text being more effective).  The paper further explores factors affecting cross-modal transfer, including model size, data augmentation, and unlearning. They also identify the "multi-hop curse" in cross-modal reasoning. Finally, they observe unintentional memorization of irrelevant personal information (PII) injected into images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of Problem:**  While memorization in single-modality models (LLMs, diffusion models) has been studied, this paper is among the first to systematically investigate the *cross-modal* aspects of memorization in VLMs. This is a timely and relevant problem, as VLMs are increasingly used in applications where integrating information across modalities is crucial.
    *   **Controlled Experimentation:** The use of a synthetic persona dataset is a significant strength. It allows for precise control over the training data and enables the authors to isolate and quantify different aspects of cross-modal memorization in a way that would be impossible with real-world datasets. This greatly aids in understanding the underlying mechanisms.
    *   **Thorough Empirical Analysis:** The paper includes a comprehensive set of experiments covering various factors, including model size, data augmentation, and unlearning. This provides a well-rounded understanding of the factors that affect cross-modal knowledge transfer.
    *   **Identification of Key Challenges:** The identification of the "multi-hop curse" and the observation of unintentional PII memorization are important findings that highlight potential weaknesses and risks in VLMs.
    *   **Clear Takeaways:** The paper presents clear and concise takeaways after each set of experiments, summarizing the key findings and their implications.
    * **Unintentional memorization of irrelevant visual PII.:** Highlighting the importance of stronger data filtering to mitigate inadvertent privacy risks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Limitations:** While the synthetic dataset is a strength for control, it also poses a limitation.  The results might not fully generalize to real-world data, which is more complex and noisy. The specific characteristics of the generated data (e.g., the realism of the images and descriptions) could influence the observed transfer rates and memorization patterns.
    *   **Limited Scope of Mitigations:** The mitigation strategies explored (data augmentation) are relatively basic. While they demonstrate some effectiveness, the paper could benefit from exploring more advanced techniques, such as adversarial training or regularization methods specifically designed to improve cross-modal generalization.
    *   **Incremental Nature:** While the problem is novel, some of the findings (e.g., larger models memorize more, diverse data helps generalization) are consistent with existing research in single-modality models. The core contribution lies in demonstrating these effects within the cross-modal setting.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** Given that they only experiment with the Gemma model family, it's hard to generalize if their finings are applicable for other VLM frameworks.
    *   **Limited Task Diversity:** The focus on a single task (name recognition) may limit the generalizability of the findings. Exploring other tasks that require different types of cross-modal reasoning could provide a more comprehensive understanding of the phenomenon.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a valuable contribution by opening up a new area of research in cross-modal memorization.
    *   The findings have practical implications for developing more robust, privacy-conscious, and efficient VLMs.
    *   The introduced synthetic dataset can serve as a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by systematically exploring the relatively unexplored area of cross-modal memorization in vision-language models. The controlled experiments, clear findings, and identification of key challenges make it a valuable contribution to the research community.  While the reliance on synthetic data and the limited scope of the mitigation strategies are limitations, the overall strengths outweigh the weaknesses.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The score reflects the novelty of the problem and the rigor of the empirical analysis. While some of the findings are incremental extensions of existing research, the cross-modal setting introduces new complexities and challenges that are worth investigating. The paper is a solid and well-executed piece of work that will likely stimulate further research in this area. However, the limitations regarding real-world generalizability and the relatively basic mitigation strategies prevent it from receiving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Transformers Meet In-Context Learning: A Universal Approximation Theory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05200v1)
- **Authors**: Gen Li, Yuchen Jiao, Yu Huang, Yuting Wei, Yuxin Chen
- **Abstract**: Modern large language models are capable of in-context learning, the ability to perform new tasks at inference time using only a handful of input-output examples in the prompt, without any fine-tuning or parameter updates. We develop a universal approximation theory to better understand how transformers enable in-context learning. For any class of functions (each representing a distinct task), we demonstrate how to construct a transformer that, without any further weight updates, can perform reliable prediction given only a few in-context examples. In contrast to much of the recent literature that frames transformers as algorithm approximators -- i.e., constructing transformers to emulate the iterations of optimization algorithms as a means to approximate solutions of learning problems -- our work adopts a fundamentally different approach rooted in universal function approximation. This alternative approach offers approximation guarantees that are not constrained by the effectiveness of the optimization algorithms being approximated, thereby extending far beyond convex problems and linear function classes. Our construction sheds light on how transformers can simultaneously learn general-purpose representations and adapt dynamically to in-context examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a universal approximation theory for understanding how transformers achieve in-context learning (ICL). Instead of viewing transformers as algorithm approximators (emulating optimization algorithms like gradient descent), the authors propose a novel perspective: transformers as *direct* function approximators. They demonstrate how to construct a transformer that, *without weight updates*, can perform reliable prediction given a few in-context examples, for a general class of functions. This is achieved by (i) identifying universal general-purpose features and (ii) building transformer layers to compute optimal linear coefficients for the task dynamically. The approximation error is bounded by terms related to the covering number of the function class, the number of in-context examples, and the number of layers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a fresh perspective on understanding ICL in transformers. The standard "algorithm approximator" view is often limited by the constraints of the specific optimization algorithms it's trying to emulate (e.g., convexity assumptions for GD). The idea of transformers acting as direct function approximators is itself not entirely new, but the *rigorous formalization of this idea within the ICL context* and the *derivation of concrete approximation guarantees for a broad class of functions* constitute a significant contribution.

**Significance:** The universal approximation theory derived in the paper is potentially quite impactful for the following reasons:

*   **Broader Applicability:** It applies to a more general class of functions compared to previous works that focused on linear tasks or settings where optimization algorithms have guaranteed convergence. This directly addresses a limitation of algorithm approximation approaches.
*   **Understanding ICL:** It provides insights into how transformers simultaneously learn general-purpose representations and adapt dynamically to new tasks presented in context. This is a core aspect of ICL that needs greater understanding.
*   **Guidance for Architecture Design:** The theoretical results offer guidance on the crucial parameters like the number of layers and the input dimension for a transformer to perform well in an ICL setting. The logarithmic dependence on the covering number is particularly interesting.
*   **Beyond Optimization Emulation:** By moving away from optimization-mimicking architectures, the paper opens up new architectural possibilities tailored towards direct function approximation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Theoretical Framework:** The paper uses tools from approximation theory to derive concrete performance guarantees. The proofs are detailed and well-structured (at least according to the table of contents, since a comprehensive analysis of the mathematical accuracy is beyond the scope of my capabilities).
*   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The in-context learning setup is clearly defined, and the assumptions are stated explicitly.
*   **Novel Perspective:** As discussed above, the shift from algorithm approximation to direct function approximation is a significant contribution.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Abstraction:** The paper is highly theoretical and abstract, making it challenging to translate the findings into practical transformer architecture designs. The construction outlined may be complex and not directly implementable using existing software and hardware capabilities, which could limit immediate practical impact.
*   **Focus on Upper Bounds:** The results primarily provide upper bounds on the approximation error. It remains unclear how tight these bounds are in practice.
*   **Constant Factors:** The O() notation hides constant factors, which might be crucial in determining the actual performance of the proposed transformer.
*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The paper focuses heavily on theoretical analysis and lacks empirical validation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a substantial *theoretical* advance in understanding in-context learning. It proposes a novel perspective, derives rigorous guarantees for a broad class of functions, and provides useful guidance for architecture design.

However, the highly abstract nature of the results, the lack of empirical validation, and the difficulty in translating the theoretical framework into practical implementations are significant limitations. While the paper's long-term impact could be considerable, the *immediate* impact on the field is somewhat limited.

Therefore, the score reflects a strong contribution with potential for long-term impact, tempered by the practical limitations and primarily theoretical focus.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### OGGSplat: Open Gaussian Growing for Generalizable Reconstruction with Expanded Field-of-View
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05204v1)
- **Authors**: Yanbo Wang, Ziyi Wang, Wenzhao Zheng, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: Reconstructing semantic-aware 3D scenes from sparse views is a challenging yet essential research direction, driven by the demands of emerging applications such as virtual reality and embodied AI. Existing per-scene optimization methods require dense input views and incur high computational costs, while generalizable approaches often struggle to reconstruct regions outside the input view cone. In this paper, we propose OGGSplat, an open Gaussian growing method that expands the field-of-view in generalizable 3D reconstruction. Our key insight is that the semantic attributes of open Gaussians provide strong priors for image extrapolation, enabling both semantic consistency and visual plausibility. Specifically, once open Gaussians are initialized from sparse views, we introduce an RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module applied to selected rendered views. This module enforces bidirectional control between an image diffusion model and a semantic diffusion model. The inpainted regions are then lifted back into 3D space for efficient and progressive Gaussian parameter optimization. To evaluate our method, we establish a Gaussian Outpainting (GO) benchmark that assesses both semantic and generative quality of reconstructed open-vocabulary scenes. OGGSplat also demonstrates promising semantic-aware scene reconstruction capabilities when provided with two view images captured directly from a smartphone camera.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OGGSplat: Open Gaussian Growing for Generalizable Reconstruction with Expanded Field-of-View":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OGGSplat, a novel method for generalizable 3D scene reconstruction from sparse views. It addresses the limitation of existing generalizable methods that struggle to reconstruct regions outside the input view cone. OGGSplat leverages the semantic attributes of "open" Gaussians (Gaussians with associated open-vocabulary semantic information) as strong priors for image extrapolation. The key innovation is an RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module that enforces bidirectional control between image and semantic inpainting, allowing the model to imagine plausible content for unseen regions. The inpainted regions are then lifted back into 3D space for Gaussian parameter optimization, enabling field-of-view expansion.  The authors also introduce a new "Gaussian Outpainting" (GO) benchmark to evaluate both semantic and generative quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using semantic information to guide extrapolation and expansion of 3D Gaussian representations is interesting and provides a practical solution to a recognized problem. The bidirectional control within the inpainting module is a genuine contribution. However, individual components, such as semantic feature injection and Gaussian Splatting, build upon existing work. The novelty primarily lies in the *integration* of these components in a specific, synergistic way to address field-of-view expansion.
* **Significance:** Expanding the field-of-view of generalizable 3D reconstruction is significant because it improves usability in scenarios with limited input data or the need to explore beyond the initially captured area (e.g., robotic navigation, VR, AR). The GO benchmark is a valuable contribution to the community, as it directly addresses the challenging problem of evaluating extrapolated content plausibility. The demonstration of the method's usability with smartphone-captured data adds practical value. However, the gains reported could benefit from more rigorous comparisons to more competitive baselines designed for similar sparse reconstruction scenarios.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and focused approach.
    *   The RGB-semantic consistent inpainting module is a well-motivated and technically sound contribution.
    *   The GO benchmark provides a valuable tool for evaluating future work.
    *   Practical demonstration with smartphone images.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on existing components (Splatt3R, semantic heads, diffusion models) somewhat reduces the perceived novelty. While the *integration* is novel, individual elements are not.
    *   Limited evaluation on a more diverse set of datasets and scenarios. The focus primarily on ScanNet++ limits the assessment of the method's generalizability in broader settings.
    *   Relatively high FID scores across all methods, as acknowledged by the authors, raise concerns about the overall quality of the generated content. Even though the proposed model outperforms baselines, it needs to be improved significantly. The high FID scores could be attributed to domain shift from training data.
    *   The ablation studies, while helpful, could be more comprehensive in isolating the precise contribution of each component of the system. A more granular ablation of individual components is warranted.
    *   Depth estimation's limitations, particularly outside indoor scenarios, restricts real-world applicability. This reliance on potentially noisy depth estimates is a significant bottleneck.

* **Potential Influence:** OGGSplat has the potential to influence future research in generalizable 3D reconstruction by highlighting the importance of semantic priors for extrapolation. The GO benchmark is likely to become a standard evaluation tool. The framework inspires integrating semantic information with diffusion models to expand scene reconstruction capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the moderate novelty (primarily through component integration), significant contribution to problem framing and new evaluation benchmark, and potential to push research, the paper receives a score below 7. But given existing framework, reliance on potentially noisy depth estimates limits real-world applicability, it cannot be lower than 5. Therefore:

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### RELIC: Evaluating Compositional Instruction Following via Language Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05205v1)
- **Authors**: Jackson Petty, Michael Y. Hu, Wentao Wang, Shauli Ravfogel, William Merrill, Tal Linzen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly expected to perform tasks based only on a specification of the task provided in context, without examples of inputs and outputs; this ability is referred to as instruction following. We introduce the Recognition of Languages In-Context (RELIC) framework to evaluate instruction following using language recognition: the task of determining if a string is generated by formal grammar. Unlike many standard evaluations of LLMs' ability to use their context, this task requires composing together a large number of instructions (grammar productions) retrieved from the context. Because the languages are synthetic, the task can be increased in complexity as LLMs' skills improve, and new instances can be automatically generated, mitigating data contamination. We evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on RELIC and find that their accuracy can be reliably predicted from the complexity of the grammar and the individual example strings, and that even the most advanced LLMs currently available show near-chance performance on more complex grammars and samples, in line with theoretical expectations. We also use RELIC to diagnose how LLMs attempt to solve increasingly difficult reasoning tasks, finding that as the complexity of the language recognition task increases, models switch to relying on shallow heuristics instead of following complex instructions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RELIC (Recognition of Languages In-Context), a framework designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform compositional instruction following. RELIC uses the task of language recognition – determining if a string is generated by a formal grammar – as a proxy for instruction following. The core idea is that recognizing a string requires composing grammar rules provided in context, mimicking the need to combine multiple instructions for complex tasks. The authors generate context-free grammars (CFGs) of varying complexity and ask LLMs to classify whether given strings are generated by those grammars. The synthetic nature allows for controlled complexity, automatic generation of new examples, and mitigation of data contamination. The paper evaluates state-of-the-art LLMs on RELIC and finds that their accuracy decreases significantly as grammar complexity and string length increase, often falling to near-chance levels even for advanced models. A qualitative analysis of chain-of-thought reasoning reveals that models resort to shallow heuristics when faced with more complex grammars. The paper concludes that RELIC provides a valuable benchmark for assessing compositional instruction following and identifies substantial room for improvement in LLMs' reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel evaluation framework using formal language recognition as a testbed for compositional reasoning. While LLMs have been previously tested on formal languages, the emphasis on zero-shot *in-context* learning and the controlled generation of grammars of varying complexity is a strong contribution. The use of context-free grammars to approximate compositional aspects of natural language and programming languages adds to the novelty. Also, the analysis of reasoning strategies, including heuristic reliance, is insightful.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: evaluating compositional reasoning abilities in LLMs. Instruction following is a key capability, and RELIC offers a more rigorous and controllable approach than relying solely on natural language instructions and datasets, which are prone to contamination and annotation biases. The findings highlight limitations in current LLMs' ability to effectively use context for compositional tasks, suggesting a need for new architectures or training methods. The public release of RELIC-500 and the code for generating new grammars makes this a potentially valuable resource for the community. The exploration of chain-of-thought reasoning provides insights into how LLMs tackle complex tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The RELIC framework is well-motivated and addresses a genuine need for better evaluation of compositional reasoning.
    *   The use of formal languages allows for precise control over task complexity and eliminates concerns about data contamination.
    *   The experiments are thorough and evaluate a range of state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   The qualitative and quantitative analysis of reasoning strategies is informative and provides valuable insights into the models' limitations.
    *   The open-source release of RELIC enhances reproducibility and allows for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The connection between recognizing strings generated by CFGs and more general "instruction following" is an assumption. While CFGs capture compositional structure, they don't fully represent the complexities of real-world instructions (e.g., dealing with world knowledge, ambiguity, pragmatics). The extent to which these experiments generalize to natural language tasks isn't fully demonstrated.
    *   Negative sampling in RELIC-500 involves generating random strings and filtering out those that are parsable by the grammar. The paper acknowledges that this method may lead to statistical differences between positive and negative examples, allowing a model relying on heuristics to still achieve above-chance performance.
    *   Although the qualitative analysis is insightful, the LLM-as-a-judge approach to analyzing chain-of-thought relies on 04-mini classifying its own outputs. It's shown that it misclassifies results and may not be a faithful representation of its decision-making process.

*   **Potential Influence:** RELIC has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating compositional instruction following. It could drive research into new LLM architectures and training techniques that are better suited for compositional tasks. The findings regarding the limitations of current models and their reliance on heuristics will likely influence the research community and motivate further investigation into the inner workings of LLMs.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper provides a solid contribution to the field with a clearly defined and novel evaluation framework. While the connection between the formal language recognition task and general instruction following isn't perfect and the negative sampling could be improved, RELIC offers a controllable and scalable method to assess a vital aspect of LLMs' reasoning abilities. The insights derived from the reasoning analysis are also compelling. However, weaknesses in the experiment design as well as limitations around its connection to instruction following in natural language lead to a lower overall score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Follow-Your-Motion: Video Motion Transfer via Efficient Spatial-Temporal Decoupled Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05207v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Ma, Yulong Liu, Qiyuan Zhu, Ayden Yang, Kunyu Feng, Xinhua Zhang, Zhifeng Li, Sirui Han, Chenyang Qi, Qifeng Chen
- **Abstract**: Recently, breakthroughs in the video diffusion transformer have shown remarkable capabilities in diverse motion generations. As for the motion-transfer task, current methods mainly use two-stage Low-Rank Adaptations (LoRAs) finetuning to obtain better performance. However, existing adaptation-based motion transfer still suffers from motion inconsistency and tuning inefficiency when applied to large video diffusion transformers. Naive two-stage LoRA tuning struggles to maintain motion consistency between generated and input videos due to the inherent spatial-temporal coupling in the 3D attention operator. Additionally, they require time-consuming fine-tuning processes in both stages. To tackle these issues, we propose Follow-Your-Motion, an efficient two-stage video motion transfer framework that finetunes a powerful video diffusion transformer to synthesize complex motion.Specifically, we propose a spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA to decouple the attention architecture for spatial appearance and temporal motion processing. During the second training stage, we design the sparse motion sampling and adaptive RoPE to accelerate the tuning speed. To address the lack of a benchmark for this field, we introduce MotionBench, a comprehensive benchmark comprising diverse motion, including creative camera motion, single object motion, multiple object motion, and complex human motion. We show extensive evaluations on MotionBench to verify the superiority of Follow-Your-Motion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Follow-Your-Motion: Video Motion Transfer via Efficient Spatial-Temporal Decoupled Finetuning" introduces a novel two-stage finetuning framework for video motion transfer using Diffusion Transformers (DiTs). The method, called Follow-Your-Motion, addresses issues of motion inconsistency and tuning inefficiency prevalent in existing adaptation-based approaches. It proposes a spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) to disentangle the attention architecture for processing spatial appearance and temporal motion separately. Additionally, it utilizes sparse motion sampling and adaptive RoPE (Rotary Positional Embedding) to accelerate the temporal tuning stage. The paper also introduces a new benchmark, MotionBench, for evaluating video motion transfer methods. Extensive experiments on MotionBench demonstrate the superiority of Follow-Your-Motion over existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA tailored for DiTs and the combined use of sparse motion sampling with adaptive RoPE. While prior work has explored decoupled tuning paradigms, this paper presents a more refined and efficient approach specifically designed for DiTs, which inherently have a more complex spatial-temporal mixed computation within their 3D self-attention blocks. The head classification method based on attention map sparsity seems like a reasonable approach to guide the decoupled LoRA. The introduction of MotionBench fills a gap in the community and is a valuable contribution, although the composition of the benchmark (30% generated) raises questions about data biases.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem: efficient and consistent motion transfer in videos using powerful DiTs. The proposed framework achieves notable improvements in terms of both performance (motion consistency) and efficiency (training time). It could influence future research directions by establishing the importance of spatial-temporal disentanglement within DiTs for motion transfer tasks. The benchmark could enable more rigorous and standardized evaluation of motion transfer methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses critical limitations in existing methods for DiT-based motion transfer.
    *   The spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA is a sound and effective technique.
    *   Sparse motion sampling with adaptive RoPE offers a significant speedup.
    *   The introduction of MotionBench is a valuable community contribution.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on the newly proposed benchmark.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The description of the attention head classification stage could be improved for clarity, particularly regarding the pseudo-ground truth generation. What is the impact of alpha? Is there an ablative analysis of alpha to justify the choice of 1.3?
    *   The novelty, while present, is incremental. It builds upon existing LoRA adaptation and spatial temporal decomposition techniques.
    *   The composition of MotionBench is questionable. 30% synthetic data may bias the evaluation towards synthetic/generative methods. It also introduces questions related to copyright/licensing that isn't addressed.
    *   Comparisons with some methods are limited because of code unavailability, which could be perceived as a bias.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on the adoption of MotionBench and the general interest in DiT-based video motion transfer. The code release will be an important factor in whether the method is widely used and built upon.
*   There's an underestimation of how complex spatial-temporal relation can be. Even sparse sampling, adaptive RoPE are not fully capturing the spatio-temporal characteristics. Further improvements can be made.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the paper presents a sound and technically competent solution to the problem of video motion transfer, the novelty is somewhat incremental. The spatial-temporal decoupled LoRA, while effective, is an adaptation of existing techniques to the DiT architecture. However, the sparse sampling/adaptive RoPE combination and, crucially, the introduction of MotionBench, elevate the significance of the paper.

The contributions significantly advance the field by addressing critical limitations in existing motion transfer techniques and introducing a benchmark for further research and standardization. However, the dataset generation methodology raises questions about data bias. Also, the dataset hasn't been vetted with copyright licenses, thereby limiting the community to reproduce and expand on the work. The incremental nature of the methodological novelty limits the impact factor.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Common Pile v0.1: An 8TB Dataset of Public Domain and Openly Licensed Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05209v1)
- **Authors**: Nikhil Kandpal, Brian Lester, Colin Raffel, Sebastian Majstorovic, Stella Biderman, Baber Abbasi, Luca Soldaini, Enrico Shippole, A. Feder Cooper, Aviya Skowron, John Kirchenbauer, Shayne Longpre, Lintang Sutawika, Alon Albalak, Zhenlin Xu, Guilherme Penedo, Loubna Ben Allal, Elie Bakouch, John David Pressman, Honglu Fan, Dashiell Stander, Guangyu Song, Aaron Gokaslan, Tom Goldstein, Brian R. Bartoldson, Bhavya Kailkhura, Tyler Murray
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are typically trained on enormous quantities of unlicensed text, a practice that has led to scrutiny due to possible intellectual property infringement and ethical concerns. Training LLMs on openly licensed text presents a first step towards addressing these issues, but prior data collection efforts have yielded datasets too small or low-quality to produce performant LLMs. To address this gap, we collect, curate, and release the Common Pile v0.1, an eight terabyte collection of openly licensed text designed for LLM pretraining. The Common Pile comprises content from 30 sources that span diverse domains including research papers, code, books, encyclopedias, educational materials, audio transcripts, and more. Crucially, we validate our efforts by training two 7 billion parameter LLMs on text from the Common Pile: Comma v0.1-1T and Comma v0.1-2T, trained on 1 and 2 trillion tokens respectively. Both models attain competitive performance to LLMs trained on unlicensed text with similar computational budgets, such as Llama 1 and 2 7B. In addition to releasing the Common Pile v0.1 itself, we also release the code used in its creation as well as the training mixture and checkpoints for the Comma v0.1 models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The Common Pile v0.1: An 8TB Dataset of Public Domain and Openly Licensed Text":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Common Pile v0.1, an 8TB dataset composed of openly licensed and public domain text. Addressing concerns about copyright infringement and ethical issues related to training large language models (LLMs) on unlicensed data, the authors collect and curate text from 30 diverse sources including research papers, code, books, government documents, educational materials, and audio transcripts. To demonstrate the dataset's usability, they train two 7-billion parameter LLMs, Comma v0.1-1T and Comma v0.1-2T, on 1 and 2 trillion tokens respectively. The paper shows these models achieve competitive performance compared to models trained on unlicensed text like Llama 1 and 2 (7B), and the authors release the Common Pile, associated code, training mixtures, and model checkpoints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a large, openly licensed LLM pretraining dataset is itself a significant contribution. While smaller, similar datasets exist, the Common Pile’s scale (8TB) and diversity of sources represent an advancement. The key novelty lies in the deliberate effort to create a dataset *specifically* for LLM pretraining, focusing on public domain/openly licensed content. This distinguishes it from generic text collections repurposed for LLM training. The extensive analysis and release of both the dataset and trained models are strong positives.
*   **Significance:** The work is highly significant for several reasons. Firstly, it provides a viable path towards more ethical LLM training practices, mitigating legal and ethical risks associated with copyright.  Secondly, the comparative performance of the Comma models showcases that openly licensed data can be used to create competitive LLMs, challenging the assumption that proprietary datasets are essential for high performance. Finally, the open release of the dataset, code, and models allows for broader research into training dynamics, memorization, and data auditing within a legally sound framework, promoting transparency and reproducibility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale and Diversity:** The 8TB size and 30 distinct sources make it a substantial resource.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The authors conduct controlled data ablation studies and train models at different scales to rigorously evaluate the dataset's quality.
    *   **Open Release:** Public availability fosters further research and development in the area.
    *   **Practical Demonstration:** Training and releasing performant LLMs demonstrate real-world viability.
    *   **Emphasis on Ethical Considerations:** Addresses growing concerns about copyright and consent in LLM training.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Potential Data Repetition:**  The authors acknowledge a potential issue of data repetition, especially in the 2T training run, which may limit performance and lead to diminishing returns. While unavoidable in the context of current openly licensed texts, this should be improved. The data weighting has been done with some care, but it still requires further optimization.
    *   **English-Centric:** The dataset's strong focus on English may limit its utility for multilingual LLM development.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation Benchmarks:** While a reasonable set of benchmarks are used for assessment, further experiments on more diverse tasks could further validate the model.
    *   **Limited Data:** Despite the efforts to collect such a large dataset, it is important to note that only a subset of the internet is in the public domain or open licensed. This dataset, while large, will be insufficient to produce state-of-the-art performance as compared to LLMs that make use of all text on the internet.

*   **Impact and Influence:**  The Common Pile is likely to have a significant impact. It offers a blueprint for creating future openly licensed datasets and encourages the community to prioritize ethical data collection. It facilitates research on safer and more transparent LLM development practices. It may also drive demand for more openly licensed content, thus fostering a more equitable data ecosystem. The models built on the Common Pile can be used for downstream tasks in legal/educational areas.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The Common Pile is a valuable contribution, but also has room for improvement. The work addresses a critical issue, namely copyright and ethical considerations, in LLM pretraining.  The thorough evaluation and open release are commendably. While the scale and approach are improvements over prior work, there are limitations regarding data repetition and limited scope of the benchmarks used. Given these factors, a high but not exceptional score seems warranted.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM-First Search: Self-Guided Exploration of the Solution Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05213v1)
- **Authors**: Nathan Herr, Tim Rocktäschel, Roberta Raileanu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable improvements in reasoning and planning through increased test-time compute, often by framing problem-solving as a search process. While methods like Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) have proven effective in some domains, their reliance on fixed exploration hyperparameters limits their adaptability across tasks of varying difficulty, rendering them impractical or expensive in certain settings. In this paper, we propose \textbf{LLM-First Search (LFS)}, a novel \textit{LLM Self-Guided Search} method that removes the need for pre-defined search strategies by empowering the LLM to autonomously control the search process via self-guided exploration. Rather than relying on external heuristics or hardcoded policies, the LLM evaluates whether to pursue the current search path or explore alternative branches based on its internal scoring mechanisms. This enables more flexible and context-sensitive reasoning without requiring manual tuning or task-specific adaptation. We evaluate LFS on Countdown and Sudoku against three classic widely-used search algorithms, Tree-of-Thoughts' Breadth First Search (ToT-BFS), Best First Search (BestFS), and MCTS, each of which have been used to achieve SotA results on a range of challenging reasoning tasks. We found that LFS (1) performs better on more challenging tasks without additional tuning, (2) is more computationally efficient compared to the other methods, especially when powered by a stronger model, (3) scales better with stronger models, due to its LLM-First design, and (4) scales better with increased compute budget. Our code is publicly available at \href{https://github.com/NathanHerr/LLM-First-Search}{LLM-First-Search}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLM-First Search: Self-Guided Exploration of the Solution Space":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLM-First Search (LFS), a novel search method for reasoning and planning tasks that leverages the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) to autonomously guide the search process. Unlike traditional search algorithms like Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), which rely on fixed exploration hyperparameters, LFS allows the LLM to decide whether to continue exploring the current search path or explore alternative branches based on its internal scoring mechanisms. The LLM acts as both the actor and the evaluator, enabling more flexible and context-sensitive reasoning without manual tuning. The paper evaluates LFS on Countdown and Sudoku tasks against baselines like Tree-of-Thoughts (ToT), Best-First Search (BestFS), and MCTS, demonstrating improved performance, computational efficiency, and scalability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of allowing an LLM to control the search process, adaptively determining exploration vs. exploitation, is novel.  Existing approaches primarily use LLMs to augment existing search algorithms (e.g., value estimation, action priors) but typically retain fixed exploration strategies or manually tuned hyperparameters. LFS, by empowering the LLM to be the primary driver of exploration, represents a significant departure.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and crucial limitation of LLM-augmented search algorithms: the reliance on fixed hyperparameters that limit adaptability across tasks and models. Overcoming this limitation has the potential to make LLM-based reasoning and planning more robust and efficient. The experiments demonstrate that LFS performs competitively or surpasses established methods in Countdown and Sudoku while demonstrating better flexibility, adaptability, and scalability with stronger models and increased compute budgets. This suggests its applicability to a wider range of complex tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Well-defined method:** The description of LFS is precise and the algorithm is easily understood.
    *   **Solid experimental evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation against strong baselines on two distinct reasoning tasks. Experiments are well-designed, and include comparisons using two different model scales, as well as multiple hyperparameter settings for the baselines. The analysis is thorough and the results are clearly presented.
    *   **Strong results:** The empirical results support the paper's claims, demonstrating LFS's competitiveness, adaptability, efficiency, and scalability.
    *   **Open source code:** The availability of the code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited task variety:** The evaluation is confined to Countdown and Sudoku. While these are useful benchmarks, evaluating on more diverse and complex tasks, especially those with real-world relevance, would further strengthen the conclusions. The limited task variety also calls into question the generality of the "self-guided" exploration abilities of the LLM, considering that both games have a similar nature, consisting of clear and understandable rules.
    *   **Dependence on LLM quality:** The self-guided nature of LFS makes it potentially vulnerable to biases or limitations of the underlying LLM. The paper would benefit from a more thorough discussion on the conditions under which LFS might fail or how to mitigate potential issues stemming from LLM imperfections. The paper only considers how LFS would behave in a setup with stronger models.
    *   **Lack of theoretical analysis:** A more formal analysis of LFS's exploration properties and convergence guarantees would enhance the paper's theoretical contribution.

*   **Potential impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM-based reasoning and planning by shifting the focus towards self-guided exploration and adaptive search strategies. The findings could lead to more efficient and robust LLM agents for complex problem-solving.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**
    This paper presents a novel approach that significantly advances the field of LLM-based reasoning. The rigorous experimental evaluation confirms the method's competitiveness and its advantages over established methods. Although it has some limitations in the diversity of its task set, its clear methodology and strong results, backed by publicly available code, will likely influence future research.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Low-Resource Morphological Inflection via Self-Supervised Objectives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05227v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Wiemerslage, Katharina von der Wense
- **Abstract**: Self-supervised objectives have driven major advances in NLP by leveraging large-scale unlabeled data, but such resources are scarce for many of the world's languages. Surprisingly, they have not been explored much for character-level tasks, where smaller amounts of data have the potential to be beneficial. We investigate the effectiveness of self-supervised auxiliary tasks for morphological inflection -- a character-level task highly relevant for language documentation -- in extremely low-resource settings, training encoder-decoder transformers for 19 languages and 13 auxiliary objectives. Autoencoding yields the best performance when unlabeled data is very limited, while character masked language modeling (CMLM) becomes more effective as data availability increases. Though objectives with stronger inductive biases influence model predictions intuitively, they rarely outperform standard CMLM. However, sampling masks based on known morpheme boundaries consistently improves performance, highlighting a promising direction for low-resource morphological modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of self-supervised learning objectives as auxiliary tasks to improve low-resource morphological inflection. The authors train encoder-decoder transformers for 19 languages using 13 different auxiliary objectives and various amounts of unlabeled data. They investigate the effectiveness of autoencoding (AE) and character masked language modeling (CMLM) with different masking strategies (iid, suffix, prefix, segment).  The key findings are that autoencoding works best when unlabeled data is very limited, CMLM becomes more effective with increased unlabeled data, and masking based on morpheme boundaries can improve performance. They analyze the results, considering language typology and the copy-bias induced by autoencoding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **extensive exploration** of various self-supervised objectives and masking strategies for a *specific* low-resource task.  Previous work has explored auxiliary task training in morphology, but this paper provides a more comprehensive experimental evaluation across a large set of objectives, languages, and data availability settings. The investigation of morpheme boundary masking is also a novel aspect, though limited to a subset of languages.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a practical problem: improving NLP tasks in low-resource scenarios, which is crucial for language preservation and technology access for a broader range of languages. Morphological inflection is an important character-level task relevant to various language documentation and educational efforts. The paper's findings regarding the effectiveness of AE and CMLM under different data constraints provide valuable insights for practitioners working with limited resources. The observation that morpheme-aware masking shows promise is also significant, as it suggests a direction for incorporating linguistic knowledge into self-supervised training. However, the magnitude of the improvements in some cases (relative to a simple baseline) are not overwhelmingly large.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation across multiple languages, auxiliary objectives, and data availability settings, allowing for robust conclusions.
    *   **Clear Experimental Design:** The experiments are well-defined, and the methodology is clearly described.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The authors provide insightful analysis of the results, considering factors like language typology, data sparsity, and inductive biases of different objectives.
    *   **Relevance to Low-Resource NLP:** The work directly addresses a significant challenge in low-resource NLP, making it relevant to a broad audience.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors plan to release their code, enhancing the reproducibility of their findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Objectives:**  While the combinations and extensive study are novel, the core self-supervised objectives (AE, MLM, T5-style denoising) are well-established. The modifications to the masking strategies (suffix, prefix) offer marginal novelty.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** Although informative, some of the performance improvements are incremental, especially when compared to the AE or CMLM baselines with iid masking.
    *   **Limited Scope of Typological Analysis:** While the paper attempts to analyze the results considering language typology, this aspect could be further developed. A deeper analysis linking specific language features to the effectiveness of different objectives could strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Segmentation Data Scope:** While the morpheme-based segmentation strategy shows promise, its implementation is limited to 5 languages and a heuristic approach for creating surface segmentations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence future research in low-resource NLP by guiding the selection of appropriate self-supervised objectives and training strategies for character-level tasks. The morpheme-aware masking approach could inspire further research on incorporating linguistic knowledge into self-supervised learning. The open-source code could also benefit practitioners working with low-resource languages.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a comprehensive and insightful analysis of self-supervised objectives for low-resource morphological inflection. While the core objectives are not entirely novel, the paper's strength lies in its systematic evaluation and analysis. The results offer valuable guidance for practitioners and provide promising directions for future research. However, the incremental nature of some improvements and the limited scope of the typological and segmentation analyses limit its overall impact. Taking these factors into account, a score of 6 is justified.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Diagonal Batching Unlocks Parallelism in Recurrent Memory Transformers for Long Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05229v1)
- **Authors**: Danil Sivtsov, Ivan Rodkin, Gleb Kuzmin, Yuri Kuratov, Ivan Oseledets
- **Abstract**: Transformer models struggle with long-context inference due to their quadratic time and linear memory complexity. Recurrent Memory Transformers (RMTs) offer a solution by reducing the asymptotic cost to linear time and constant memory usage. However, their memory update mechanism leads to sequential execution, causing a performance bottleneck. We introduce Diagonal Batching, a scheduling scheme that unlocks parallelism across segments in RMTs while preserving exact recurrence. This approach eliminates the sequential constraint, enabling efficient GPU inference even for single long-context inputs without complex batching and pipelining techniques. Because the technique is purely a run-time computation reordering, existing RMT models adopt it with no retraining. Applied to a LLaMA-1B ARMT model, Diagonal Batching yields a 3.3x speedup over standard full-attention LLaMA-1B and a 1.8x speedup over the sequential RMT implementation on 131,072-token sequences. By removing sequential bottleneck, Diagonal Batching reduces inference cost and latency, thereby strengthening RMTs as a practical solution for real-world, long-context applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Diagonal Batching," a novel scheduling scheme for Recurrent Memory Transformers (RMTs) and Parallel RMTs (PRMTs).  Diagonal Batching unlocks inter-segment parallelism during inference by reorganizing layer and segment computations into independent "diagonals," enabling concurrent execution on the GPU. This approach eliminates sequential bottlenecks without altering the model architecture or requiring retraining.  The authors demonstrate significant speedups (up to 3.3x over standard Transformers and 1.8x over sequential RMTs) when applied to LLaMA models with ARMT on long context tasks (up to 131,072 tokens). They also present empirical evidence that the approach maintains comparable accuracy to the original ARMT models and can be combined with other efficiency optimizations like FlashAttention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of Diagonal Batching is a clever runtime reordering of computations. While it leverages existing concepts like layer-level memory in PRMTs, the application of a diagonal scheduling scheme to achieve inter-segment parallelism is genuinely novel. The paper clearly identifies the bottleneck in previous RMT/PRMT implementations and provides a practical solution.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical improvement it offers for long-context inference. RMTs/PRMTs are promising for handling very long sequences, but their sequential nature limits their efficiency. Diagonal Batching directly addresses this, making these models more viable for real-world applications. The fact that it's a runtime optimization that doesn't require retraining is a huge plus. The empirical results convincingly show substantial speedups.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the performance bottleneck in existing RMT/PRMT implementations.
    *   **Simple and Effective Solution:** Diagonal Batching is conceptually straightforward yet delivers substantial performance gains.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The technique requires no model retraining, making it easy to adopt for existing models.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering different model sizes, sequence lengths, and hardware configurations. The comparison to baseline ARMT and standard Transformer implementations is compelling.
    *   **Addresses a key limitation:** The paper makes a step towards addressing the compute requirements of extremely long context length models which is critical for many real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Applicability:** As acknowledged by the authors, the method is not directly compatible with the original RMT architecture with inter-layer recurrence. It requires a layer-level recurrence (PRMT) to function.
    *   **Implementation Complexities:** The paper mentions some implementation complexities related to heterogeneous layers and manual engineering for grouping logic. The ideal load distribution, the authors note, is not currently achieved.
    *   **Performance gains capped based on model size:** The authors note the performance improvement decreases for smaller models as the hardware utilization nears peak.

*   **Potential Impact:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the adoption of RMTs/PRMTs in long-context applications. By making these models more efficient, it could enable new applications that were previously impractical. It will also likely influence future research in memory-augmented transformers and efficient inference techniques.

**Justification:**

The paper delivers on its promises. It presents a novel and effective technique that significantly improves the inference speed of RMTs/PRMTs. The empirical evaluation is strong, and the authors acknowledge the limitations of their approach. The significance stems from making long-context inference with these models more practical. The presented solution demonstrates significant value as runtime improvement without impacting quality, therefore I am adjusting the overall score.

**Score: 8**

The paper provides significant insight into improving the efficiency of long-context transformers. The diagonal batching method is both novel and practically valuable, particularly given its ease of integration and lack of retraining requirements. While there are limitations, such as incompatibility with certain architectures and implementation complexities, the overall contribution warrants a high score reflecting its positive impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05231v1)
- **Authors**: Severi Rissanen, RuiKang OuYang, Jiajun He, Wenlin Chen, Markus Heinonen, Arno Solin, José Miguel Hernández-Lobato
- **Abstract**: Recent research has focused on designing neural samplers that amortize the process of sampling from unnormalized densities. However, despite significant advancements, they still fall short of the state-of-the-art MCMC approach, Parallel Tempering (PT), when it comes to the efficiency of target evaluations. On the other hand, unlike a well-trained neural sampler, PT yields only dependent samples and needs to be rerun -- at considerable computational cost -- whenever new samples are required. To address these weaknesses, we propose the Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion (PTSD), which trains diffusion models sequentially across temperatures, leveraging the advantages of PT to improve the training of neural samplers. We also introduce a novel method to combine high-temperature diffusion models to generate approximate lower-temperature samples, which are minimally refined using MCMC and used to train the next diffusion model. PTSD enables efficient reuse of sample information across temperature levels while generating well-mixed, uncorrelated samples. Our method significantly improves target evaluation efficiency, outperforming diffusion-based neural samplers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Progressive Tempering Sampler with Diffusion (PTSD), a novel approach to amortized sampling that aims to combine the strengths of Parallel Tempering (PT) and diffusion models. PTSD sequentially trains diffusion models at decreasing temperatures, using a novel temperature guidance mechanism to generate approximate samples at lower temperatures based on models trained at higher temperatures. These approximate samples are then refined using MCMC and used to train the diffusion model at the lower temperature. This allows for efficient reuse of sample information across temperature levels while generating uncorrelated samples. Experiments demonstrate that PTSD achieves significantly improved target evaluation efficiency compared to other diffusion-based neural samplers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration strategy of PT and diffusion models. The temperature guidance mechanism is a straightforward but effective way to leverage information from higher-temperature models to initialize sampling at lower temperatures.  The sequential training process across temperatures, combined with the MCMC refinement, is a solid contribution. The approach seems well-motivated, addressing specific shortcomings of both pure diffusion-based samplers and traditional PT. The concept of "bootstrapping across temperatures" is a good way to frame the contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the field of amortized sampling: achieving both high sample quality *and* efficient target evaluation.  The experimental results suggest that PTSD makes a significant step towards this goal, outperforming existing diffusion-based samplers in terms of target evaluation efficiency while maintaining competitive sample quality. This could have a real impact on the feasibility of applying amortized sampling to complex problems where target evaluations are computationally expensive. The performance boost compared to directly fitting a diffusion model on PT-generated data is also significant.  If the method proves to be robust across a wider range of problems, it could become a standard baseline for future research in this area. It shows a promising pathway for integrating traditional sampling algorithms and neural samplers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear motivation and problem statement.
    *   Well-defined algorithm with a novel temperature guidance mechanism.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on a variety of target distributions.
    *   Significant improvement in target evaluation efficiency compared to existing methods.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrating the effectiveness of key components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvements in sample quality are only incremental over state-of-the-art methods. The gains are primarily in efficiency. This places a higher burden on the demonstration of efficiency. The wall-clock time experiments could be improved by optimizing the code to take full advantage of parallelization.
    *   The description of the temperature guidance mechanism could be more intuitive.
    *   The paper relies heavily on comparisons with other diffusion-based samplers. A stronger comparison against other amortized sampling methods (e.g., those based on normalizing flows) would further strengthen the paper.
    *   The authors state limitations regarding training cost, non-parallelizable execution, and sensitivity to hyperparameters and temperature schedule.  These are important limitations that need to be considered when assessing the practical applicability of the method.
    *   Limited discussion of the potential impact of biased samples on the performance, this could be due to the approximate guidance.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper could have a notable impact on the development of more efficient amortized samplers. It is likely to spur further research into integrating traditional sampling techniques with neural samplers. It could become a standard approach for difficult sampling tasks.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of amortized sampling by introducing a novel method that effectively combines the strengths of PT and diffusion models. While the improvements in sample quality are modest, the significant gains in target evaluation efficiency are substantial and make the approach a promising direction for future research. The paper is well-written, the algorithm is well-defined, and the experimental evaluation is comprehensive. The limitations are clearly stated. Overall, this paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MesaNet: Sequence Modeling by Locally Optimal Test-Time Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.05233v1)
- **Authors**: Johannes von Oswald, Nino Scherrer, Seijin Kobayashi, Luca Versari, Songlin Yang, Maximilian Schlegel, Kaitlin Maile, Yanick Schimpf, Oliver Sieberling, Alexander Meulemans, Rif A. Saurous, Guillaume Lajoie, Charlotte Frenkel, Razvan Pascanu, Blaise Agüera y Arcas, João Sacramento
- **Abstract**: Sequence modeling is currently dominated by causal transformer architectures that use softmax self-attention. Although widely adopted, transformers require scaling memory and compute linearly during inference. A recent stream of work linearized the softmax operation, resulting in powerful recurrent neural network (RNN) models with constant memory and compute costs such as DeltaNet, Mamba or xLSTM. These models can be unified by noting that their recurrent layer dynamics can all be derived from an in-context regression objective, approximately optimized through an online learning rule. Here, we join this line of work and introduce a numerically stable, chunkwise parallelizable version of the recently proposed Mesa layer (von Oswald et al., 2024), and study it in language modeling at the billion-parameter scale. This layer again stems from an in-context loss, but which is now minimized to optimality at every time point using a fast conjugate gradient solver. Through an extensive suite of experiments, we show that optimal test-time training enables reaching lower language modeling perplexity and higher downstream benchmark performance than previous RNNs, especially on tasks requiring long context understanding. This performance gain comes at the cost of additional flops spent during inference time. Our results are therefore intriguingly related to recent trends of increasing test-time compute to improve performance -- here by spending compute to solve sequential optimization problems within the neural network itself.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MesaNet," a sequence modeling architecture based on a novel parallelizable version of the Mesa layer, which performs optimal test-time training. Unlike traditional transformers, MesaNet achieves constant memory and compute costs during inference. It builds upon recent advances in linearized self-attention, viewing recurrent layer dynamics as arising from in-context regression objectives.  The key innovation is a numerically stable, chunkwise parallelizable Mesa layer that minimizes an in-context loss to optimality at every time point using a fast conjugate gradient solver.
